# Tidal Lock (The Jovian Ledger, Book One)

> Partial: 18 chapters written of 18 planned. This book is unfinished.

## Chapter 1: Weigh-In

The shuttle *Prudent Interval* came down into the Valhalla basin the way a coin settles through water: slowly, rocking, with the strong suspicion that it had stopped trying.

Piq Varga had the window seat because nobody else wanted it. The other eleven passengers were contract labor rotating in from the Ganymede transfer. They had seen Callisto before and slept with their mouths open, heads lolling against the padded rests in the half-gravity of deceleration. Piq kept her forehead a centimeter from the polycarbonate and counted.

The basin's rings came first, three hundred kilometers of them, ridge after concentric ridge. They looked like the ripple of a stone dropped into a pond four billion years ago and frozen before the water could settle. Everything was dark, a dirty gray-brown so old that the craters had craters. Above the rim, low in the west, Jupiter hung where it always hung, because Callisto had stopped turning its face long before anyone came to look. The planet was a fat, banded lamp the size of her outstretched palm. Its cream-and-rust light lay over the regolith in long smears and turned every frost-dusted crater wall the color of weak tea.

She had seen pictures, and the pictures had not mentioned the stillness. On a cycler there was always motion: the slow wheel of the spin section, stars marching past the ports every ninety seconds. Here the sky simply stayed where it was put.

"Asgard approach, *Prudent Interval*, thirty kilometers, on profile." The pilot's voice was bored and Martian-accented. "Hold onto your teeth."

The station resolved out of the dark as a scatter of geometry on the inner ring plain. There were landing pads, a cluster of radiator fins, and the dull glint of the regolith berms. Under those berms nineteen hundred people lived like ticks under a hide. To the north the Sling ran out toward the horizon, a single ruled line forty kilometers long, so straight it looked drawn with a blade. Its far end was lost in the curve of the moon.

Then the depot came into view.

It sat east of the pads, a squat terraced mound of stored ice blocks, each the size of a shuttle cabin. Over its sunward face stretched a gauzy canopy, the sunshade, hung from a lattice of masts. The sun was a hard white point, small and far, and it had climbed perhaps a third of the way up the sky in its sixteen-day crawl. Its light came in at a low angle across the depot's eastern terraces, under the edge of the shade.

The eastern terraces were frosted.

Piq looked at them for a long four seconds. Sunlit ice lost its frost; it did not grow it. A white rime on the sunward face meant water was coming off the depot somewhere, in quantity, and landing where the light should have stripped it away.

Or there was a perfectly good explanation in a maintenance log she hadn't read yet.

She wrote it down anyway, on the tally pad clipped to her knee, in the shorthand her father had taught her before she could reliably spell: *Depot E face, frost, sunward. ?* The question mark was the important part. Gustav Varga had been weigh-master on the cycler *Long Patience* for thirty-one years, and he had held that the question mark was the most honest symbol in mathematics. *Everybody wants to write the equals sign,* he used to say. *The equals sign is where you stop looking.*

The shuttle kissed the pad. In 0.126 g the kiss lasted a long time.

---

Asgard Yard's mass intake was a room built for disappointment.

It was long and low, with a ceiling of sprayed sealant over regolith fill and a floor that had been scrubbed so often the antislip grit had worn to a shine. Six weigh stations stood in a row, each a steel platform flush with the floor beside a terminal and a clerk. Above them a sign in four languages read EVERY KILOGRAM IS SOMEONE'S. Below it, in smaller letters added later and less officially: DECLARE OR PAY. ALSO PAY.

The air smelled of ozone and wet stone. Piq breathed it in and felt something in her chest unclench a quarter-turn. It was the smell of electrolysis, of water being cracked into breath somewhere close. Every habitat she had lived in smelled like this, and none of them had been home for longer than a contract.

The contract laborers went first, in a shuffling line, their kit bags on their shoulders. Piq watched them weigh in and noticed which ones paid without blinking: the ones who'd hidden something better elsewhere. A broad woman with a Tharsis co-op patch came in three hundred grams under and looked insulted about it.

"Varga, Piq," said the clerk at station four. He was a narrow young man with the boneless posture of someone born in low gravity. His name tag said FITCH, and his face said he had been awake for a long time. "Ibis Metrology, certified auditor, inbound. Personal allowance twenty kilos. Bags on the plate, please, and then yourself."

She put her duffel and her instrument case on the platform. The display considered, settled, and blinked.

**21.304 kg.**

Fitch looked at the number. He looked at Piq. He did not look surprised. In low gravity it took a long time for anything to fall, so it took a long time to build a sigh, and he built a very thorough one.

"One point three oh four over," he said. "Excess personal mass is tariffed at the Jovian Development Authority standard rate, which is"—he consulted nothing, because he knew—"four hundred and twelve Compact marks per kilo, prorated to the gram. Or you can jettison."

"Jettison where?"

"Reclamation bin. We crush it, sort it, and render it into feedstock." He gestured at a hopper by the wall, which had a slot in it like a mouth. "People leave all kinds of things. Last week someone left a wedding dress. Silk. Very heavy, silk."

Piq did the arithmetic while he spoke. Five hundred and thirty-seven marks was not ruinous; Lagrange Mutual would reimburse it under a miscellaneous code. But a line on a JDA tariff ledger reading *1.304 kg excess, personal, unspecified* would sit on file in a security office. It would be there for anyone curious enough to ask what an auditor carried that she couldn't do without. And there was a second problem. Every kilo over allowance was flagged for itemized declaration, which meant opening the instrument case on Fitch's counter and explaining, item by item, why a metrologist needed a hollow cylinder with a receiver inside it sensitive enough to hear a dosimeter cough on the far side of the moon.

"It's not personal mass," she said.

Fitch's expression said that everyone said that.

She unlatched the instrument case, which was what an innocent woman would do, and lifted out the top tray of calipers and strain gauges so he could see the foam bed beneath. Five polished cylinders sat in it, graded from fist-sized down to a pair of pins no bigger than rice grains. The lid bore an Ibis Metrology certification plate, and the cylinders gleamed with the faintly smug finish of objects that knew precisely what they were.

"Reference masses," Piq said. "OIML class E2. One kilogram, two hundred grams, one hundred grams, two grams, two grams. I'm here to audit the depot's mass flow, which means calibrating every load cell between your pads and the Sling breech. You can't audit a scale with a scale. You need a standard."

"They're mass," said Fitch.

"They're the definition of mass." She tapped the certification plate. "JDA Fiscal Code, Schedule Nine, paragraph four. Metrological standards certified for the calibration of Authority weighing equipment are classed as Authority-serving instruments and tariffed at the instrument rate. Not the personal rate."

Fitch looked at her for a while. Then he pulled up the code on his terminal, because he was the kind of clerk who checked, and Piq liked him for it. She watched his lips move through paragraph four. They stopped moving at the part where it agreed with her.

"Your overage," he said slowly, "is exactly a calibration set."

"That's why I'm over."

"Exactly. To the gram."

"It would be strange if it weren't. I packed to the allowance and then added the standards. I was raised on a cycler." She said it the way other people said *I was raised Catholic*: as an explanation for a set of habits that would otherwise look like a disorder.

Fitch absorbed this. He gave the impression of a man filing a new category of passenger.

"Instrument rate is nine marks a kilo," he said. "But instruments have to be logged individually. Serial, nominal mass, measured mass, purpose." He slid a small certified scale across the counter, a palm-sized plate under a draft hood with a display that read to the tenth of a milligram. "Measured mass. Here. Now."

"Of course."

She did the small ones first, because that was correct practice and because it gave her hands something to do. The two-gram pins came in at 2.0000 and 2.0001. The hundred-gram read 100.0002, and the two-hundred read 199.9998. Fitch typed each into the ledger with two fingers, slowly, reading the serials aloud off the engraved bases as though they were the names of the dead.

Then she lifted the kilogram.

It was the largest of the five and the only one that mattered. It was machined from a nickel alloy and polished to the same mirror finish as its siblings. Inside it, in a cavity Lagrange's engineers had lined with tungsten shot until the whole assembly came in at nominal, sat a burst transceiver, a wideband receiver and four months of encryption pads. Somebody at L4 had spent eleven weeks getting it to one kilogram. Piq had spent two evenings on the inbound leg checking their work with a borrowed balance, because she did not trust anyone's mass but her own.

She set it under the draft hood. The plate took the weight a fraction of a second slower in this gravity than it would have on Luna, and the display ran through its digits like a man counting change.

**1000.0004 g.**

"Four tenths of a milligram over nominal," Fitch said.

"Within E2 tolerance. Half a milligram either way at one kilo."

He leaned close to the hood and looked at the cylinder. Piq knew what he was seeing, because she had looked at it the same way on the inbound leg. A true E2 kilogram in stainless steel was a squat little thing you could close your hand around. This one was a finger's width taller than it should have been, because a hollow object had to be bigger to weigh the same. Her father would have spotted it from across a cargo bay. *Mass is honest,* he used to say. *Volume is where people lie.*

Fitch did not know her father.

"It's bigger than the others," he said. "Proportionally."

"Different alloy." That was true. "Lower density." That was also true, in the aggregate. "The air buoyancy correction is in the certificate. Page two."

Fitch opened page two. Page two was a table of air densities, temperatures and correction factors in eight-point type. Piq had found in her working life that very few people would argue with a table of correction factors, and Fitch was not one of those few. He typed *1000.0004*. He typed *Calibration of Authority weighing equipment*. He typed the serial, which had been engraved on the base by a Lagrange machinist working from a genuine Ibis numbering sequence. Then he sat back.

"Total instrument mass, one thousand three hundred and four point zero zero zero five grams," he said. "Instrument rate, nine marks per kilo. That's eleven point seven three six zero zero four five marks."

"Eleven seventy-four."

"Eleven seventy-four, plus the rounding fee."

"There's a rounding fee?"

"Point four of a centimark is a fractional mark," Fitch said, "and fractional marks have to be rounded, and rounding is a service." He turned the terminal toward her. "Three copies. JDA Fiscal, Ibis Metrology, and the station instrument registry. Each one's a separate attestation. You're certifying under your Ibis seal that the declared items are metrological standards and nothing else, and that you'll present them for inspection on demand."

So there it was, in plain type: *nothing else.*

Piq picked up the stylus. She had signed a great many things in her career with Lagrange's Special Losses division, under her own name and under others, and she did not lose sleep over signatures. A signature was a mass declaration for a promise. It recorded what you were willing to be weighed for. The cylinder really did weigh one kilogram. It would really calibrate a load cell, if she ever pointed it at one. Every number on the form was true. She was lying only about what the number contained, and she was very good at that kind of lie, because it was the kind nobody ever thought to weigh.

She signed the first copy. She signed the second. The stylus slipped as she reached for the third and left her fingers, and she watched it go. It drifted down in a long, lazy arc, turning once end over end, so slowly that she had time to think *I could catch that* and then time to decide not to bother. It landed on the counter with a tap and rolled half a centimeter. Fitch did not even look up. Everyone on Callisto dropped things all the time, and nobody hurried after them.

She picked it up and signed the third.

Fitch pressed a small adhesive tag onto each cylinder's case slot, a printed square with the JDA crest and the word INSTRUMENT. The kilogram got one too. It sat on her contraband like a halo.

"Welcome to Asgard Yard, Auditor Varga," he said. "Dose intake's through the blue door. Don't argue with the warden."

"Why would I argue with the warden?"

Fitch gave her the look of a man who had just watched her argue her way through Schedule Nine, paragraph four.

"Everybody does," he said. "Once."

---

The blue door led to a room half the size of mass intake and twice as quiet. The walls were lined with something dense and gray. Piq guessed borated polyethylene behind the paint, from the faint plastic give when she brushed it with her knuckles. A single bench ran along one side. On the other stood a desk, a terminal, a rack of dosimeter badges in charging cradles like eggs in a carton, and a woman.

Piq catalogued her the way she catalogued freight, because it was faster than having an opinion.

She was perhaps a hundred and seventy centimeters tall and fifty-eight kilos, give or take a liter of water, built along the lean, long lines of someone who had grown up in the gravity of a real planet and then stopped eating like it. She was forty, or close to it, with gray coming in at both temples in a pattern too even to be stress and too early to be age. Her hair was dark and cut short, regulation length for a helmet seal. She wore a JDA medical service jumpsuit with the sleeves pushed up, and a dosimeter of her own hung on a cord around her neck, worn and scuffed, an older model than anything in the rack. A thin white scar crossed the back of her left hand. Her nametape read ANAND, T. — DOSE WARDEN. Her eyes were brown and fixed on the terminal, and they did not come up when Piq came in.

That came to a total. Piq added it twice, and both times it came out a little higher than neutral, which was annoying, because she had not asked it to come out anywhere at all.

"Varga," the dose warden said. "Ibis Metrology. Sit."

Piq sat. In 0.126 g the bench took her weight like a polite suggestion.

"You came in on the cycler *Ascending Node* to Ganymede, then the *Prudent Interval* transfer." Anand was reading, and her voice had the flat cadence of a recitation she had performed many times without ever coming to enjoy it. "Nineteen months of cycler transit under a water wall, which was adequate. Two days in Ganymede orbit inside the magnetosphere, which was not. Your lifetime accrued dose as of the shuttle's last telemetry is zero point one eight seven sieverts."

"That seems high."

"It is high. You've spent a lot of your life on cyclers." She still did not look up. "Your contract runs four months. JDA occupational guidance for non-radiation workers caps annual exposure at zero point zero five zero sieverts, and your lifetime trajectory puts you on the conservative curve, so I'm allocating you zero point zero one seven for the duration of your stay. That's seventeen millisieverts. Callisto's surface gives you about a third of one per day unshielded. Inside the berms you'll take almost nothing, and you'll spend almost nothing, and if you go outside without a reason I'll know."

"I'll have a reason," Piq said. "I'm auditing the depot."

"Then I'll know your reason too." Anand pulled a badge from the rack and held it out at arm's length, the way one offered a treat to a dog of uncertain temperament. "This logs dose, location and time. It talks to the station mesh every ninety seconds. You wear it at all times outside your quarters."

Piq did not take it. She took a folded document out of her jacket instead.

"I'd like to request a waiver," she said. "My Ibis contract specifies a self-calibrated personal dosimeter, certified against our own reference sources. Audit chain of custody. If my dose logs feed a station network, the station can alter them, and an audit has to be independent of the thing it's auditing." She held out the waiver form, already filled in, because she had filled it in on the inbound leg and had expected to need it. "I'll still report daily. You'd have my readings. You just wouldn't have my position."

That was the real point, and she had wrapped it as well as she knew how. A badge that pinged her location to the mesh every ninety seconds would hand anyone with mesh access, which meant JDA security, a perfect map of where an auditor went at night.

Now Anand looked up.

It was, Piq would think later, a little like the shuttle touching down. It took a long time in this gravity, and it was unmistakably contact.

"No," Anand said.

"You haven't read it."

"I've read forty of them. They all say the same thing. 'Chain of custody.' 'Independence.' 'Our own reference sources.'" She did not take the form. "You know who files dosimeter waivers, Auditor? People who want their dose to be a private matter between themselves and their employer. Contractors who want to work a double shift on the surface without me pulling them in. Ice crews who want to stand next to a reactor a little longer than they should, because the bonus is good and the bill comes in thirty years. And companies that want to own the number." Her voice did not rise. It got more precise. "On this station I own the number. I set it, I watch it, I'm the one who grounds you when you run out, and I'm the one who writes it down if you don't."

"I'm not trying to cheat a dose limit."

"Nobody is. Until they are."

"I'm an auditor," Piq said, and heard in her own voice the edge her father used to get when someone disputed a load cell. "I'm here to make sure numbers are true. That's the job."

Anand laughed. It was short and without much air in it.

"Ibis certifies for Lagrange Mutual," she said. "Half the scales your company stamps feed straight into Lagrange's risk tables. You count it, they price it, and somewhere down the line someone gets told their claim's denied because a decimal point didn't reconcile." She set the badge on the desk between them and slid it across. "Auditors and insurers. Same species of vulture. One of you lands after the thing's dead and counts what's left, and the other decides what it was worth."

It was a fair hit, fairer than Anand could know. Piq felt it land somewhere under the sternum and logged the mass of it without looking at it directly. There was a story behind that sentence. You did not get that much contempt into seven words without having paid for it. She filed that away too, under *?*.

"I'm not here to deny anyone anything," she said.

"Then you won't mind the badge."

Piq looked at it. She looked at the waiver in her own hand, which had been perfectly drafted and which nobody was going to read. She weighed the cost of pushing harder against the cost of being remembered as the auditor who pushed harder, and the second number was larger.

She took the badge.

The clip was stiff. She fumbled it against the thick seam of her collar, and Anand made a small, impatient sound, rose, and came around the desk. In this gravity she did it in two long strides that were almost a glide. She took the badge from Piq's fingers and pressed it onto the collar herself, two fingertips flat against the clip until it seated with a click. Her hand was warm. It smelled faintly of nitrile and of the ozone that hung over everything here. It stayed against Piq's collarbone for perhaps half a second longer than the clip required, or perhaps exactly as long, and Piq could not tell which, and that bothered her more than the badge did.

"There," Anand said. She stepped back. "Zero point zero one seven. Don't make me come find you."

"Is that a threat?"

"It's a budget." The dose warden was already sitting down again, eyes back on the terminal. "On this station they're the same thing. Next."

There was no next. The room was empty except for the two of them. Piq stood up and went out through the far door, and she did not look back. She was proud of that for nearly eleven meters.

---

Her quarters were in Ring C, two levels down from the surface, at the end of a corridor that curved slightly to follow the buried contour of the berm. They measured six cubic meters, which the station directory described as *efficient*. There was a fold-down bunk, a fold-down desk and a fold-down sink, and there was a wall locker that did not fold down and so looked faintly embarrassed among the rest.

The door would not close.

She pushed it. It slid three-quarters of the way and stopped with a gritty, grinding hesitation. Piq crouched and ran her thumb along the seal track. It came away black. The regolith here was fine as flour and charged with old static, and it got into everything. It worked into the rubber lip of the seal in a dark, glittering line. She picked at it with a thumbnail, cleared enough to get the door to seat, and heard the soft pneumatic sigh of the gasket meeting its frame. On a cycler a bad seal was an emergency. Here it was apparently a Tuesday.

There was a port. She had not expected one. It was a narrow slot of thick laminated glass set high in the wall at the end of a light pipe that angled up through the berm to the surface. When she stood on the edge of the bunk she could look up the shaft and out.

The outer pane was frosted. Fine feathers of rime grew across it from the corners inward, where warm air leaking from the habitat met the cold of the glass and gave up its water. Through the frost, through the gap at the center where the rime had not yet closed, Jupiter hung low over the basin rim, banded and immense and absolutely still. It was in exactly the place she had seen it from the shuttle, an hour and a quarter before and four hundred kilometers away. It would be in that place tomorrow, and in a month, and for the rest of her life if she stayed. The frost on the glass caught its light and broke it into a thousand pale copper threads.

She watched it for a while. Then she got down, unpacked her duffel, laid out her tally pad and stylus on the fold-down desk, and opened the instrument case.

The kilogram came apart at a seam so fine that only someone who had been told where to look could have found it. She twisted the cap a quarter-turn against a detent, then another quarter-turn the other way, and it lifted free. Inside, in its nest of tungsten shot, the transceiver's status light came up a patient amber. She keyed her passphrase, a cycler weigh-master's mnemonic for the trim tables of the *Long Patience*, and the briefing unpacked onto her slate.

Augustin Pell wrote the way he weighed risk, as though every word were billed by the gram and the society were short of grams.

*VARGA.*

*FILE SL-2214-0391. ASGARD YARD, VALHALLA DEPOT.*

*Depot ice inventory reported to Authority and to us flat for six quarterly cycles. Our independent tonnage estimate, from Sling throw logs against consignee receipts, does not support flat. Shortfall indicated, magnitude unknown, est. hundreds of tonnes. Not declared. Not claimed. Not explained.*

*Attached: intercept fragment. Acquired through commercial intercept broker (Halloran Clearance, Ceres). Purchased in a mixed lot. Provenance unverified. Originating station unknown. Confidence in authenticity moderate. Confidence in meaning low.*

*Text, in full: "throw before conjunction, no one will see it until it's done."*

*Two readings.*

*One. Weapon. The Sling throws ballistic. A shelled slug on an unlisted inner-system trajectory is a kinetic weapon with a multi-year fuse. Undetectable once released if released during conjunction blackout. Target unknown.*

*Two. Fraud. Mass declared thrown, never thrown, then declared lost en route and claimed against our cargo book. Or a hull loss staged around a throw. Undetectable until filed.*

*Either costs the society. The first costs more than money.*

*Jupiter solar conjunction: Mars relay begins degraded batching shortly after your arrival, total blackout 38 days from your landing. Blackout duration 19 days. During blackout you will have no line to me and I will have no line to you.*

*Identify the throw. Identify who. Report early and often. Use the cover. You hold it legitimately; use the legitimacy.*

*Trust the ledger. Not the people in it.*

*PELL.*

She read it twice. The second time she heard it in his voice, which she had met in person exactly once, across a conference table at L4 under the long gray light of the habitat's false afternoon. It was a dry voice with all the commas taken out, the voice of a man who had spent forty years discovering that the warmest thing you could do for an adjuster in the field was to tell her the truth with nothing on it. *Trust the ledger. Not the people in it.* On the page it read like an order to be cold. Piq suspected that in Pell's head it read like *be careful*. She also suspected that the difference between the two would cost somebody something before this was over. She filed that under *?* as well. The column was getting long.

Thirty-eight days. She wrote it on the tally pad and boxed it.

*Throw before conjunction.* It was an ugly little sentence. It had been written by someone who expected nobody to look up until the thing was done, and who had perhaps been right about that until a broker on Ceres had sold it in a mixed lot to the one institution in the solar system whose whole business was looking up.

She reached for the stylus. It rolled away from her fingers, off the edge of the fold-down desk, and began its descent, a slow pale arc through the cabin air, turning once. She watched it all the way to the floor. In thirty-eight days, she thought, she would be alone with whatever it was. She picked the stylus up and put it in her breast pocket where it couldn't leave.

---

Station night on Asgard was a convention, not a fact. The sun would not set for another five Earth days. The corridor lights in Ring C dimmed to amber at twenty-two hundred by station clock, and the ventilation dropped half a note, and people went to bed because the lights said so.

Piq did not go to bed.

Her Ibis credential opened the depot ledger at read-only depth, which was all an honest auditor would need and therefore all she would ask for. It was a genuine credential; she had earned it, at a genuine desk, by genuinely failing the practical exam once and passing it the second time. The ledger came up on her slate in the standard JDA layout, columns of tonnage running back six years. She started where her father had always started, with the conservation equation, because mass did not care who you worked for.

*Opening inventory, plus intake, minus outflow, minus declared losses, equals closing inventory.*

Intake came from the harvester crawlers working the ice-rich crater floors to the south, block by block, each block weighed at the pad scales and tagged. Outflow was the Sling, which threw shelled slugs of ice at a few kilometers per second toward buyers who would wait years to receive them. There were also propellant sales, ice cracked and sold as hydrogen and oxygen to tugs and tankers, and the station's own draw, the water that became air and drink and shielding for nineteen hundred people. Declared losses were the small honest leaks of any ice store: allowance sublimation, handling spill and the shell fraction rejected at the Sling breech.

She took the last quarter first, the smallest and freshest, and reconciled it line by line. Intake against pad-scale tags. Throws against breech logs. Propellant sales against buyer receipts, which she pulled from the trade register. Station draw against the life-support plant's own electrolysis meters, which logged the ozone-scented work of turning ice into breath in increments of a tenth of a kilogram.

It did not close.

She checked her transcription, then the tags. She checked the calendar alignment, because quarterly figures were sometimes cut at different hours by different departments, and a few hours of Sling throws could vanish into the seam between two reporting periods. That accounted for nine tonnes, and there was more left over than that.

She took the quarter before, and the one before that. She did all six cycles in which the reported inventory had sat flat, perfectly and improbably flat, the same number to the tonne from one quarter to the next, as if someone had decided what the depot ought to hold and stopped weighing.

Every quarter ran short. None of them was short by much. Fifty tonnes, sixty, forty, seventy. Spread across a depot that held tens of thousands, each shortfall was smaller than the error bars on the survey lidar. Each one, alone, could have been rounding.

Piq did not believe in rounding. Rounding was a service, and somebody always paid for it.

She ran it a third time, from scratch, by hand on the tally pad in her father's shorthand, because a slate could carry a mistake forward forever and a hand could not. The corridor lights had long since gone amber. Somewhere above her, through two levels of fill and a frosted pane, Jupiter hung where it always hung. On the depot's sunward terraces the frost that should not have been there went on not making sense. She wrote the columns and added them and carried the totals and subtracted, and at the bottom of the last page she wrote the figure the equation demanded and did not write the equals sign.

The reported inventory said the depot held what it had always held. The weights said otherwise, and the weights did not lie. Something less honest had been standing between them.

She underlined the number twice and sat back. The cabin hummed. Her badge ticked off a dose so small it barely registered.

Over six quarters, without a claim or a declaration or a single line anywhere that admitted it, three hundred and forty tonnes of water ice had gone missing from the Valhalla depot.

## Chapter 2: The Ledger

The depot ledger was honest, and that was the problem with it.

Piq had spent the small hours reconciling it by hand. She had gone to sleep with the number 340 sitting in her chest like a swallowed stone. She woke at 0600 station convention with the stone still there and the sun exactly where she had left it, a third of the way up a black sky. Callisto did not do mornings. It did shifts.

She read the ledger again over reconstituted tea that tasted of the electrolysis plant, faintly metallic and faintly of struck matches. Every entry carried a timestamp, a scale ID, an operator code and a countersignature. The scales had been calibrated on schedule. Ibis had certified them, and therefore so had she, in a sense, as a member of the profession. Each quarter the inflows from the regolith processors matched the outflows to the Sling cradle and the propellant farm within tolerance. Then each quarter the standing inventory, the great buried mass of the depot itself, came out forty to seventy tonnes lighter than the arithmetic said it should. Someone had then quietly restated it to match last quarter's figure.

Six times. Flat as a tabletop. Nobody lies with a flat line by accident.

She wrote in the margin of her tally pad, in her father's shorthand: *Books clean, pile short. Not a scale fault. Someone restates. Who signs the restate?* She checked. The restatements carried the Director's office code and no name. She added a question mark to the column that already held the frost, the dose warden and Pell's tone. Then she put the pad in her thigh pocket and went to do the job she was actually being paid for. That was the job that would give her reasons to be everywhere.

---

The pads were a set of pressurized bays cut into the basin rim. Each opened onto the surface through a lock wide enough to swallow a tug's cargo frame. Piq's Ibis schedule had her verifying outbound manifests on Lagrange-rated cargo: weigh the frame, check the declared mass against the rated mass, sign or don't. The work was boring and precise and almost entirely real. She liked it more than she would have admitted to anyone at L4.

Bay Four held a survey-support tug called the *Paper Lantern*, licensed by the JDA for a run to Europa orbit with relay buoys and a crew of three. Its cargo frame stood on the scale deck under the work lights. Strapped around the crew can were six blue bladders, each the size of a sleeping person, sweating faintly in the cold.

"Twenty-two point four tonnes of water," said the captain, a woman named Galloway with a shaved head and a voice that had been sanded down by years of shouting over pumps. "Twenty-two point four tonnes I can't drink, can't burn, and can't sell. Go on. Weigh it. Weigh it twice. It doesn't get lighter."

Piq weighed it. The deck scale read the frame at 61.218 tonnes. She checked the bladders individually against their tags with the handheld load cell, walking the cell from strap to strap while the mate held the bladders steady. At 0.126 g a person could shoulder a tonne. Steadying it was another matter, because a tonne swinging at walking pace still wanted to keep going.

"It's the clause," said the mate, Szabo, to nobody in particular. He was young and thick-wristed and had the low-g build that ran to height. "It's always the clause."

"Lagrange's water," Galloway said. "That's what we call it. Lagrange's water. You know what it costs us? I put that on the frame and I lose, what, Pritt?"

The engineer didn't look up from her tablet. "Two-eighty, two-ninety meters a second on the outbound leg. More on the return if we burn heavy to make the window."

"Two hundred and ninety meters a second," Galloway told Piq, as if Piq had personally carried each bladder aboard. "So we run soft. Lower energy, longer transit. And the longer we're in there—"

"The more water the schedule wants," Pritt said. "Yes."

Piq finished the last strap, noted the figure, and lowered the load cell. She could have let it go. Auditors who argue get remembered. But she had been raised in the weigh-master's office of the *Long Patience*, with the trim tables on the bulkhead. Some things she had learned before she learned to lie, and they came out of her the way her father's sayings did, whole and without asking.

"Clause Fourteen, sub c," she said. "Any hull rated under the Society and bound for the Europa environment shall carry shielding water of not less than the scheduled minimum for its crew complement. The minimum scales with projected transit time inside the torus. The water is to be disposed about the crew volume and is not to be counted as propellant reserve."

Szabo looked at her the way people look at a dog that has started reciting poetry.

"The schedule wasn't drawn up in an afternoon," Piq went on. "It's forty years of crew dose data off every hull Lagrange has ever rated in the Jovian system. You get about five sieverts a day on Europa's trailing hemisphere. Inside the torus, in transit, it's less, but you're there for days and it adds. Two hundred and ninety meters a second is a cost. A crew with marrow failure is a total loss." She paused. "And it's not to be counted as propellant, because the first thing a captain does when she's short of delta-v is burn her shielding. The clause assumes you'll be tempted. It's right to."

There was a short silence. A drop of condensation slid off the nearest bladder and fell toward the deck. It took long enough that Piq could have counted it in.

"She's got the whole thing by heart," Szabo said.

"She's an auditor," said Galloway. "They don't have hearts, they have schedules." She signed the manifest anyway, hard, so the stylus squeaked. "Water keeps you alive. Fine. Tell that to the meters per second."

"I'll put it in my report," Piq said, and made a note. The note read only *Paper Lantern, Bay 4, 61.218 t, in tolerance.* She believed the clause, the way she believed in the mass of a kilogram. The grumbling was what crews did with numbers they couldn't change. Her father had heard it every day for thirty-one years. *They'll argue with the scale, Piq. Let them. The scale doesn't care.*

---

The comms office sat one level below the pads, in a bay that smelled of warm electronics and somebody's lentils. Piq had two reasons to be there. Ibis audit uploads went out through station bandwidth, so she had to register her traffic class. And she needed to see the shape of the pipe she would be talking down for the next five weeks.

The duty tech was a round, delighted man named Benno Lisk, who introduced himself while still eating.

"Ibis! Bulk class, then. Routine." He spooned lentils with one hand and tapped her into the system with the other. "You'll want to get the heavy stuff out early. Has anybody told you about the relay?"

"Tell me about the relay," Piq said, because chatty people were a gift and you didn't refuse gifts.

Lisk put down the spoon. "Right now we've got a clean line to Earth-Luna, forty-five, forty-six minutes one way, getting longer. Jupiter's sliding round toward the far side of the Sun from home, yes? Line of sight gets closer and closer to the solar disc, and the Sun is very loud. Over the next couple of weeks it gets noisy enough that we stop talking straight to Earth and start bouncing everything through Mars."

"The store-and-forward relay."

"Deimos Relay, God bless it. They take everything from everybody out here, us, Ur, the Trojans, every tug with a transmitter. They stack it up, and when their window to Earth opens they send it. But they don't send it in the order it came in." He held up a lentil-flecked finger. "They sort. Priority first: JDA flagged, Compact flagged, medical, safety of life. Then small packets ahead of big ones, because they can fit small ones into the gaps. Big fat routine bulk goes when there's room. And the closer we get to conjunction, the less room there is."

"And then it goes dark."

"Nineteen days." He said it with relish, the way people say the number of stitches they once had. "No line. Sun's right in the way. Deimos holds what it's got and we hold what we've got, and everybody plays cards. Your last week before blackout, a big upload might sit at Deimos four, five days before it goes. Might sit right through blackout. Nobody minds. Who's in a hurry for a calibration table?"

"Nobody," Piq said, and meant it in two ways.

Every covert report she sent would go out the way Lagrange had taught her. She would encrypt it by hand against a one-time crypto pad, then wrap it inside forty or fifty megabytes of perfectly genuine Ibis bulk: scale drift logs, temperature compensation curves, the full certification history of every load cell in the depot. Nobody on Callisto would bother to open that, and nobody at Deimos would bother to prioritize it. The padding made the reports invisible. That was the point. She filed Lisk's explanation in the part of her mind where cover details lived. *Bulk goes late. Good. Late is quiet.*

"Bulk class," she said. "Routine. Thank you."

"Ibis bulk class," said a voice behind her. "The most reassuring words in the language."

She turned at an unhurried pace. She knew he had chosen the moment.

He was in his fifties, pale in the way of people who were born in a spin habitat and never browned since. He was slim, with a grey JDA security jacket cut well enough that someone had paid to have it cut. His hair was close-cropped iron. His face was pleasant and his eyes were not quite. He held out a hand at a height that made her lean slightly to take it.

"Marek Saar. I run security for the Authority here. I like to meet everyone who comes down on the *Interval*. It's a small station. I'd hate to be a stranger."

"Piq Varga. Ibis Metrology."

"I know." He smiled. Piq had sat across from loss adjusters, fraud investigators and two customs services. She had met very few smiles that took inventory. This one did. It went to the Ibis seal on her lapel and the dosimeter badge clipped to her collar. It went to the tally pad in her thigh pocket, the scuff of regolith on her left boot and the slight hollow in her right cheek where she bit it when she was thinking. Then it came back to her eyes, having logged everything and countersigned it.

"I saw your intake," he said. "Five reference masses. A full set. We rarely get that level of care."

"The depot scales are overdue for an independent check," Piq said. "Ibis likes its auditors to bring their own truth."

"Do they." He seemed pleased. "I've always liked auditors. You write everything down. It makes my job so much easier." He let a beat pass. "You're on the depot, the pads, and the Sling, I understand. Anything farther afield?"

"Farther afield than the Sling?"

"Some of our visitors develop an interest in the inner moons." He said it lightly, as though they were talking about a cold. "So, for the avoidance of doubt: Europa is a JDA exclusion zone under the Compact claim of 2209. Nothing lands, nothing loiters, nothing transmits without an Authority license. If you hear anyone talking about Europa in a way that worries you, I'd be very grateful to know about it."

"I'm a mass auditor, Mr. Saar. The only thing I want to know about Europa is how much it weighs."

"Four point eight times ten to the twenty-two kilograms," Saar said. "Give or take. And it's Marek." He tapped two fingers on Lisk's console. The tech, who had been chewing very quietly, stopped altogether. "Your reference masses. I'm sure they're exactly what the attestation says. I'd love to see them sometime. Professional curiosity."

"Any time," said Piq. "They're presented for inspection on demand."

"So they are." The smile again, a little warmer, which was worse. "Welcome to Asgard."

He left. Lisk breathed out and resumed his lentils with the air of a man who had just watched a large animal pass by without stopping to eat him.

"He does that," Lisk said. "Meets everybody. It's very friendly."

"Very," said Piq.

She went back to her quarters by the long way, and on the way she took apart, in her head, the fifth of her five reference masses: the one-kilogram steel cylinder that was not a reference mass at all. Its end cap came off with a quarter-turn and a reverse quarter-turn, on threads cut fine enough to pass for a machining seam. Inside it was hollow, and the hollow held Lagrange's kit: the burst transceiver, the folded antenna lead, the crypto pad, the wideband receiver. Hollow steel weighs less than solid steel, so the cylinder had been made a finger's width taller than a true one-kilogram billet. A collar of tungsten shot packed around the cavity made up the difference. On a calibrated balance it would weigh exactly one kilogram, which was all the intake officer had checked. A densitometer did not care what a thing weighed. It mapped what a thing was made of, slice by slice, and a real reference mass read as one uniform block of steel. This one would show a dense ring of tungsten, an empty core and a tidy little constellation of electronics. Hold it up to a densitometer and it would light up like a festival.

*Presented for inspection on demand.* She had signed that three times.

She added a fifth line to the question-mark column: *Saar. Knows the attestation by heart.*

---

Hesper Okafor-Lund met her at the north lock at 1300 with two crawler suits and a thermos.

The chief engineer of the Sling was somewhere past sixty. She was broad through the shoulders and carried herself as though she had spent decades lifting things in gravities that made lifting dishonest. Her white hair was cropped to stubble and she had a quiet voice. Piq soon learned that she never raised it, because she never had to. People leaned toward Hesper to hear her.

"You've seen it from the shuttle," Hesper said as they suited up. "That's nothing. From the shuttle it's a line."

The crawler was an open-frame rover with a pressurized cab the size of a coffin for two. Hesper drove it with one hand along a graded track. On both sides the regolith of the Valhalla basin lay dark and churned, old impact ejecta beaten flat by four billion years and a decade of construction. Jupiter hung where it always hung, low over the western rim, banded in rust and cream and a pale ochre. It was enormous and perfectly still, a door that had been painted onto the sky. Its light fell on the frost at the edges of the track and turned it the color of weak tea.

Then the ground fell away on their left, and there was the Sling.

It ran north, a straight-edged trench cut into the basin floor. It was so straight that the eye kept trying to find where it bent, and it never bent. Ring after ring of coil housings marched along it, each a squat grey torus bolted to a sintered regolith footing. The gap between them shrank with distance until they blurred into a single bright seam that met the horizon. Forty kilometers, Hesper said. Piq knew the number already. Knowing it was different from seeing a straight line run over the edge of the world.

"Twelve-tonne slugs," Hesper said. She stopped the crawler at a pullout above the cradle end, where a gantry stood over the first coils. "Ice, pressed and frozen, in a sintered shell. The shell takes the acceleration and spalls off on the way out, like a seed husk. We throw them at six point one kilometers a second. It's ugly to stand next to and very beautiful to watch from here."

"What's the acceleration?"

"Forty-six g, peak." Hesper poured tea from the thermos into a bulb and passed it over. "Ice doesn't mind. Ice is the most patient thing in the solar system. A slug we throw this month gets shepherded by a tug at Jupiter departure. Then it falls inward for two years, three, four, depending on the customer. Somebody at Ceres or Vesta or a Martian cooperative catches it and drinks it, or breathes it, or burns it, or wraps it around a ship. We'll never meet them." She drank. "I like that. It's like writing letters."

Piq watched her face. She was used to people talking about their work as a job, or a cost, or a grievance. Hesper talked about the Sling the way her father had talked about the *Long Patience*'s trim, as a living thing you kept in balance by paying attention.

"The crews are young," Hesper said, and nodded toward the gantry. Figures in suits moved there, slow and deliberate, walking with the long careful glide of people who had learned low g properly. "Most of them came up through my school. Loading, coil alignment, cradle work, then pilots. I've trained four classes of tug pilots off this pad." She paused, briefly, the way a person pauses on a stair they know is uneven. "Good ones. The best I've had. You could hand them a bad trajectory and a worse tug and they'd bring it back."

"You keep track of them."

"I keep track of everybody I've ever sent out of the cradle." Hesper looked at her, and for a moment the quiet voice had something under it like rock under regolith. "That's what the job is, auditor. Everything you throw, you're responsible for where it lands."

Piq said nothing, and made a note of the nothing.

They drove the first two kilometers of the trench at walking pace. Hesper named coil sections the way other people name children's friends. She stopped twice to put a gloved hand flat against a housing and wait, listening through her palm for something Piq couldn't hear. On the drive back she pointed out the depot: the low terraced mound of the ice pile, its vast black sunshade canted overhead like a wing. Piq looked for the frost on the east face and found it, a thin pale crust where the sun got in under the shade's lower edge.

"That frost," she said. "On the sunward side."

Hesper glanced at it the way she might have glanced at a stranger's coil housing, interested but not responsible. "Out-gassing condenses wherever it finds a cold surface."

"It's the sunward side."

"Hm." Hesper squinted, and her free hand made a small flattening gesture against the dash, the same one she had made against the coils. "Then the shade's not seating at the lower edge and you're getting sun on the face. Thermal cycling, probably. That wing belongs to Facilities, not me." She drove on. "I'll put in a ticket. They'll lose it. They lose all my tickets on the shade."

"All of them?"

"It's a big wing, auditor. It doesn't throw anything, so nobody loves it." She said it with an engineer's mild contempt for any machine that wasn't hers, and moved on to the cradle's coolant loop.

Piq didn't push. She noted that the chief engineer of the Sling had not known about the frost, had diagnosed it in under ten seconds, and had then dismissed it as somebody else's problem. She also noted *They lose all my tickets on the shade*. Two clean facts from the same answer, and neither of them explained a damned thing yet.

---

The afternoon was where the station punished her for being competent.

There was a message waiting when she came in from the lock. It came from the Director's office in an upbeat tone that suggested a staffer had been told to be nice to the Ibis auditor. Asgard's annual residential mass declaration spot-check was due. The JDA's own assessor had rotated out to Ganymede. Since a certified Ibis auditor was conveniently on station, would she be so kind? Twenty households, random selection, standard schedule. The Director would be *so grateful*. The message was signed with the office code, the same code that had restated the depot six times in a row, and with a name: *Ilse Rook, Director.*

It was not part of her contract. Refusing would have been perfectly legal and a little odd. Piq took the handheld densitometer out of her Ibis case and went door to door through Ring B.

Asgard hid mass the way old ships hid rust: everywhere, a little at a time, and with great ingenuity.

The first household declared 211 kg of personal effects. Piq's sweep found 214. The difference was a hollow ballast brick on the windowsill, holding four hundred grams of saffron in a vacuum sleeve. The owner was a soft-spoken processing tech. He explained that the brick was *decorative*. The saffron, he said, had been a gift from his mother, who had died. Piq logged it at the spice rate. He paid, and then he gave her a pinch, which she did not log.

The second household had an accordion.

It was not on any manifest. It had not been on the shuttle manifest that brought its owner up from Ganymede six years ago. By its owner's own admission it had come down from orbit inside a hollowed-out spare oxygen candle. It weighed 9.2 kg, which at the personal tariff was a little under four thousand marks.

"It's a family instrument," said its owner. She was a large, red-faced woman who ran the hydroponics night shift. She said it with her arms folded across the accordion as though Piq might try to repossess it on the spot.

"It's still nine point two kilograms," Piq said.

"It's my grandmother's nine point two kilograms."

"I don't doubt it. I have to log it."

"Log it as *ballast*," the woman said, and then, with dawning cunning, "It's *trim*. For the ring. You need mass distributed. I'm distributing it."

Piq considered this. It was very nearly a good argument. "The ring has a trim schedule. You're not on it."

"Put me on it."

Piq put her on it. She logged the accordion as *distributed rotational trim, musical*, at the instrument rate of nine marks per kilogram, under Schedule Nine, paragraph four. That was very nearly the same thing she had done for herself at intake, and she was aware of it. The woman insisted on playing her something, as either a thank-you or a threat. It was a Latvian folk tune, mournful and slow. In a 6 m³ room at 0.126 g it was very loud.

By the seventh household Piq had found three more decorative ballast bricks. One held saffron, one held tea, and one held, inexplicably, more ballast. She found a set of cast-iron cookware listed on a manifest as *heat sink*. A Martian-made bicycle had been declared at its dismantled frame weight, minus the wheels, which the owner claimed were *circular art*. She found nothing that came anywhere near three hundred and forty tonnes. Nobody in Ring B was eating a depot one ballast brick at a time.

It was the ducts that caught her.

The residential spot-check schedule included a volumetric sweep of common spaces: corridors, ducts, crawlways. It was meant to catch exactly what she was actually hunting, ice quietly bled off the depot and cached somewhere it could be moved later. Piq ran the densitometer along the duct runs of Ring B's eastern quadrant with real interest. She found condensation, a lot of it, beading on the inside of the ducts and slicking the access plates. She noted that too, because she noted everything.

At Junction 14, where a main air return dropped into the ring's lower plenum, the densitometer chirped and showed her a number.

*4.1 kg. Hydrated. Mean density ~1.0. Warm.*

She looked at it for a long time.

Hydrated, near the density of water. Ice would read cold, unless it had been bagged and insulated, unless someone was keeping a small cache inside the warm air return so it wouldn't frost the duct and give itself away. It was four kilograms, a sample, a test run. She thought of the flat line in the ledger and the restatements under the Director's office code, and her pulse picked up.

She signed out an access tool from the ring maintenance locker. She logged it, because she always logged it.

With the tool in her hand she felt the small double tick of her badge against her collarbone, the mesh handshake it made every ninety seconds, and she stopped. The badge was a dosimeter first and a locator second, and the locator was not shy. Somewhere in the station mesh a log was already building a tidy column of entries: *VARGA, P., Ring B east, Junction 14*, every ninety seconds for as long as she stayed there. Anyone with mesh access could read it, and Saar had mesh access the way he had a jacket. This time it didn't matter. A volumetric duct sweep was on the Director's own schedule, and the log would read like an auditor doing exactly what she'd been asked. But Lagrange had a phrase for a cover that only held because the paperwork happened to agree with you: *borrowed luck*. She filed the badge in the column of things that would matter later, then unbolted the plate at Junction 14 and wormed into the air return headfirst with a lamp in her teeth.

The duct was half a meter square and lined with dust. It smelled of warm metal, recycled breath and something faintly musky she couldn't place. She went in up to her hips and then her knees. In low g a body in a duct isn't heavy so much as unwieldy, all elbows and inertia. Every push carried her a little farther than she meant to go, and she had to brake with her palms against the walls.

Three meters in, where the return opened into a junction box warmed by a heat exchanger, the lamp found the cache.

It was curled in a nest of shredded insulation and what looked like at least two stolen thermal blankets. It was striped brown and black and orange like a tabby rug. It had one ear with a notch out of the tip, a white bib, and yellow eyes that regarded her from a distance of forty centimeters with the total calm of a creature that has never once been made to declare itself.

It was a cat.

Piq lay in the duct with the lamp in her teeth and did the arithmetic anyway, because she couldn't help it. Roughly four kilograms. Roughly sixty percent water. Mean density near one. Warm. The densitometer had been exactly right, and she had been exactly wrong. Her father would have laughed until he had to sit down. *You read the number. Then you look at the thing. In that order, Piq. Never only the first one.*

"You," she said around the lamp, "are not on any manifest."

The cat yawned at her. It showed a great many teeth.

There was no way this animal had come down legally. Every kilogram landing at Asgard was weighed. Pets were technically possible and catastrophically expensive: four kilograms at personal tariff was sixteen hundred marks just to land it, and then there were air, water and protein. It would have had to be smuggled in a case or a crate, or born here. The notch in its ear was old and healed. It had been in these ducts for years, living on warm air and whatever it could find, and nobody had ever entered it into anything.

She thought about logging it. She thought about what logging it would mean: a report to the Director's office, a question about why the Ibis auditor had been three meters inside Ring B's air return, and an answer in her own handwriting with Saar's smile reading it. Beside that answer would sit the mesh log, which would put her at Junction 14 for exactly as long as she was taking.

The cat had moved while she was thinking. It rose, unhurried, and stretched with its front legs out and its rear in the air. It took two steps toward her and sniffed delicately at her chest pocket. The protein bar she'd forgotten was in there was apricot-flavored and had cost her forty grams of her personal allowance.

"No," said Piq.

The cat hooked a claw into the pocket's flap, flipped it open with insulting skill, and took the bar in its teeth.

"*No,*" said Piq, and grabbed. In a duct, at 0.126 g, with the lamp in her teeth and her arms pinned at her sides by sheet metal, she achieved nothing. She slid half a meter backward, struck her head on the duct ceiling and lost the lamp.

The cat turned with the bar and gathered itself. It leapt.

It was the slowest, most graceful thing she had ever seen an animal do. The cat went up and over her, its body a long striped bow, through the lamp's tumbling beam. It rose and rose against the duct's dim upper corner. It seemed to hang there for a long second, ears flat, tail streaming, the apricot bar clamped in its jaw like a trophy. Then it came down in a soft unhurried curve past her shoulder and onto the far wall. It pushed off again without a sound and vanished down the plenum into the dark.

The lamp landed on her face.

Piq lay in the duct for a moment longer, breathing dust. Then she took out the tally pad and wrote, by feel: *Ring B, Junction 14 return, 4.1 kg, hydrated, warm. Biological. Feline. Undeclared. Owner: ?* She looked at it. Then she drew a careful bracket around it and wrote beside the bracket, in the shorthand she used for things she didn't yet know how to file: *query.*

The densitometer was still chirping softly on her wrist. It had begun to register her.

---

At 2200 by station convention she sat cross-legged on her fold-down bunk in her 6 m³ quarters, in thermal leggings and an undershirt. Her jumpsuit hung on the hook by the door with the badge still clipped to its collar. Jupiter was a hard banded disc behind the frosted light-pipe. The steel cylinder sat on the bunk beside her, its end cap off.

The first report to Pell went out in pieces. She encrypted it on the burst transceiver from the crypto pad, page fourteen, line two. She wrapped it in forty-six megabytes of genuine Ibis bulk: drift logs from the depot load cells, the *Paper Lantern*'s manifest, and the full residential spot-check, accordion and all. She queued it under her registered traffic class, *Ibis bulk, routine*, so that it would go out with the rest of the station's commercial traffic and look exactly like the rest of the station's commercial traffic. Lisk would never open it. Deimos would sort it with the other fat packets and send it when it had room.

*Late is quiet,* she thought, and was satisfied.

Inside the wrapper the report was short, because Pell liked short.

*SL-2214-0391. Arrived. Cover holding. Depot inventory short 340 t over six quarters, restated flat each quarter under Director's office code, no name. Not scale fault. Not yet traced. Sling, pads, depot crew under observation. JDA security (Saar) attentive; aware of my instrument attestation, which is a vulnerability. Europa reiterated as exclusion zone, unprompted. Frost on sunward depot face; sunshade suspected, cause unknown. Proceeding.*

She paused, thought about it, and added a line beneath. It was half a joke and half a genuine cover problem. Pell would answer it either way, because Pell answered everything.

*Cover query: residential spot-check found undeclared feline, 4.1 kg, in ventilation. Logging would draw attention to my duct access. Not logging is a false attestation if discovered. Advise.*

She sent it and watched the queue mark it *sent—routine*.

Then, because Lagrange had trained her to do it every night and because she was awake anyway, she plugged the wideband receiver into the cylinder's power cell, threaded its antenna lead up through the light-pipe housing, and ran a sweep.

The station mesh was a wall of noise on the bands she expected: badge pings every ninety seconds, including her own from the hook by the door; crawler telemetry; the Sling's coil controllers chattering to one another; the pads; the long thin hiss of the downlink carrier to Ganymede. She let the receiver walk upward through the spectrum. She did it the way her father had walked the trim tables, line by line, not hurrying, because the point of a sweep is that you don't skip.

Above the mesh, there were bands nobody used anymore. The station's frequency allocation table had them listed as retired. One was an old telemetry channel from the first construction phase. One was a disused beacon band from the original survey. One, labeled in the table with a dry little footnote, was the *dosimetry maintenance band*. Before the mesh upgrade, every personal dosimeter on Asgard had used it to send its calibration checks to the dose warden's office. It had been retired four years ago, when the badges went onto the mesh. It should have been silent.

It wasn't.

The receiver's display showed her a line of noise, flat as the depot ledger. In the middle of the line, faint, almost below the floor, there was a pulse.

She waited and watched it come again. And again: short, long, short, short, a pause, then short again. It was not regular in the way an automated beacon is regular. It had a slight hesitation between some groups, and the spacing varied in a way her ear wanted to call deliberate. It was very faint, at the edge of what the receiver could find, as though whatever sent it was either far away or very weak, or both.

Someone was keying on a band no one should be using.

Piq sat very still. She looked at the badge on her jumpsuit collar across the room, telling the station mesh every ninety seconds that Piq Varga was in her quarters, as far as anyone could prove, asleep. Tonight that was true enough. But tomorrow she meant to follow somebody, and the badge would follow her, a ping at every junction, and Saar would only have to read the column. She could leave it on the hook. Then the mesh would put her safely in bed while she was elsewhere, which was a good alibi right up until someone knocked. It would also log zero dose for every hour it hung there, and the dose warden, who had already refused her one waiver, did not seem like a woman who missed a zero. She could wrap it in something wet and dense to choke the signal, but a badge that went quiet in a corridor was louder than a badge that pinged. None of the options balanced. She wrote *BADGE* at the bottom of the question-mark column and ruled a line under it.

She was aware of the 340 tonnes, and of Saar's smile, and of Hesper's hand flat against a coil housing in the cold, listening for something only Hesper could hear.

She took out the tally pad and began a new sheet. She wrote the date, the station hour and the band. Under that she ruled a column for time and a column for duration. She made the first mark, then the second, then the third.

At the top of the page, above everything else, she wrote one word in her father's shorthand and underlined it twice.

*Who.*

Beside it, on the right margin where she kept the running count, she wrote the other number she would be counting from now on.

*Day 2 of 38.*

## Chapter 3: Pattern

By the fourth night the log sheet had become six log sheets, clipped to the fold-down desk with a binder clip that Piq had declared at intake as eleven grams of stationery. The pulses lived on them in her father's shorthand. Each line carried a time stamp to the second, a duration, a pattern code and a signal-to-noise estimate she had stopped trusting after the first hour. A column at the right edge held her doubts.

The signal was not doing what signals were supposed to do. It did not keep a schedule. It came in clusters of eight or twelve or twenty marks, then nothing for forty minutes, then a single short and a long as if someone had sat down, thought better of it, and stood up again. The marks were hand-keyed. She was sure of that by the second night. A machine would have been either perfect or broken in a consistent way. This was neither. The long marks stretched when the sender seemed tired, and there was a recurring hitch, short-short-pause-short, that she had begun to think of as a stammer.

She tried Morse first because everyone tried Morse first, and got VTE EEI TT, which was not a word in any language she could pull from the station library. She tried the old Compact maritime set, three commercial telemetry framings and a five-bit teleprinter code from a museum file. Then she wrote the raw marks out in a long ribbon across sheet four and looked at them the way her father had looked at a trim table that refused to close. She was not asking what it said. She was asking what kind of thing it was.

It was a person, keying slowly on a band that had been dead for four years, at a power so low that her receiver could barely pick it out of the hiss. It was either far away or deliberately whispering. On this station she could not decide which was more alarming.

She wrote *Who* again at the top of sheet five, because the first *Who* had been on sheet one and she wanted it in front of her.

---

At 1940 on her fourth evening, the pulses stopped.

She noticed the way you notice a ventilation fan cutting out: as an absence before it became a fact. She had been reconciling the depot's intake flow meters against the cradle-loader cycles, real Ibis work for which someone was actually paying, and the receiver's soft audio chirp had become part of the room's texture, like the tick of the heat exchanger and the grit grinding in the door seal whenever the pressure shifted. Then the room was quieter, and she looked up.

She waited an hour. She ran the gain to the point where the hiss became a roar and the roar became a headache. Nothing came through.

Equipment failure, she wrote, with a question mark. Then, because the sender had seemed tired, she added a second line: *sleep?* and a third: *caught?*

The third one kept her up. If someone was keying on a retired dosimetry band from inside Asgard, the obvious way for that to end was for someone else to walk in on them. She thought of Saar's smile, the one that took inventory, and of his voice saying *any talk you find worrying*, and she found she did not want the pulses to have ended that way. She noted the wanting and did not log it.

At 0150 on her sixth day, thirty hours and ten minutes later, the receiver chirped.

Short, long, short, short. Pause. Short.

It was the same opening as the first night, she was almost certain. Then came a run of new marks, faster than before, as though the sender had a backlog. She sat in the dark in her sleep shirt with a stylus in her teeth, writing as fast as her father's notation allowed, and felt something she had not felt since her first year with Special Losses, when she had found the Vesta shipping fraud by noticing that a refrigerated container was the wrong temperature on the outside. It was an embarrassing, uncomplicated delight. She had a pattern. She did not yet know the shape of the thing, but there was a thing.

Thirty hours of silence was not a fault. Faults did not end on a round number and then resume with a stammer. Thirty hours was a decision.

---

The administrator's office occupied the top of Ring A, under three meters of regolith instead of the usual five, which was the station's way of saying *rank*. Its light-pipes were wider and brought down more of the sky. When Piq arrived at 1000 on day five, answering a message that had cheerfully asked for *a few minutes of your notarial time*, the sky was a flat black with the sun hanging in it a little lower than yesterday. It cast long, hard shadows from the depot's black sunshade across the frost of the basin floor.

She had not known she had notarial time.

"Oh, you're on the register," said the aide, a small brisk woman named Odette Prew. She wore her hair in a knot skewered with two styluses and had the energy of someone who had not been interrupted by a real emergency in years and looked forward to one. "Ibis Metrology, notary public, general and commercial, JDA-recognized. It came through with your credential packet. You didn't know?"

Piq thought back to the credential course on Luna. She had sat a notarial module in the second week, two afternoons on attestation, witnessing and chain of custody, because it was bundled with the metrology certificate and cheaper than opting out. She had passed it without paying attention, the way you pass the safety brief on a shuttle you intend never to crash.

"I knew I'd sat the module," she said.

"Well, it took." Prew turned a slate toward her. On it was the Ibis register entry, with Piq's face looking out of it at a sensible angle. "And it makes you the only notary within six hundred thousand kilometers. The last one was a Compact clerk on Ganymede, and he retired back to Earth in the spring. He's about four months out by now, I suppose, eating something with a real crust."

"There's a notary at Ur Station, surely."

"Martian civil," Prew said, with the air of a woman defusing an objection she had defused many times before. "Ur's notaries can certify under Martian cooperative code, which the JDA doesn't recognize for property, inheritance or" (she paused for effect) "marriage. And ours can't certify under theirs. You, however, are Ibis. Ibis is commercial and neutral. You're recognized by both."

Piq felt the floor of the conversation shift under her the way the shuttle's deck had shifted during the last burn.

"Marriage," she said.

"Cross-moon!" Prew produced a second slate as though she had been holding it behind her back for days, which, Piq realized, she might have been. "Dov Mbeki, tug pilot, ours. Ziva Kalnins, tanker pilot, theirs, out of Ur on a propellant hauler. They've been trying to marry for nineteen months. The jurisdictional problem has been, well." She made a small gesture that took in the entire political arrangement of the outer solar system. "And now there's you."

"I'm here to audit a water depot."

"And you can audit it on all the other days." Prew smiled. "It's a live link. Three-second lag at the present geometry, depending on where her tanker is. You'd just need to witness, certify, seal and say the words. There's a form for the words."

The office's inner door opened before Piq could find a way to refuse that would not sound like a woman with something to hide. Refusing would make her the auditor who had come to the most isolated civil jurisdiction in human space and declined to marry two pilots who had waited nineteen months. People would talk about it in the canteens. Saar would hear them.

"Ms. Varga." Director Ilse Rook came out with her hand already extended, at the correct height. She was tall and spare, perhaps fifty-five. Her grey-blond hair was cut very short, the way long-tenure residents wore it so it stayed out of suit seals, and her face had been arranged into warmth with what Piq judged to be real effort. "I've been wanting to thank you. The spot-check. Twenty households in an afternoon, and nobody's lodged a complaint. Do you know how unusual that is?"

"The accordion was a close call."

Rook laughed, a beat too late, then on time. "The accordion. Yes. I heard it played. It was lovely." Her eyes went past Piq's shoulder to the wall display behind Prew's desk. It showed the depot schedule: intake windows, cradle loading slots and a column of figures for standing inventory that Piq could have recited with her eyes shut, because she had spent four nights failing to reconcile it. Rook looked at it the way people look at a door they have heard a noise behind. Then she looked back. "Odette's asked you about the wedding?"

"She has."

"Good. It would mean a great deal to the station. It's been a hard year." She said *hard year* the way people on Asgard said everything about money, low and to the side, as if Lagrange might be listening through the vents. In a sense it was. "The repricing has everyone very careful. Something hopeful would be welcome. And a wedding with Ur, of all places, shows our neighbors we're still neighbors. You understand."

Piq understood that she was being asked to perform a small act of diplomacy for an administrator who signed station messages with the same office code that had restated the depot inventory flat six quarters running. She understood that Rook had looked at that inventory just now as if it were capable of looking back. She bit the inside of her right cheek, felt herself doing it, and stopped.

"I'd be glad to," she said.

"Wonderful. Wonderful." Rook's warmth briefly stiffened into something like relief, then settled back. "Odette will send you the packet. And, Ms. Varga, if you need anything for the depot audit, any access at all, come to me directly. Not through Facilities." She hesitated, and the hesitation weighed more than the sentence had. "Facilities is very stretched."

She went back through the inner door. Prew pushed the packet across, beaming.

"Appendix F is the seal's statutory powers," Prew said, in the voice of someone reading a sign aloud because the sign required it. "Attestation of disputed mass. Certification of livestock weight for transit, which we don't have, and of hydroponic yield for tithe, which we do. Witnessing of bequests under one tonne. Custody of unclaimed effects. Impound of contested assets. Annual sign-off on the canteen spoon inventory."

"The spoon inventory."

"Eleven years ago someone declared three hundred spoons on intake and there were two hundred and ninety-one in the drawers. It went to arbitration." Prew's expression did not change. "Now a notary counts them every year. It's in the statute. You'll be doing it in the spring, if you're still here. I have to make sure you've read Appendix F."

"I've read it," Piq lied, and signed that she had.

---

The wedding packet ran to forty-one pages, and two of them were not paper.

The first was a voice memo from the groom. It played as soon as she opened it in her quarters and did not stop for six minutes and twelve seconds.

"Hi! Hello. Ms. Varga, auditor-notary, very official, this is Dov. Lucky, everybody says Lucky, you'll say Lucky, I'm telling you now to save time, ha. Odette said you said yes. I don't know if you said yes yet, actually, she might have been planning ahead, she does that, but if you said yes, thank you, and if you didn't say yes, please say yes. Nineteen months. Nineteen. Do you know how many rescheduled windows that is? Eleven. I counted. I'm superstitious about counting but I counted anyway, and I knocked on the console three times after, so it's fine. So: she's on the Margin, the *Steadfast Margin*, Ur tanker, she's the best pilot in the system, don't tell her I said that, she'll say it's inaccurate, she says everything's inaccurate, it's the romance of it. The lag is three seconds, maybe two-nine, maybe three-one, depending, and we've practiced, sort of, we practiced saying hello and it went wrong, but in a nice way—"

Piq pressed pause, wrote *Mbeki: talks* on the pad, wrote *Mbeki: knocks x3* under it, and pressed play again to get the rest of the facts out. The facts amounted to a date eleven days away, a request for a reading of a Latvian poem the bride's grandmother had liked, a request for the officiant to say *by the power vested in me* even if no power was vested in that exact form of words "because it's the best part," and the information that the groom had been born in a Ceres cylinder, had flown tugs for Hesper Okafor-Lund's training school since the second class, and was "honestly the luckiest person in the Jupiter system, apart from the obvious, haha, the obvious being—" Piq had to listen to the obvious being described for some time.

The second was a text attachment from the bride. It said, in full:

*Officiant. I accept the terms. Please do not let him write the vows. —Z. Kalnins, Master, SM-114*

Piq read it three times. She found that she liked Ziva Kalnins enormously and also could not say a single thing about her, which she supposed was the point.

She logged it all under *Cover: duties, unrequested*, and under that, because her father had raised her to see that every entry had a second side, she wrote: *Hesper's school, second class*. It was a thread, if not much of one. The depot's chief engineer trained the pilots, and one of those pilots had a reason to spend time talking to Ur.

---

The sun set on the evening of her sixth day. It did not do it quickly. On Callisto, nothing involving the sun was quick.

She watched part of it through the light-pipe port in her quarters. The disk was small and white, a fifth the width she had grown up with in the cycler's sun-side windows. It sat on the basin's western rim for what the station clock insisted was most of an hour. The depot's sunshade threw a shadow across the frost plain that went on forever, a long black wedge pointing east. The frost on the sunward face of the depot caught the last light and glittered where no frost should be. She noted it again in the column where it already was, the one headed *unexplained*, and did not move it.

Then the sun was gone, and Jupiter was the only light. It hung where it always hung, low over the western rim, a vast pale banded disk. It did not rise or set. It did not move. It simply was, a lamp left on in a room you had not been in for a long time. In its light the frost went the color of weak tea. The Sling's long trench vanished north into a darkness that, the station calendar informed her, would last eight days.

She thought about pulses being sent at night, by people who did not sleep on a schedule because there was no sky to schedule it by.

---

On the morning of day seven she went to the blue door for her weekly read.

It was not mandatory to go in person. The badge pinged the mesh every ninety seconds and the dose warden's office pulled the integrated totals automatically.
