But Piq had kept her own parallel log since intake, because she kept parallel logs of everything, and the station handbook said discrepancies between self-logs and badge totals should be reconciled at the dose office "in a spirit of cooperation." She wanted to see what Teo's spirit of cooperation looked like.

It looked like Teo Anand sitting at a terminal with her sleeves rolled to the elbow. Her dark hair was tied back with what appeared to be a length of cable insulation, and the old analog dosimeter hung on its cord at her throat, scuffed brass with a cracked face. Piq had noticed it on intake day and put it in the column. Teo did not look up when the door slid open. The borated walls swallowed the corridor noise like a hand over a mouth.

"Varga," Teo said to the screen.

"Warden."

"You submitted a self-log."

"The handbook encourages it."

"The handbook encourages a lot of things." Teo finally turned. Her eyes were very dark and very steady, with the unhurried fatigue of someone who had stayed awake reading other people's totals. "Mostly people use it to argue with me. Are you here to argue with me?"

"I'm here to reconcile."

Something moved at the corner of Teo's mouth and was put back. "Sit."

Piq sat. The chair was the same one as on intake day, and so was the faint ozone smell, from the oxygen plant two levels down, or from the electronics, or from Teo herself, who seemed to Piq the kind of person who would smell faintly of electrolysis if she stood still long enough. Teo pulled up two columns side by side: Piq's badge on the left and Piq's self-log on the right.

"Your badge says you're at ninety-five point three microsieverts since intake," Teo said. "Over six days and, let's call it, nineteen hours."

"My log says seventy."

"Your log says seventy," Teo agreed. "Your log also says that your background in quarters is ten microsieverts a day, flat, every day, which is the handbook figure. Your surface time on the Sling tour was forty minutes, which you've logged at eight. And at the end of each day you round your running total down to the nearest hundredth of a millisievert and carry the rounded figure forward."

"It's a self-log. It's indicative."

"It's wrong." Teo touched the screen and Piq's own figures redistributed themselves. "The handbook figure assumes five meters of regolith overhead. Your quarters are on Ring C, two levels down, outboard side. You have three point eight. Your background isn't ten a day. It's twelve point four. Your forty minutes on the surface weren't forty. The north lock cycled you out at 1258 and back in at 1347, so it was forty-nine. And you spent at least four of those minutes on the east side of the depot, where the reflected flux off the frost is about twice what the open plain gives you. That's eleven, not eight." She looked up. "I assume you were looking at the frost."

Piq kept her face still. "It's wrong."

"The frost? People mention it. Facilities says it's vent plume." Teo's voice did not change, but her attention sharpened by a degree. "Your rounding is also wrong. You round down. Everybody rounds down. Two or three microsieverts a day doesn't feel like anything. Over four months it's close to three hundred, which is fifteen percent of your two-millisievert stay allocation. That's fifteen percent you'd think you had and wouldn't."

"So I'll stop rounding."

"So you'll stop rounding," Teo said. "Which is a very strange thing for me to have to tell an auditor."

It landed. Piq felt it land, in a place she would not have predicted. It was not her pride, exactly. It was nearer the place where her father's voice lived. *Read the number, then look at the thing.* She had read her own number and not looked at the thing, and here was a woman who did nothing but look at things, telling her so.

"In my defense," Piq said, "I didn't expect anyone to read it."

"I read everything." Teo turned back to the screen and made two small corrections in Piq's self-log, in a different color, without asking. "That's the job. Everyone thinks it's the badge. It's not the badge. The badge counts. I read."

"That's my job too."

"Then you should be better at it." The corner of her mouth moved again. This time Teo let it stay a fraction of a second longer before she put it away. "Ninety-five point three. Carry that. Not the rounded one."

Piq stood. At the door she said, because some instinct she did not trust wanted to leave the room with the last entry, "The dosimeter on your cord. That's not station issue."

Teo's hand did not go to it. Piq had seen enough people protect a thing to recognize when someone was deliberately not protecting it.

"No," Teo said. "It's not."

The door slid shut between them. In the corridor Piq walked eleven of her long low-g strides before she realized she had brought her own pad up and written *95.3 µSv* in the dose column, with no rounding, and under it, in the right-hand margin, a question mark beside nothing at all.

---

The second silence began at 0742 on day nine.

She had expected it, and that was the strange part. She had done the arithmetic at the end of the first gap: if thirty hours of silence was a decision, then it could be a recurring decision, and the place to look for its recurrence was a station rhythm. Asgard had several. Its calendar ran seven-day weeks for payroll, 16.7-day local days for surface operations, and twelve-hour shifts for nearly everything. None of those made a thirty-hour gap. A thirty-hour gap was two and a half shifts, which was nobody's off-duty.

Then she found the depot.

The depot's ledger, to which Ibis had given her read-only access, contained staffing tables because staffing was a cost and every cost touched mass somewhere. The Sling and depot crews did not work the station's seven-day pattern. They worked what the tables called a nine-shift cycle: nine twelve-hour shifts of rotation through the cradle, the coils, the intake and the pumps, which came to 108 hours. Four and a half days.

She had sat back in the fold-down chair, which creaked in that unhurried low-g way, as if surprised to be leaned on, and looked at the figure for a long time.

At 0742 on day nine, four and a half days and a few minutes after the first silence had begun, the receiver's chirp stopped mid-pattern. It cut off after short-long-short. The stammer never arrived.

She was ready. She had both tables open, the depot's nine-shift roster and her own pulse log, and she began laying one over the other with the care of someone fitting a stencil to a wall.

The roster had forty-one names. She filtered by role first, then by duty pattern, looking for anyone whose off-rotation matched the gaps. The first gap had run from 1940 on day four to 0150 on day six. The second was running now. Nobody matched either gap perfectly, because nobody's rest window was thirty hours. The nine-shift cycle allowed two consecutive off-shifts, twenty-four hours at most, with a sleep shift tacked on either side if you traded. But four names had a rest block that covered most of the first gap, and the system logged them as trading the edges.

One of the four was H. Okafor-Lund, Chief Engineer.

Hesper had been off-roster for twenty-six of the first thirty silent hours. For the other four she was logged as "on call, residence," which was a polite way of saying she had been reachable and doing nothing official.

The second gap was messier. Hesper's roster showed her on duty for its first eight hours, logged for a coil inspection at the north end of the trench, thirty-eight kilometers from the cradle. Then she was off.

Piq made herself write the inconvenient half first: *H. on duty, start of gap 2*. Then she wrote the half she wanted: *Coil inspection, north end, 38 km. Unreachable. Eight hours alone on the trench.* And then, under it: *Being somewhere no one can check is the same as being off.*

Of the other three names on the first gap, one had flown out on the *Paper Lantern* on day four and could not be keying anything from Asgard. One was a pump technician with a medical exemption. One was a twenty-three-year-old cradle loader whose rest block covered the first gap entirely and the second gap not at all.

Hesper had trained four classes of pilots. Lucky Mbeki was one of them, and Lucky was about to marry an Ur tanker pilot. Hesper had paused when she said *the best I've had*. Hesper owned the Sling, and the Sling was the thing you would use to throw anything before conjunction where no one would see it until it was done. When Piq had asked about the frost on the depot's sunward face, Hesper had answered in a single breath: intake-heater plume drifting off the vents and refreezing on the shade struts, happened every local day, nothing to log. It was an answer quick enough to have been either practiced or true. Piq had left the frost in the unexplained column anyway, because plume did not usually choose the sunlit side to freeze on. But either way the answer meant that Hesper knew the depot better than anyone, and that if 340 tonnes of ice were going somewhere, she was the person who would know where.

Piq looked at her tally pad. She felt the rare, physical pleasure of a ledger approaching balance, the sense of figures leaning toward each other across the page, wanting to meet.

*The equals sign is where you stop looking.*

She heard it in her father's voice and did not stop.

---

The trouble with following anyone on Asgard was the badge.

She had turned the problem over for a week, the way you turn a coin that won't land. The badge pinged every ninety seconds. Saar could read the pings, or could have them read, which amounted to the same thing. Leaving it on its hook gave her an alibi of being in bed and a flat line of quarters background with no corridor time in it, which Teo would catch within a day and question within two. She had just been shown, with some precision, exactly how closely Teo read. Shielding it made a badge that went dark in the middle of a corridor. That was worse than a badge that logged where she went.

So she let it log where she went. She simply made sure it had a reason to.

On day ten she filed a work order with Facilities under her Ibis contract: *Flow meter verification, depot feed lines, service levels 3 through 5, ring-wide.* It was real work. The meters were on her schedule, and three of them were overdue for calibration under Ibis rules. She set the window at 1700 to 2300, which covered the evening shift change, and the scope as ring-wide, which covered nearly the whole underside of the station. Anywhere the badge put her in those six hours, she would have a meter to point to.

Facilities approved it in four minutes, with an automated stamp and a note from a human that read *ty, they've been overdue since spring*. Piq felt a small, unhelpful pang of guilt at being thanked for her cover.

At 1748 she was at the meter on feed line D-7, one level below the Sling crew's changing rooms, with a calibration rig plugged in and genuinely running. She watched the corridor junction ahead through a curve of polished pipe lagging that made a serviceable mirror. The depot crew came down from the changing rooms at shift change in loose twos and threes. They walked with the lope of people who had lived a long time in an eighth of a gravity: knees soft, each stride a low flat arc, never letting the feet leave the floor more than they had to.

Hesper came at 1804, alone, with a thermos clipped to her belt and her broad shoulders slightly rounded, like a woman carrying a weight that was not on any scale. She did not turn toward the residence rings. She turned down.

Piq let the rig finish its cycle, because the meter would know if she didn't, logged the reading, and followed.

Following in 0.126 g was not like following on a cycler's spin deck, where she had learned the trade, and not like following under the full Earth weight of the training rooms at L4. The rules here were made by momentum. You could not stop quickly. Every stride committed you to a distance, and if the person ahead turned a corner you could not check yourself without catching a handrail and making a noise. So you did not follow by sight, which needed you to be close. You followed by count.

She had walked the service tunnels twice already on genuine meter runs and had their geometry in her head the way she had once held the cycler's trim tables. She knew the stride length of a tall woman loping in low g: about one point nine meters for Hesper, she judged, from watching her walk the Sling. She knew the rhythm of the soft footfalls carried through the deck plating: the low thud of a heel and the scuff of the follow-through, almost a second between each. Piq dropped back to the edge of hearing and counted. Twelve strides. A pause, a junction. A change in the acoustics, a narrower tube. Twenty more strides. A hatch cycling; she heard it through the plating, the particular groan of an old seal with grit in it. She reached it nine strides later, read the number stenciled beside it, and followed the map in her head down.

Level five. Then level six, which was not on her work order, because level six was not supposed to have any feed lines on it.

At the hatch to six she stopped and looked at her badge. It was pinging normally, and the mesh nodes were still lit along the ceiling. She had a choice to make, and she made it with the speed of someone who had already made it before leaving her quarters. She would go on. If the badge logged her on level six, she would say she had traced an unmapped branch of the D-line, which was exactly the sort of thing an auditor with a work order might do. It was even, faintly, possibly true.

Level six was old. The walls were raw sintered regolith rather than panelled. The light came from construction-era strips set every five meters, half of them dead. The air was colder, and it had a smell Piq had not met on Asgard before: not the ozone of electrolysis or the dry mineral breath of the regolith, but something wet. She thought of ice, of a freezer opened.

The mesh nodes ran out thirty meters past the hatch. She watched the last one go by overhead, dark, its housing cracked. Her badge kept pinging, but now it pinged into nothing. Somewhere in a security log her trace was ending at the edge of a mapped zone. She wrote it on the pad without slowing: *Badge goes dark past L6 junction. Explain later.* Then she underlined *later* twice, because she had no idea how she would.

Hesper's footfalls had stopped.

Piq stopped too, which took four meters and a hand on the rough wall that left grit under her nails. She listened. There was a sound ahead, not footsteps. It was rhythmic and high, a grinding hiss that rose and fell. Under it ran a lower thump, like something heavy being set down slowly. And under that she heard voices, a few, quiet, the way people talk when they have been working a long time on something they are not supposed to be doing.

She went forward at a crawl, one hand on the wall, letting each step settle before taking the next. She had learned that it was almost impossible to walk silently in low g. It was very easy to walk slowly.

The tunnel opened onto a gallery that overlooked a hangar.

It had once been a construction hangar from the station's first decade, a vault cut deep into the basin floor for assembling the Sling's tugs out of the weather and the light. She could tell from the scale: thirty meters across, perhaps twenty high, a great curved roof of sintered regolith ribbed with old steel. She could tell from the rust-brown JDA decommission notice still bolted to the gallery rail, SEALED PENDING SALVAGE — NO ENTRY — AUTH. FACILITIES. Below it someone had chalked *ha*.

The hangar was not sealed.

There were eleven people on the floor. Hesper was among them, her thermos set down on a crate, her palm resting flat against something in the way Piq had seen her rest it on the Sling's coil housings, listening. Overhead lights had been run from a portable power cell and hung on cables from the roof ribs, so the space was lit in pools and shadows like a theater in rehearsal.

In the middle of it sat a tug.

It was old: a blunt, workmanlike hull with a stubby nose, reaction-control clusters bolted on like afterthoughts, and a drive bell scarred dark at the rim. Its registration plate had been removed and the bolt holes painted over in a slightly different grey. The hull had once carried a name in block letters, and those had been sanded off too. Someone had put a new one on the nose cone in fat white marker, in the round confident hand of a person who believed in signs:

MULE.

And they were wrapping it in ice.

The grinding hiss came from the far corner, where two people worked a wire saw through a block the size of a footlocker. The block was white and cloudy and steaming faintly in the lights. The thump came from the hull itself, where four more people with a manual hoist and a lot of patience were easing a sawn block into a frame of aluminum strapping bolted around the crew section. Only the crew section: the drive and the tanks were bare. They had done perhaps two-thirds of it. The blocks were stacked edge to edge in a rough armored shell. Where the blocks met, someone had filled the seams with slush that froze as Piq watched into a dull, pebbled grey.

Piq did what she had been raised to do. She counted.

Blocks on the hull: twenty-two that she could see, with more hidden on the far side. Call it thirty. They were roughly a meter by a meter by half a meter. At the density of pressed depot ice that came to about four hundred and sixty kilograms a block. Thirty blocks made fourteen tonnes on the hull. Finished, the shell might run to twenty. That was a radiation jacket for a small crew cabin, heavy and serious, and very nearly the number she would have written herself if someone had asked her what a tug wanted for shielding on a dirty run.

Against the near wall stood the rest, and the rest did not fit. There were ninety-odd more blocks, uncut, wrapped in thermal blankets that she was fairly sure had come from the same station stores as the two in Ballast's nest. Beside them sat a row of squat bladder tanks with depot fittings, and a melt rig she could not see the far end of. Forty tonnes, perhaps sixty, if the tanks were full. She had no way to tell from the gallery whether it was more armor waiting its turn, feedstock to be melted down for the drive, or something she did not yet have a column for.

She stood very still at the gallery rail with her badge pinging into a dead mesh and her heart doing something she did not log, and she understood two things at once, the way a scale settles.

The first was that she had found where the ice was going.

The second was that she had found where *some* of it was going. Shell and stack and tanks together came nowhere near three hundred and forty tonnes. And no tug needed three hundred and forty tonnes of anything. No tug could fly with it. The ledger that had been leaning so pleasingly toward balance in her quarters, Hesper, the roster, the gaps, the Sling, now tipped hard to one side. Below her, the woman she had built it around lifted her palm from the hull, said something too quiet to hear, and the saw crew laughed. It was not a conspirator's laugh. It sounded like people who had been working hard and believed they were working for something.

Piq did not know what a hand-shielded, unregistered tug in a sealed hangar was for, or where it meant to go. She did not know how its armor could be an answer to *throw before conjunction*, or how either one could be an answer to a whisper on a dead band that went silent for thirty hours every four and a half days.

She did know that she now had twenty-eight days.

Below her, on the hull of the *Mule*, a man she could not quite see reached up and knocked three times on the nose cone, *tap, tap, tap*, and said something to the ice, too low to hear.

## Chapter 4: Mule

It was Day 10, and she gave the hangar eleven more minutes. Eleven was the number left in the calibration window she had built for herself, once she subtracted the walk back to a live mesh node and a margin for being stopped and asked friendly questions.

The man who had knocked on the nose cone stayed close to the ice for a while, one hand on it, the way you'd stand beside a sleeping animal you weren't sure of. Then someone below called "Lucky, you're blocking the hoist," and he answered "I'm *blessing* the hoist," and moved off. Piq wrote **LUCKY** on her pad and put a box around it. A name was a fact. The voice she had already filed. She had heard six minutes and twelve seconds of it in Rook's wedding packet, a man who apparently breathed through his ears so he never had to stop talking. Here was the groom, telling two-thirds of a tonne of hand-cut ice that it was doing a good job.

She counted the people on the floor again and got eleven both times. She counted the blocks on the frame and got twenty-two, and she counted the empty bays in the strapping and got eight. The frame blocks were big, a long stride on the long edge and nearly a meter deep. She put them at a little under two-thirds of a tonne apiece, which made the frame about fourteen tonnes. She ran a slow tally of the stockpile under the thermal blankets along the near wall and made it ninety-four blocks, though two might be one block split. Those were not the same blocks. They had the same face but were cut thinner, perhaps three-quarters the depth, a shade under half a tonne each. Two sizes meant two jobs, and only one of the jobs was on the frame. The melt rig hummed. A long gray seam of slush, poured between two blocks, set as she watched, the way grease sets in a cold pan.

At 1911 she left the way she had come. On the gallery steps she kept her feet low and flat, because a stride that cleared the floor in this gravity carried you a meter and a half, and a meter and a half in the dark was how you met a handrail with your teeth. The L6 hatch seal ground grit when she closed it behind her. Thirty meters on, the first live mesh node blinked green on its bracket, and her badge reacquired with a small electronic hiccup she felt more than heard.

At meter D-7 she finished the paperwork on the calibration she had actually done. The meter really had been drifting, two-tenths of a percent high on the feed line, which was the kind of fact that made a cover sound true. Then she opened an addendum to the work order and typed it the way she would if it were real. Part of the reason she wanted it in writing was that it was half real.

*Traced unmapped branch of D-line feed downward from L5 junction. Branch continues to construction-era level 6. Mesh coverage ends approx. 30 m past L6 hatch; final node dark, housing cracked. Recommend Facilities node repair and as-built drawing update. Branch not metered. Further survey pending.*

She read it twice. Every sentence in it was true. She had followed a pipe, and Hesper had been walking beside the pipe at the time. A security officer reading the badge log would find a gap, and right under the gap he would find an auditor complaining about the gap in the dry, put-upon voice of a woman who wanted the drawings fixed. Her father had taught her this without knowing he was teaching it: the best place to hide a discrepancy is inside a report about a discrepancy.

She filed it at 1958. The Facilities queue acknowledged it in under a second and assigned it a ticket number with seven digits. Piq thought of Hesper on the Sling tour saying *they lose all my tickets on the shade*, and felt a small, unwanted pity for the ticket.

---

In her quarters she ate standing up. In six cubic meters, sitting down was a decision. The light-pipe showed Jupiter in its usual place over the western rim, banded and enormous and not moving, because nothing on this side of Callisto ever saw it move. The night was four days old, and the frost outside had gone the color of weak tea.

The receiver was running. She had left it logging while she worked. The pulses had come back at 1352 that afternoon, thirty hours and ten minutes after the second gap began at 0742 on Day 9, opening with the same pattern as before: short, long, short, short, pause, short. That made two gaps of thirty hours and ten minutes each, a hundred and eight hours apart. Two was not yet a pattern, but she wrote the third anyway, because a prediction you don't write down is a prediction you'll lie about later.

**Gap 3 (pred.): begins ~1942 Day 13.**

Then she pulled up the Sling crew roster and found Hesper's line for Day 13. *Coil inspection, north end, 1600–2400. Single crew.* Thirty-eight kilometers away, on her own, where no one could check.

Piq looked at that for a while. It sat a little too neatly in her ledger, like a crate that weighs exactly what the manifest says, and she didn't like the feeling. She ran it once more and it came out the same. She drew a line under it.

Under the line she wrote **MULE** and listed what she knew:

*Tug, ~1st-gen ore-hauler hull, reg. plate removed. Crew section only being wrapped: 22 blocks (~0.64 t), ~14 t now; 30 bays, ~19–20 t finished. Stockpile ~94 blocks, thinner cut (~0.46 t), ~43 t, plus bladders (depot fittings). Melt rig. Eleven crew. Hesper. Lucky (groom).*

Then the arithmetic. Fourteen tonnes in the frame, forty-three on the floor, a few more in the bladders. Call it sixty-five at the outside. Three hundred and forty were missing from the depot. That left about two hundred and seventy-five tonnes that she had not seen, which was a far larger quantity than the one she had.

And the eight empty bays would take five tonnes of the big blocks, not forty-three of the small ones. The stockpile was eight times what the frame still needed, and cut to a size the frame didn't use. She wrote *thin blocks: for what?* in the unexplained column and left it there.

Twenty tonnes of ice wrapped around a crew compartment was not ballast or trim, and it wasn't decoration. It was a shield. Clause 14(c), which she had recited to the *Paper Lantern*'s crew word for word, set minimum shielding water for Europa-bound vessels scaled by transit time. Twenty tonnes was a long transit or a hot one.

She wrote **Europa?** under the thin blocks. Saar's voice came back to her unprompted from the corridor on her second day: *If you hear Europa talk that worries you.* She had not liked that he was the kind of man who would ask. She liked even less that she now had some.

---

The public traffic board on the Ring A concourse was the closest thing Asgard had to a town square. Through the morning of Day 11, people stopped in front of it with their tea the way people on old Earth once stood in front of weather reports, wanting to be told that the sky was behaving.

It was a wall of slow light. Callisto sat at the center as a gray disc. Jupiter was a banded smear at the edge of the display, its scale fudged so it would fit. Ganymede was further in, with the Ur beacon ringed in amber, because amber in JDA iconography meant *Martian, technically allowed*. Small green tick marks were JDA-registered traffic: survey tugs, depot shuttles, the *Paper Lantern* six days out and crawling. The Sling's next scheduled throw was a dotted line wandering out toward the inner system, with a launch time nine days away printed beside it in a font that made it look important.

One mark was amber and alone, on a long looping ellipse that ran between Ganymede and Callisto and then bent back again.

Piq tapped it. The board offered a JDA traffic advisory, number 2214-188. She read it the way she read any document with a signature block, last line first.

*Issued by authority of the Office of Security, Asgard Yard. M. Saar.*

Then the body:

*Ur-registered propellant tanker STEADFAST MARGIN (SM-114) continues in a Ganymede-Callisto transfer orbit for which no destination filing has been received. The vessel has now completed three periapsis passages without circularization or declared purpose. The Office regards this as a provocative posture inconsistent with routine propellant operations. All JDA-registered traffic is advised to maintain separation of no less than 2,000 km. Further advisories will follow as appropriate.*

SM-114. *Master, SM-114.* She did not have to check, because she remembered Ziva Kalnins's entire contribution to the wedding packet: *Officiant. I accept the terms. Please do not let him write the vows.*

So the bride was the provocation.

Piq stood very still in front of the board while a maintenance tech with a bulb of tea leaned past her, read the advisory, and said to nobody, "*Posture*," as if the word had a smell. He moved on. Piq stayed where she was.

A transfer orbit between the two moons was a road you could leave cheaply at either end. A tanker coasting on it was parked. It was sitting in the one place in the system where it could reach Callisto's neighborhood for very little fuel, and it had been sitting there long enough for Saar to write *posture*.

She worked it through. A Sling throw was twelve tonnes of shelled ice at six point one kilometers per second, nominally sent on a multi-year trajectory toward a customer in the inner system. An Ur tanker in the right place could match a slug's path and take it aboard.

She stopped there, because that was the sentence a briefing would write, and she ran the number instead. Six point one at the muzzle was well over Callisto's escape speed. Take the moon's pull out of it and the slug still left at better than five and a half kilometers per second. A tanker idling at the Callisto end of its ellipse might be moving a kilometer or two relative to the moon. Matching the slug would cost four kilometers per second, likely more, and on a loaded tanker that was most of a tank. Nobody stole twelve tonnes of ice by burning a hundred.

Unless the slug never left at six point one. The Sling was a coil, and a coil could be turned down. Throw at two and a half, a little over escape, aimed to fall onto the tanker's ellipse at nearly its speed, and the tanker could meet it with a few hundred meters per second, a burn too small to file. Log the throw at nominal. The depot would book it as thrown, Lagrange would insure it as in transit, and it would arrive nowhere at all. *Throw before conjunction, no one will see it until it's done.* During conjunction the relay at Mars went dark for nineteen days. Nobody in the inner system would be watching the books, and nobody would see a slug fail to appear until long after the tanker had gone home to Ganymede with it.

That made it fraud. Pell had named two fears, and this one fit the facts with only one joint showing. The cradle crew would have to falsify the throw log and the coil power draw, and the pilot school had trained everyone involved. But Asgard's own tracking would watch a slug leave at less than half speed on the wrong heading, so somebody in the tracking office would have to be looking the other way. That was one more conspirator than she had any evidence for. The groom was marrying the tanker.

It fit, and she disliked how well it fit. She found she was biting her right cheek, and stopped.

It didn't explain the Mule or the twenty tonnes of shielding. You didn't need an armored tug to steal ice. You needed a bribed cradle loader and a quiet night. She made herself write both halves on the pad and leave the line between them open.

*SM-114 loitering G-C transfer, 3 periapses, unfiled. Ziva = master. Fits: short throw (~2.5 km/s, logged 6.1) onto SM-114 ellipse, cheap match, fraud. Weak: local tracking would see it, so who's in tracking? Doesn't fit: MULE shielding. Why shield a crew to steal ice?*

On the way out of the concourse she passed a bulletin screen showing the wedding date, Day 16, under the heading **COMMUNITY**, with a cartoon of two rings joined by a dotted line meant to suggest a link from one moon to another. Somebody had drawn a tiny tanker on the line in stylus.

---

The Facilities as-builts for Level 6 were thirty years old and had been scanned from paper. That gave every line a soft, furred edge, like a drawing that had been rained on. Piq had pulled them under the flow-meter work order, which was the one task she could justify, and she read them in a corner of the depot control annex while the shift around her argued about a pump schedule.

The construction hangar showed up on the sheet as a rectangle with a cross through it and the annotation **TEMP**. Like everything on Asgard marked temporary, it had outlived the people who planned it. Along the north wall the drawing showed a cable tray running the length of the roof, between the second and third ribs. In the station's first decade they had strung power through it to the big fabrication lamps. The tray was crawlable: the drawings gave its section as 0.9 by 0.7 meters, with a hatch at the eastern end that opened onto a ventilation plenum serving the levels above. The plenum, in turn, connected to a utility ladder that came out on Level 4, beside hydroponics.

She marked the route on her pad in pencil with distances in strides, then rubbed out the strides and wrote meters. Strides were too optimistic.

At 1830 she logged into the work order again. *Continuing D-line branch survey, L5–L6.* She packed a headlamp, a stylus, her pad, the small camera she used to photograph scale tags, and a protein bar in a sealed foil sleeve, which she had come to think of as standard procedure on Asgard. Then she went back down.

The tray was worse than the drawings. Thirty years of regolith dust had settled in it so finely that it behaved like a liquid. Every time she pulled herself forward with her elbows, it rose in a slow cloud and refused to settle, taking whole minutes to drift back down at an eighth of Earth's gravity. She learned to breathe through her teeth. The old cable was still there, bundled and dead, and she slid along beside it with her shoulders brushing both walls. Every three meters there was a grating, and through each one the hangar floor below was lit gold by the portable lamps hanging from the ribs.

Forty-one meters in, she reached a grating directly above the Mule's crew section and stopped.

From above, the tug looked less like a ship and more like an arm in a cast. The ice frame ran from just behind the stubby nose to the start of the tank section. The blocks were cut square and fitted with a tolerance she would have accepted on a shipping crate. Two new ones had gone in since last night: twenty-four now, the seams still wet, both of them the big cut. Behind the frame the tug was bare metal, old tanks and older plumbing, and the drive bell was scarred dark, as if somebody had once held a blowtorch to it out of spite.

Hesper stood at the frame with her palm flat on a block. The other hand held her thermos. She wasn't doing anything that Piq could see except standing there, and nobody bothered her.

Lucky was underneath the tug on a creeper, which in this gravity he didn't need. Piq gathered he used it because he liked rolling. He had a wrench and an opinion.

"I'm just saying," Lucky said, "for the record, which nobody is keeping, I want it noted."

"Noted," said Hesper.

"Every Europa job I ever flew. Every single one. Lagrange's water blankets cost me three hundred meters per second. Three hundred. You know what I could do with three hundred meters per second, Hes? I could go to *lunch*. I could take a *scenic route*. Instead I carry a swimming pool on my head around Europa so some actuary on L4 can sleep, and I cut every corner on the trajectory, and I come home on fumes with a dose badge like a Christmas tree, and that's the *safe* way."

"It is the safe way," Hesper said.

"I know it's the safe way. I'm not complaining it's the safe way. I'm complaining it's *expensive*." He rolled out from under the tug, sat up in a gentle arc, and pointed the wrench at the ice. "And now look. Twenty tonnes. Voluntarily. With my own hands. With my own hands and Rafe's hands and Mo's hands and everybody's hands, we are strapping a swimming pool onto this poor animal, and nobody is even making us."

"You'll want every centimeter," Hesper said. She said it quietly and without inflection, as though she were reading a gauge, and Lucky stopped talking for nearly two seconds, which Piq suspected was a record.

"Yeah," he said finally. "Yeah, I know."

Piq wrote down *300 m/s* with a careful hand. Twenty tonnes of shielding water on a tug that massed, by the look of the tanks, maybe forty or fifty tonnes fueled. That changed the mass ratio a great deal. The cost came out of the exponent, and an exponent never forgave you. Every kilogram you carried had to be pushed by fuel, and every kilogram of fuel had to be pushed too. Three hundred meters per second was a plausible bill. Lucky was a man who knew to the meter what his rules cost him.

*You'll want every centimeter.* Somebody was going to fly this thing somewhere hot.

"Speaking of hands," Lucky said, rolling back under, "Ziva says the officiant sounds like a mortician."

Piq stopped writing.

"Ziva hasn't heard the officiant," said someone else, a younger voice off to the left. Piq angled her head against the grating and found him: a lanky man with a shaved head, coiling hose. She thought it was the twenty-three-year-old cradle loader from the roster, Rafe Danjuma. His duty photo had made him look about fifteen.

"Ziva has read the officiant's *email*," said Lucky. "Ziva says the email was very correct. Ziva says it was the most correct email she's ever read and it frightened her. Ziva says—"

"Lucky," said Hesper.

"What? I'm only saying she's the auditor. She's the one going round with the scanner. She found the cat, did you hear? Juno in Ring B told Mo she crawled into the duct at Junction 14 and came out with *nothing*, no bar, no dignity, and she didn't log it. Didn't log the cat. Mo says that's either the nicest thing anybody's done on this rock all year, or she's planning something. Ziva says if she's doing the vows, we should find out which before the—"

"*Lucky*."

"—wedding. Before the wedding. I was going to say before the wedding."

Hesper said nothing at all. Below her, the Mule ticked as its old metal settled in the cold.

Piq lay in the dust with the pad against her chest and felt her face go hot in a way she couldn't put a number to. She didn't know which part had done it: being discussed, being called the nicest thing all year, or *planning something* from a man named Mo whom she had never met. She wrote **Mo** and **Juno, Ring B** and did not underline either.

She stayed two hours and seven minutes. In that time two more blocks went into the frame, someone brewed a pot of something that smelled of cardamom and machine oil, and Hesper talked once, briefly, to a woman at the melt rig about *the bladders and the timeline*, too low for Piq to catch any numbers. Nobody touched the thin blocks under the blankets. The dust settled on Piq in a fine coat until her sleeves were the same color as the tray.

At 2041 Rafe Danjuma climbed a ladder to rehang a lamp that was swinging, and looked up.

Piq didn't move. Moving was what got you seen. The lamp was between them, though, and the lamp was the problem. It put a hard gold disc of light straight onto the grating, and the grating had a face above it. Rafe's hand stopped on the lamp hook.

"Hey," he said, not loud, as though he wasn't sure. Then he was sure, and much louder: "*Hey!*"

---

She reversed along the tray by pushing off the cable bundle with both hands, and found out at once that going backward in a dust cloud in low gravity is like swimming through flour. She couldn't see. She counted instead. She had come in forty-one meters, and the drawing put the hatch at the eastern end, so it was forty-one meters back to a hatch she had last seen as a dim square. Shoulders against both walls, elbows working. The dust rose and hung around her.

Below and behind, a ladder clanged, and something heavy landed on a floor too fast and slid.

The hatch was where the drawing said. The plenum beyond it was a vertical shaft with a wind in it. Station air, forced through at speed, smelled of filters and faintly of the ozone that hung over everything in Asgard that had once been water. The utility ladder ran up the wall. She went up it hand over hand, hardly using her feet. In this gravity your arms did the work and your legs came along.

Somebody opened the tray hatch below her at the moment she reached the Level 4 landing.

The landing door read **HYDROPONICS — GALLERY TWO — CLOSE BEHIND YOU — HUMIDITY CONTROLLED**. She opened it, went through, and closed it behind her, because a door left open is a door someone mentions.

Gallery Two was a long barrel vault under the grow-lights, warm and wet after the plenum. Racks of greens ran in tiers on either side to the ceiling, with a central aisle seven meters wide. The air was heavy with nutrient mist and the green, slightly bitter smell of growing leaves. Water whispered through the troughs. A man in a smock was working at the far end with his back to her.

The far end had a door, which was the other point in its favor. It was perhaps sixty meters away.

Piq had grown up on a cycler's spin deck, most of her childhood spent walking its steady counterfeit gravity behind her father and his scales, and she had spent the last two years in the full weight of L4 headquarters. She had been on Callisto for eleven days. Eleven days had taught her that a running stride here was a slow bound. You learned to keep it flat and low and to skim. She had not yet absorbed that frightened legs forget everything they have learned. Behind her, the landing door rattled.

She pushed off.

It was a good push, an L4 push, the push of a woman with full-weight muscle she had kept by habit. It carried her up, then a little further up, and kept going. The aisle floor dropped away and the second tier of lettuce went past at eye level. She had a long, unhurried moment to understand, the way you understand a column that won't add up, that she was going to keep rising for some time yet and that the gallery curved.

Her trajectory topped out a meter below the grow-lights. On the way down she had time to see that she would miss the aisle, to see exactly which rack she would hit instead, and to put her arms up in a gesture that accomplished nothing.

She went into the third tier of the left-hand racks in a slow, spreading crunch, shoulder first. A rack of butterhead lettuce gave way under her like a mattress. Nutrient solution sloshed out of its trough and came down around her in fat, lazy drops. Several heads came loose and floated away in long, sorrowful arcs. She stopped, half in the rack, with a cold wet leaf plastered across her face and her right foot caught on a drip line, and stayed there.

Down the aisle, the landing door opened.

Piq held her breath, which was easy, because she had no idea what had happened to it.

Footsteps came in: Rafe, judging by the stride, long and young and badly managed. He stopped. A voice at the far end, the man in the smock, said with enormous injury, "*What* in the—"

"Sorry," Rafe said. "Did someone come through? Running?"

"*Running*," the gardener said, in the tone of a man repeating a swear word to make sure he'd heard it. "In my gallery?"

There was a pause. Piq could see none of it. She could see one leaf and the edge of a trough, and a single drop of nutrient solution falling past her nose at a rate she could have timed by hand.

"No," said Rafe, slowly. "No, I suppose not." He waited two more seconds and then left by the far door. She counted his footfalls until they were gone.

The gardener came down the aisle at a careful walk and stopped under her rack.

"Good evening," said Piq, from inside the butterhead.

He was in his seventies, she thought, with a seamed brown face, spectacles pushed up into white hair, and the stillness of a man who had finally found the thing he'd suspected all his life. His smock was embroidered **W. ACHTERBERG — AG SEC.** He looked at her, then at the rack, then at the lettuce that was still coming down here and there like slow green rain.

"You're the auditor," he said.

"I am."

"I've seen your picture. On the notice. 'Mass audit in progress, cooperation appreciated.'" He frowned. "Is this the audit?"

"I was," said Piq, freeing her foot from the drip line and descending from the rack in a controlled two-handed lower she was rather proud of, "verifying an unmapped branch of the D-line. I took the plenum ladder, misjudged the step-off from the landing, and I am very sorry about your lettuce."

He considered that. The truthful parts had gone in so neatly beside the false ones that she couldn't find the joins herself.

"Misjudged the step-off," Achterberg repeated.

"I grew up on a cycler. Spin gravity. My legs still think they know what a floor is."

"Mm." He went to the rack, gently removed a ruined head, and held it up the way an engineer holds up a failed part. Then he reached into his smock and took out a small handheld scale. It was old and yellow, with a brass weighing cup, and an Ibis certification tag was wired to the handle. Piq read the tag automatically and saw it was nineteen days overdue for recalibration.

"Incident report," Achterberg said, "requires the loss by mass."

"Of course."

He weighed every damaged head and some of the leaves. He weighed a fragment of the trough lip that had snapped off. He weighed, for reasons she didn't ask about, a single leaf he took from her collar, and wrote the number down with satisfaction. Piq stood beside him, dripping and turning slowly pale green at the edges, and was not allowed to help.

"One point three seven kilos of butterhead," he announced. "Plus point oh four of trough. Plus that leaf, which I'll grant you was already on its way out."

"Generous."

"Thirty-one percent of the rack's tithe-weight for the cycle." He looked at her over his spectacles, which he had at some point lowered. "Which is a *hydroponic yield for tithe* matter. Which is notary business. Appendix F. And you," he said, with a gardener's terrible patience, "are the notary."

Piq opened her mouth to say *conflict of interest*. She saw from his face that he had been waiting eleven years for someone to say *conflict of interest* to him, and shut it.

She notarized the loss. She signed as responsible party in the box marked *Cause*, then signed again, and sealed, in the box marked *Witness and Attesting Officer*. Achterberg read both signatures as if they might be forged and pronounced them in order. Under *Description of Incident* he wrote, in a careful small hand: *Auditor arrived from plenum, above. Recalibrating.*

"Your scale's overdue," Piq said, because she couldn't help it.

"I know," said Achterberg. "Facilities lost the ticket."

She left by the far door at an elderly, flat-footed walk, with a leaf in her hair she didn't know about. Rafe was nowhere in sight. She had no idea whether he had seen her face in the gold light through the grating, or only a shape, or only dust. She didn't know whether he had gone back to the hangar to tell Hesper *it was nothing* or *it was her*.

In the corridor she took out her pad and wrote **Rafe: saw?** in the unexplained column, as far down the page as possible, as though distance from the other entries would help.

---

She went back that same night.

She argued herself into it in her quarters, sponging nutrient solution out of her collar with a towel she'd have to account for, as though she were reviewing someone else's claim. If Rafe had seen her, tomorrow there would be a lock on that floor, a watch kept, the stockpile moved. Whatever she could learn from the Mule she could learn tonight or perhaps never. If Rafe hadn't seen her, nothing had changed except that she'd lost a rack of lettuce and some self-respect, and tonight was as good as any night. Either way, tonight. She noticed that the argument was symmetrical. Arguments like that usually meant the decision had been made earlier, somewhere she wasn't looking, and she let it go.

What she needed was the reactor. Weight told you how big a thing was. Shielding told you how bad it was. A tug wrapped in twenty tonnes of ice for its crew was carrying something hot: a reactor run hard, a long dose, or both. She wanted the shadow shield and the drum housings, any service tags, and the serial on the core plate. If this was a theft machine she wanted to know how far it could go, and if it was something else she wanted to know that too.

At 0230 on Day 12 she went down without a work order. The mesh would log her going down and stop logging her at the cracked node, and she would deal with it.

The drawings showed a personnel door at floor level, off the Level 6 corridor, close to where the gallery steps came down. The Facilities seal tag on it had been cut cleanly and reattached with a piece of yellow tape, and there was nothing on the tape. She hesitated at that, longer than at anything else that night, and couldn't have said why.

Inside, the hangar was dark and cold. The lamps were off and the melt rig was silent. She could hear, faintly, the ice ticking as it gave up the last of the day's heat. Her headlamp made a small moving room of light. She kept it low and walked along the wall, counting meters to the tug's tail.

The Mule's drive section loomed out of the dark like a cliff. Bare tanks, old plumbing, the scorched bell. Forward of the bell sat the reactor housing, a squat drum of metal ringed by six bulging control-drum actuators. Forward of that was the shadow shield, a thick truncated cone of layered tungsten and lithium hydride whose job was to throw a radiation shadow over the crew section so that the people up front lived in its shade.

She came at it from the front, from the ice side, inside the cone where the crew would sit, and photographed the shield face and its stencils from there. Then she looked for the core plate and found it where she had feared it would be. It was riveted to the aft quarter of the vessel and faced outboard and back toward the bell, out in the open where the shadow had never been meant to reach, because nobody was supposed to stand there. The plate would not come to her. She stepped out of the cone and went to it.

She had expected the plate to be scratched off like the hull registration, and it wasn't. Whoever had sanded the Mule's name had not thought to hide its reactor. She got the serial: *TK-Δ / 2187 / 22-0419*. That made it twenty-seven years old. The actuator cabling was another matter. It was new, bright and clean, zip-tied in tidy runs, and two of the actuator housings had brand-new seal rings with the grease still white.

Her badge made a small sound.

It wasn't the chirp. It was the soft sub-audible tick it gave each time it passed a dose increment, which she had stopped noticing on her first day because it happened only twice a day, at background. Now it happened again, and then again.

She put her hand on the vessel wall. The metal was faintly warm. Nobody runs a tug reactor in an enclosed hangar. But someone had brought this one up to idle, very low, for some length of time, recently enough that the structure still glowed with induced activity. It was something like a stove after the burner is turned off.

You test a drum actuator by running it. You run it because you mean to use it.

She had taken that warmth in through her palm and through everything else she had standing out here. Each tick was a line she would not get to strike. She took the serial a second time to be sure of it, then stepped forward along the vessel and back into the shadow of the shield, where she would have stood if she'd been crew.

The ticks slowed. They didn't stop. The cone was a geometry and the hangar was not. The core had warmed its own shield, the ribs and the bare tanks were throwing a little of it back, and twenty-four blocks of ice were scattering some of the rest into the shade. Nothing in a closed room was ever a clean shadow. She worked quickly from the edge of it. She photographed a maintenance tag clipped to an actuator housing: *ACT. 4 — RESEAT — HOL — FUNC OK — D9.* That was Day 9, three days ago. She photographed the floor around the tail, where a fine dark spill of regolith had been swept into a neat pile and left. For the drum housings she needed two angles so she could compute their size later, and the second angle was out in the open again. She leaned out for it, took it, and came back.

Tick. Tick. Tick.

She had been counting the ticks. Eleven, then twelve. Each one was a small slice of her budget that she couldn't get back. Somewhere a dosimeter reader on a mesh server was patiently waiting for her badge to come back into range so it could learn the number. Somewhere behind the blue door Teo would read it, because Teo read everything.

At fourteen she stopped. That was enough. She backed away from the shield along the ice, and the ticks slowed further, then stopped. She went out along the wall the way she had come, at a flat, counted walk.

The personnel door. The yellow tape. The cold corridor, every other light strip dead and the smell of freezer ice. She counted thirty meters to the cracked node, then the first live node beyond it, a little green light on its bracket like a cat's eye in a hedge.

Her badge reacquired the mesh with its small electronic hiccup.

"Ms. Varga," said Marek Saar.

He was standing beside the live node with his back to the wall, in the dark strip between two working lights, where she would have walked straight past him if he hadn't spoken. Gray jacket, iron hair, hands folded in front of him like a man waiting outside an office. He looked as if he had been there a while and had not minded at all.

"Chief Saar," she said.

"It's Marek," he said. "I did say." He let his eyes go over her: the camera at her hip, the dust in the seams of her sleeves, a small green leaf she now realized was stuck to her collar. He looked at the leaf with real interest.

"I read your addendum," he said. "The D-line branch. Very thorough. 'Recommend Facilities node repair.' I put a priority on it for you. Facilities should have a crew down here in a month or two." He smiled, and the smile took its usual careful inventory of her, item by item. "In the meantime I've been wondering what an auditor finds to verify in a hangar sealed for salvage, at half past two in the morning, without a work order."

She opened her mouth. She had something, an answer half assembled out of a pipe, a pending survey and her own good name.

"Auditors who wander," Saar said gently, "get audited."

At her collarbone the badge finished uploading its night to the mesh, compared it with the threshold someone behind a blue door had set for it, and chirped. It was bright, clear and unmistakable in the dead corridor, a small electronic bird announcing to everyone on the network exactly where she had been and how much of it she had carried back.

Twenty-six days remained to the blackout.

## Chapter 5: The Dome

Saar walked her home.

He did it courteously, a pace behind and a little to the left, the way a man might escort a guest who had wandered into the wrong wing of a house. They climbed from L6 to Ring C in a silence measured by the slap of their soles on the gritted treads. At 0.126 g every stair took a half-second of float, so the climb had the rhythm of a slow, insincere waltz. At her door he waited while she palmed the lock. The seal rasped on its grit, as it always did.

"Your badge flagged," he said. "That isn't my department. I'll ask the warden for your number anyway, and she'll tell me no. She always tells me no." He seemed pleased about it. "Sleep, Auditor Varga. The station's quite safe. I've looked."

The door closed on his smile. Piq stood in her six cubic meters with her back to it and counted to a hundred and twenty before she let herself sit. Then she wrote on her tally pad in her father's shorthand:

*0304. S. waited at first live node. Read addendum. Knew time.*

Under it, smaller:

*Badge = beacon. 90 s. Forgot.*

That was the real failure, and it was worse than being seen. She had planned the night around the dead node at L6 and the thirty meters of corridor beyond it where the mesh went blind. The badge on her collar had pinged faithfully every ninety seconds right up to the edge of that blindness, and then it had stopped. Anybody watching would have seen the stop. The badge had picked up again when she walked back into coverage, and its next ping had uploaded fourteen ticks of dose all at once, like a drunk confessing.

Saar had not needed to follow her. He had only needed to read where she vanished and wait where she would reappear.

She lay down on the bunk in her coveralls. Above her the light-pipe held Jupiter in a coin of pale ochre, unmoving, as it had been since she arrived and would be until she left. A sliver of something green was stuck in the seam of her sleeve. She picked it out, weighed it on her fingertip by habit (under a gram, call it 0.3), and could not think what to do with it, so she ate it.

---

The summons came at 0611, as a line of text across her badge's little display:

**REPORT DOSE OFFICE 0800. BRING YOUR LOG. — T. ANAND**

No *please*, and no reason given. Piq approved of it professionally and resented it personally, and she spent the hour and forty-nine minutes until eight building her log.

It was a good log. She had kept it honestly through Day 11 and only lightly fictionalized Day 12, and the fictions were the kind an auditor could defend. She had been surveying the D-line branch, which was true. She had traced it to L6, also true, and the meter at D-7 really had drifted. She had followed the conduit beyond the mesh to check routing against the as-builts. That was nearly true, if a hangar counted as routing.

She would have to account for forty-six minutes and roughly eighty microsieverts. She assembled the minutes first, because minutes were easier. She gave herself eleven for conduit inspection, fourteen for photographing junction labels, nine for a pump-room door that was genuinely stiff, and twelve for retracing her route after losing her bearings in an undocumented space. Ninety seconds per ping meant thirty-one pings missed. She checked the arithmetic twice.

At 0758 the blue door slid back on its borated-poly smell and its faint ozone, and Teodora Anand looked up from three screens and did not stand.

"Sit," she said. "Hand me the log."

Piq sat. The chair was hard, and set at the exact height where the desk edge caught her forearms. She handed over the tablet. Teo's hair was tied back with the same length of cable insulation, grey this time and fraying at the cut end. Her sleeves were rolled to the elbow. The old brass dosimeter hung from its cord against her shirt with its cracked face turned inward, like a locket somebody had stopped opening.

Teo read the log. She took her time, and her mouth did nothing at all.

"Eleven minutes of conduit inspection," she said.

"The branch is unmapped. I had to verify the run."

"At L6 the conduit is buried in regolith fill, under four point two meters of overhead in that stretch. The fill reads point four microsieverts an hour. I know because I surveyed it after the floor slumped in 2209 and I had to sign the reoccupancy." Teo did not look up. "Eleven minutes at point four is seventy-three nanosieverts. Go on."

"Fourteen minutes photographing junction labels."

"Same overhead, same fill. Ninety-three nano." She scrolled. "A stiff door for nine minutes. I'll grant you the door. Doors here are very stiff. They don't emit."

"I lost my bearings," Piq said. "The as-builts are thirty years old and wrong about—"

"Twelve minutes of being lost." Teo put the tablet down flat on the desk and finally looked at her. Her eyes were as dark and steady as Piq remembered. Being looked at by them felt like being placed on a scale and left there. "Forty-six minutes in L6 fill comes to three hundred and seven nanosieverts. Point three one micro, if you like round numbers, and I don't. Your badge is reporting eighty-one point six. You're out by a factor of two hundred and sixty-six. Auditor Varga, let me tell you what I think you're doing, and you can correct my rounding."

"I'd be glad to."

"You've given me a sum of minutes that equals the minutes. Forty-six exactly. That's the part that's right, and you're presenting it as if the dose will follow from it." She turned one screen around. A histogram filled it, a sawtooth of narrow peaks across a band of energies. "Your badge doesn't just count. It bins. It keeps a spectrum, a very poor one, but I can read it. This is Ring C." She tapped a smooth low hummock. "Callisto regolith, a little cosmic, a little Jupiter leaking in through the overhead. This is the Sling tour with Hesper, which had the frost face." She tapped a second trace, the same hummock with a shoulder on it. "And this is last night."

The third trace was not a hummock. It was a broad continuum that climbed the energy axis much higher than the others had any reason to go, and near its upper end, alone, a single needle stood up out of the noise.

"Two point two two three MeV," Teo said. "Do you know what that is?"

Piq did. Her father had kept a chart of capture lines taped inside the weigh-master's locker on the *Perihelion Ladder*, because the cycler's reactor shield had been water and he had liked to know what the water was doing. She said, "No."

"No," Teo repeated, as if entering it in a column for later reconciliation. "Then I'll tell you, and you can be surprised. A free neutron wanders into hydrogen, slows down, and gets caught. The hydrogen becomes deuterium and throws off the difference as a gamma, always the same gamma. Two point two two three. It's the cleanest line in nature. You can't fake it with rock, and you can't fake it with cosmic background, not at that height. Neutrons have to come from somewhere, and the hydrogen has to be standing close enough to catch them." She let the needle sit between them. "Water, say, packed around something that makes neutrons."

The ozone in the room seemed to sharpen. Piq kept her hands still on the desk edge, where the chair had put them.

"And the continuum," Teo went on, in the same tone, "is what you get when you're near the thing that makes them. Prompt gammas, activation products, the whole ugly shelf. On this station there's one licensed fission source with that profile. It's in the depot pumphouse behind two meters of borated wall. Your badge did not go to the pumphouse." She leaned back. "So my rounding says you spent some meaningful fraction of forty-six minutes within a few tens of meters of an operating or recently operated reactor that is wrapped in ice and isn't on my register. Correct me."

Piq could have said a great many things. She could have said *junction labels* again, with feeling. She could have said *calibration fault*, and watched Teo take that apart in under a minute. She sorted through them as she would sort a cargo manifest for the item that would bear inspection, and found none.

"I'd want to see the badge's calibration history," she said, "before I accepted a single needle as dispositive."

Something happened at the corner of Teo's mouth. It was not a smile. It was the place a smile would have been if she had been a different person, or this a different conversation.

"That's the most honest thing you've said since you sat down."

The desk chimed. A text banner slid across the top of the leftmost screen, and because the screen was still turned half toward Piq she could read it:

**JDA SECURITY / SAAR, M. — REQUEST: CURRENT CUMULATIVE + 24H INCREMENT, RESIDENT VARGA, P. (TEMP). PURPOSE: SITE SAFETY.**

Teo looked at it for about as long as it took to read twice. Then she pulled the keyboard toward her and typed with two fingers, unhurried:

**DOSE RECORDS ARE MEDICAL UNDER JDA HEALTH STATUTE 4.11. NO SAFETY CONDITION EXISTS. REQUEST DECLINED. — T. ANAND, WARDEN.**

She sent it. The banner folded itself away.

"He said you'd tell him no," Piq said, before she could weigh whether to.

"He asks every time someone goes somewhere he can't see. I always tell him no. It's the only regular conversation we have." Teo's eyes went back to the histogram. "It wasn't for you."

Piq waited for the rest of the sentence, the part that said who it was for. It didn't come. Teo cleared Saar's banner from her history with a flick and did not open a report form. There was a report form. Piq had seen its tab on the second screen: *NONCONFORMING SOURCE — NOTIFICATION*, with a red field for the reporter's name. Any warden who found an unregistered neutron source on her station was obliged by about four statutes to fill that form in the same hour. The hour was passing. Teo's cursor stayed in the spectrum view.

Piq tried to tally it and couldn't. On one side was a woman who believed rules were the only thing that had never lied to her, a woman who had refused Piq a dosimeter waiver on Day 1 with a contempt so pure it was almost restful. On the other side was a reactor in the ice, a form unopened, and a security chief told no. The columns didn't foot. There was a missing entry somewhere, and Piq could not find even its line number.

"You're not going to file it," she said.

"I'm going to file your dose." Teo touched a key. "Eighty-one point six microsieverts, source unattributed, investigation open. Investigation will remain open until I'm satisfied." She looked up. "I'm rarely satisfied."

"That isn't the same thing."

"No. It isn't." Teo held her gaze a moment longer than the sentence required, and whatever she was weighing, she did not share the scale. "You also smell of the hydroponics gallery. There's a lettuce rack in Gallery Four that came down off its rail yesterday, and the gardener filed a complaint about an *airborne auditor*. Is that also in your log?"

"It's in my log as an unplanned contact with produce."

"Mass of produce?"

"Roughly two hundred grams displaced," Piq said. "Point three consumed."

Teo shut her eyes briefly, the way people did when they decided not to laugh, and that was worse. It was much worse, because it meant there had been a laugh to decide against.

"Your badge," Teo said, opening her eyes, "is a cheap Ibis unit with a crystal that drifts warm. If I'm going to hold an open investigation on it, I want it calibrated against a reference I trust. Take it off."

Piq unclipped it from her collar and held it out. Teo didn't take it. She stood, picked a hard case off the shelf behind her, and put on her jacket.

"Not here," she said. "My check sources are in here. The background in this office is contaminated by my own good intentions. Bring it."

There were three techs on the dose staff, and any one of them could have walked an Ibis badge to a jig. Piq noted this. Teo, sealing her case, gave no sign of having noticed anything. Neither of them said it aloud, and the omission went into the ledger unbalanced alongside the rest.

---

The viewing dome sat at the top of the oldest shaft in Asgard, a blister of laminated glass and polymer under a hood of regolith that could be cranked back on a winch. It was the only place on the station where a person could stand under sky with nothing between her and Jupiter but a few centimeters of window and a polite fraction of a millisievert a day.

Teo cranked the hood. It ground back along its track, shedding grit that fell upward-and-then-down in slow, lazy arcs and pattered on the glass like the beginning of a rain that would never commit. Light came in.

Piq had seen Jupiter every day since arrival through the light-pipe in her room, reduced to a coin. Here it was the size of a held-out hand, low over the crater rim and fixed there, because Callisto had stopped turning its face away a billion years ago and would never start again. The bands were bigger than any photograph had prepared her for: cream and rust, tan and a grey that was nearly blue, ruled across the disc with a draftsman's patience. The edge of the dome's outer pane was furred with frost. The banded light lay across the frost in stripes, ochre on one crystal and white on the next, as though the old man were pressing his own ledger into the ice.

"It doesn't move," Piq said.

"It moves. Very slowly. You'll see it nod about seven degrees over a libration cycle if you stand here long enough." Teo set her case on the bench. "Nobody stands here long enough."

The calibration jig was a squat aluminum fixture bolted to the bench, with a lead-lined well and a clamp. Teo opened her case. Inside, nested in foam, lay three small sealed discs, each labeled in her square hand. She lifted the cesium source with tongs, dropped it into the well, and held out her other hand for the badge.

Piq gave it to her. Their fingers didn't touch. Piq noted the distance anyway: about a centimeter, though it was a centimeter that had cost something to keep.

"Six-six-two keV," Teo said, clamping the badge over the well. "If your crystal is honest, the line lands in bin forty-one. If it's warm, it'll walk." She watched the display. "It's walking. Bin forty-three. You're reading two percent hot."

"So the needle last night—"

"Was still two point two. Two percent of two point two two three is forty-four keV. Your needle is in the right place for a hot crystal." She looked up without lifting her head. "You were hoping I'd say otherwise."

"I was hoping you'd say it with less satisfaction."

"I'm a scientist. It's all the satisfaction I'm allowed." She keyed a correction into the badge through her own tablet, and the display flickered and settled. "Now you're honest. Clip it on."

Piq lifted it toward her collar and fumbled, because the clip was a stiff cheap thing and she was watching the frost. The badge slid on her knuckles and dropped. At 0.126 g it took its time, falling in a slow tilt that gave everyone present ample opportunity to catch it.

Teo caught it. Then, instead of handing it back, she stepped in and clipped it to Piq's collar herself. Her knuckles brushed the line of Piq's jaw as she seated it. Her breath smelled faintly of the station's bitter tea. The procedure took four seconds, and Piq would later be able to reconstruct each of them and assign none of them a value.

"Collar, left side, face out," Teo said, stepping back. "The way the form says. You've been wearing it face in."

"I haven't."

"Your Ring C spectrum is attenuated by about a centimeter of soft tissue. It's been face in for at least four days." She closed the case. "Your log is a work of fiction and your badge is upside down. You'd make a terrible auditor."

"I'm told I'm an excellent one."

"Then Asgard is in a great deal of trouble."

The cesium source went back into its foam. Teo didn't crank the hood closed. She sat down instead on the far end of the bench, under the banded light, and after a moment Piq sat at the near end. Between them lay the case and about a meter and a half of scuffed polymer, which was a measurable distance and, Piq judged, a deliberate one.

The badge clicked softly on Piq's collar. It had an audio mode for field work, a quiet tick per detected event, and Teo hadn't switched it off. Up here, under thin cover, it ticked more than it did in Ring C: a sparse, unhurried rain, two or three clicks to a breath.

"Listen to that," Teo said. "Jupiter's background. Most of what you're hearing is the old man's belts leaking out this far. Callisto sits outside the worst of it, so it's barely a drizzle." She tilted her head. "The old wardens said you could tell the time by it."

"By the click rate?"

"By the song." Teo's voice changed slightly, as if she were quoting someone she'd been fond of. "*Europa ducks behind the old man every four and a half days, and the dosimeters sing differently when it does.* Haralsen used to say that. He was warden before me. He'd sit up here with an open counter and a cup of something illegal and swear he could hear it."

"Could he?"

"He was drunk most nights after 2200, so it's hard to audit." She shrugged one shoulder. "It's folklore. The physics says Europa's torus is nowhere near us and shouldn't make any difference to a counter on Callisto. But I've logged six years of night readings, and there's a little dip in the high-energy bins that repeats. It's not much. It's in the noise, mostly. It's there if you want it."

"Every four and a half days."

"Europa's synodic period from here. Four point five. Everything on Asgard keeps that clock. Sling windows, depot shifts, half the station's sleep. You'll start keeping it too, if you stay."

Piq turned the number over. Four point five days. The silences she'd been logging on her covert receiver came about thirty hours in every four and a half days, steady as a shift change. She had read them as a roster on Day 9, and she read them as a roster now, with a click of satisfaction that felt like a column footing: of course the cabal kept Europa's clock. The depot shifts ran on it. Everyone here ran on it. Whoever was keying those pulses worked the same rota as the Sling, went off-watch when the windows closed, and came back when they opened. It was precisely what she would expect of people who weren't careful.

She filed it, tidy, and didn't ask herself why it felt like the old man's light on the frost: one band striped across two different crystals.

"My sister used to sit up here," Teo said.

Piq kept her face as it was. It took some effort, and she was good at it.

"Amaya. She's five years younger than me. She learned to count clicks on this bench when she was twelve, on Haralsen's counter, with his illegal cup confiscated. She was better at it than me. She could hear the difference between a cosmic and a belt electron by ear, or she said she could. I could never prove she was lying." Teo was looking at Jupiter, not at Piq. "She went on to be an engineer. Tugs. She was on the *Ninefold Grace*."

The name went through Piq like a dropped tool, slow and certain.

"You'll know it," Teo said. "You're from that side of the business. A Europa tug, lost with all hands fourteen months ago. Lagrange Mutual underwrote the hull and the crew's lives. They denied the claim." Her voice didn't rise; it got flatter, as if it were being rolled. "Pilot error. The pilot *deviated to a low-energy trajectory*. That's the phrase. I've read the denial forty times. I could recite it to you. They didn't come out, they didn't send anyone, and they decided from forty-five light-minutes away that four people died of a bad choice, which meant nobody owed anyone anything. Then they raised our rates."

Piq's hands lay on her knees. She checked that they were still, and they were. She checked her breathing: even. She checked her face, which she could not see, against the memory of what it was supposed to look like, and judged it acceptable. Under all of it, in a place she did not normally keep accounts, something had gone cold and tight around the word *Lagrange*, and she let it stay there unexamined, because she had no column for it.

"I'm sorry," she said, which was true and which was the smallest true thing available.

"Don't be. You're an auditor." Teo glanced at her, quick and hard. "Not that you're so different. You lot write the rules, and they write the checks, and the checks bounce off the rules. Same species of vulture. I told you that on Day One."

"You did."

"I meant it."

"I know."

They sat in the ticking for a while. The banded light had moved, or seemed to have; a stripe of rust now lay across the frost where cream had been. Piq couldn't tell whether that was Jupiter's slow nod or simply that she was looking at the frost from a slightly different angle, because at some point she had turned on the bench to face Teo more than the window.

"Why did you bring me up here?" she asked. "One of your techs could have run the jig."

Teo considered the question the way she'd considered the log: thoroughly, without visible reaction.

"The background's stable," she said. "Good reference."

"That's a reason."

"It's the reason I'm giving." Teo stood and picked up her case. "Wear your badge face out. Stay out of L6. If your number goes up again by a factor of two hundred and sixty-six, I'll ground you, and I won't tell Saar why, and I'll enjoy both."

She went to the winch and cranked the hood. Regolith ground along the track, and the banded light narrowed over the frost to a strip, then to a line, and then went out. The ticking on Piq's collar slowed almost at once to the Ring C drizzle, as if something had stopped singing.

At the hatch Teo paused without turning.

"Amaya would have liked you," she said. "She liked people who were wrong in interesting ways."

Then she was gone down the shaft in a long, unhurried low-g drop, and Piq was alone under the closed hood, holding a tally that would not close. It had too many entries on one side and nothing she could name on the other. Her father would have said that when the ledger wouldn't foot, you hadn't finished counting. Her father had never sat under that light.

---

Back in her six cubic meters, with the door sealed and a towel stuffed in the vent grille out of a superstition she couldn't justify, Piq took the comms unit out of its housing.

The unit was the hollow one she had declared at the weigh-in as *audit reference masses*, 1.3 kilograms of Lagrange engineering pretending to be lumps of calibrated steel. Opened, it unfolded into a receiver no bigger than her palm, a burst transmitter, and a crypto core that would turn to slag if anyone pried at it wrong. She ran the receiver's lead up the light-pipe, where it could see a little of the sky through the station's surface optics, and tuned to the retired dosimetry maintenance band.

The pulses were there. They were faint, patient and regular: short groups of keyed carrier, a pause, another group. She had stopped trying to read them on Day 9; whatever code they used wasn't one Lagrange had given her. She logged timing and carrier, and she let the receiver's little waterfall display paint them down the screen in green dashes.

Tonight, because she'd just had her own badge proven two percent hot, she checked the carrier frequency with the same suspicion.

It wasn't where she'd left it.

She pulled the log. On Day 9 she'd written the center frequency to six figures. On Day 10 it was a hair higher, and Day 11 higher again. Tonight it was lower than Day 9. She plotted it, center against time, and the points didn't scatter as noise would. They traced a curve: a slow, smooth rise and fall, as if the transmitter's whole voice were breathing over a period of days.

Piq looked at it for a while.

Then she did what any auditor did with an anomaly too tidy to be fraud: she looked for the cheapest explanation. Crystal oscillators drifted with temperature. Her own badge had drifted two percent warm, and that was a factory-calibrated Ibis unit, not some backroom transmitter built out of salvage by people who stole ice. A cheap oscillator in an unheated space would wander with the station's thermal cycle, and on Callisto the thermal cycle followed the local day, sixteen point seven Earth days of slow light and slow dark. Or it followed the depot's duty cycle, pumps running and resting, on that same four-and-a-half-day clock that everything here obeyed. A careless cabal would bolt a cheap rig to a warm pipe and never think to compensate. The drift was, if anything, a signature. These were people who could steal three hundred and forty tonnes of ice and couldn't be bothered to buy a temperature-compensated crystal.

She wrote it down:
