*Carrier drift, slow periodic. Thermal? Cheap osc., uncompensated. Pumps or local day. Careless.*

She underlined *careless*, because it pleased her, and didn't notice that the period of the curve fit neither the local day nor the pump cycle as neatly as she'd written.

The crypto core chirped.

It was a quiet sound, designed to be mistaken for a badge alarm, and it meant a packet had come down the Lagrange channel and passed authentication. She set the receiver aside and opened it. The header read **ORIG L4 / PELL A. / SENT 0941 UTC / RECV 1030 LOCAL**. Forty-nine minutes. It had been written before she ever sat in Teo's hard chair, by a man answering a version of her that was already gone.

The body was three lines. Pell never used four where three would hold.

**ACKNOWLEDGE THROW FRAGMENT CORROBORATED. PRIORITY: IDENTIFY THROW CABAL, PERSONNEL AND LAUNCH ASSET. TREAT DEPOT CREW AS HOSTILE UNTIL CLEARED. PELL.**

Piq read it twice, the way Teo had read Saar's request: once for what it said, and once for what it would cost.

*Depot crew* meant Hesper, who had shown her the Sling with the pride of a woman showing off a child. It meant the gardener in Gallery Four, and Lucky, whose wedding she was apparently going to officiate. It meant every one of the faces she'd seen in the hangar last night, wrapping ice blocks around a tug with the care of people dressing a wound. Forty-nine minutes ago, in Pell's ledger, they had all become a single entry, and the entry was red.

She thought, without wanting to, of a centimeter of air between two fingers over a calibration jig. She thought of a report form left unopened on a screen.

The receiver on her bunk kept painting green dashes down its little screen, patient and regular, the carrier breathing up and down on its slow, careless curve. Somewhere, someone she had just been ordered to treat as an enemy was keying them.

Piq set her stylus against a fresh line of the tally pad, and for the first time in her working life she didn't know which column to write in.

## Chapter 6: Vows

The third gap opened at 1942 on Day 13, to the minute.

Piq watched it happen from her bunk with the towel stuffed in the vent grille and the receiver's thread of antenna running up the light-pipe. The carrier had been ticking along at its usual feeble strength: short-long-short-short, pause, the stammer, the long fallow stretches of nothing that might have been someone thinking. Then it was gone. It did not fade or sputter. It was there, and then the band was empty hiss.

She wrote the time on her tally pad in her father's shorthand and drew a box around it. Then she pulled up the depot roster she had copied from the shift board outside the pumphouse, and ran her finger down the column she already knew by heart.

OKAFOR-LUND, H. — COIL MAINT., SOLO — 1600–2400.

Three for three, if you were generous with the first one. Piq was not generally generous, and she noticed that she was being generous now. She noted the noticing and set it aside, the way she would set aside a weighing taken on a scale she had not personally zeroed.

The carrier came back at 0152 on Day 15. Thirty hours and ten minutes. The same as before, as if someone kept a very good clock.

When it returned it was lower in frequency than she had last logged it. That was a small thing, a few parts per million, but her receiver's readout was honest even if the hardware on the other end was not. She plotted the new points beside the old ones. The curve kept climbing and falling in its slow, patient swell, like a tide in a basin. She considered it for a while.

Sunrise had come over the Valhalla rim on Day 14. Eight days of night had ended in a long, grey brightening, and now the regolith overhead was warming by tenths of a degree. Somewhere in the cabal's equipment a cheap crystal was warming too, and drifting.

*Careless,* she wrote. She did not write *?* beside it, which her father would have told her was a mistake.

---

The wedding was in Commons Three, the oldest public room on Asgard. It sat at the base of the original shaft and had been dug when the station was forty people and a drill. The ceiling was raw regolith sealed in resin the colour of weak tea. Someone had strung lights along the cable trays, and someone else had argued for an hour with Facilities about the extra wattage and lost. So the lights were the low-draw kind, a scatter of amber points that made the room look like the inside of a lantern somebody had forgotten to finish.

Seventy-one people came. Piq counted them as they arrived, because she could not stop herself. She also counted the chairs, which were fifty-four. The difference was absorbed by the tug crews, who preferred to stand along the back wall with their knees slightly flexed in the way of people who had spent their lives expecting the floor to move.

The screen took up most of the forward wall. On it, nine hundred thousand kilometres and three seconds away in each direction, was the bridge of the Ur tanker *Steadfast Margin*. It was a tight grey space crowded with handholds. Five Ur crew had strapped themselves into various attitudes of formal attention, one of them upside down relative to the others, and in the middle of them was Ziva Kalnins.

Piq had seen her face only once before, in a three-second clip attached to the wedding packet. In person, or in whatever the screen counted as, Ziva was tall even folded into a crash couch. She had pale hair cropped close and a long, still face that seemed to be keeping its expressions in a locked drawer. She wore an Ur flight jacket with the collar turned up. On it someone had pinned a single sprig of something green, which must have cost her a hydroponics tithe and a favour.

Lucky Mbeki stood under the screen in a borrowed jacket a size too large, vibrating. He had knocked on the doorframe three times on the way in, on the table three times, and on the bulkhead beside the screen three times. Piq expected that if the ceremony ran long he would knock on her.

"You're the mortician," he said, shaking her hand with both of his. "She said you sounded like a mortician. I told her morticians are very reliable. Nobody complains about a mortician's work, you know? It's one of the few—"

"I've read the packet," Piq said.

"All of it?"

"I read everything." The phrase came out in Teo's cadence, which she had not intended. Somewhere in the second row, she was aware without looking, Teo Anand had just sat down.

The link tech was a young woman called Ines with a headset and the expression of someone who had been told, firmly and by several parties, that this was not to go wrong. She gave the Ur camera a thumbs-up, then turned and gave Piq one. Six seconds after the first, a full round trip, an Ur crewman on the screen gave a thumbs-up back, by which time Ines had already looked away.

Piq stood at the lectern. It was an inverted cargo crate with a cloth over it, and under the cloth she could feel the stencil: MASS 14.2 KG TARE. She set her notary seal down beside her tablet. The seal was the size of a large coin, brass-cased, with a cryptographic chip and an Ibis Metrology mark. It was genuine, which remained the most confusing thing about her life.

"Good evening," she said. "We're here to witness the joining of Dov Mbeki and Ziva Kalnins—"

"Good evening," said Ziva from the screen, to the beginning of the sentence, some time after Piq had finished it.

"—under the Civil Schedule of the Jovian Development Authority—"

"Is it starting?" said someone on the Ur bridge, also late. Their muffled voice overlapped Piq's word *Schedule*, so that for a moment the commons heard *Civil Scheduling*, which was not a thing.

Piq stopped. She had officiated nothing in her life. She had, however, sat through three hundred and eleven cargo sign-offs on her father's cycler and in the warehouses after, and every one of them had had the same problem. Two people need to agree on a single number. They cannot both talk at once. Somebody has to say *over*.

"Ines," she said, "I'd like to suspend the ceremony for a procedural note."

Ines looked stricken. On the screen, three seconds behind, Ziva's eyebrows went up a millimetre. Piq had the impression that this was the equivalent of a shout.

"There's a three-second lag each way," Piq said, to the room and to the screen at once. "If anyone answers before the question has arrived, we'll have two weddings happening slightly out of phase. I'm only notarized for one. So we'll do this the way a weigh-master does a handover. One party speaks. When you've finished, say *over*. The other party repeats back the essential term, then responds. I'll countersign each exchange. Nobody speaks while the light's in transit. Are we agreed? Over."

There was a silence in the commons, then a laugh from the back wall, where the tug crews had recognized the protocol. Three seconds later the laugh arrived from the *Steadfast Margin*'s bridge as a second, smaller wave. Three seconds after that, the Ur crew were laughing at the Asgard crowd's laugh, which they had just heard, and then the Asgard crowd laughed at being laughed at. For a quarter of a minute the joke ricocheted between two moons at the speed of light, losing amplitude on every bounce, like a ball dropped in low gravity.

On the screen, Ziva waited until it had decayed entirely. "Agreed," she said. "Over."

"Agreed, agreed, absolutely agreed," said Lucky. "Over. Sorry. Over."

"Countersigned," Piq said, and touched the seal to the tablet. It gave a tiny tone. She felt, absurdly, steadier.

---

The poem was next. Ziva's single request in the packet had been a short Latvian verse, transcribed phonetically by someone who had clearly not met Piq. Piq read it out with the care she would give a manifest in a language she didn't speak, which was the only care she had.

"*Kā gulbis baltā,*" she said, "*pāri ezeram—*"

"*Ezeram,*" came Ziva's voice three seconds later. Her pronunciation was different from Piq's in a way Piq could not have described. The difference was the length of a vowel and the place a word leaned.

Piq kept going. "*—iet pa ūdeni, neslīkst—*"

"*Neslīkst.*"

"*—jo ūdens viņu nes.*"

"*Nes,*" said Ziva, a correction delivered with such gentle precision that nobody in the room laughed, not even the back wall. Piq looked up from the tablet and found Ziva's still face in the screen looking not at her but somewhere just below the camera, at Lucky, a full second before Piq's last word could have reached her. She had known where the poem ended.

"What does it mean?" Lucky whispered. He was not over. Nobody stopped him.

Ziva heard him three seconds later and, unusually, answered out of turn. "It means don't let him write the vows. Over."

"I wrote the vows," said Lucky.

"I know," said Ziva, three seconds into the future and also, Piq realized, not answering his statement but predicting it. The commons laughed, and so did the bridge. The two laughs crossed in the middle somewhere near the orbit of Ganymede.

---

Afterwards Piq would reconstruct the vows from the feed, because in real time they had become something closer to weather.

She had asked Ines, quietly, whether the link was holding. "Are we stable?"

Lucky, who had the attention of a man listening very hard for the one thing he had rehearsed, said, "I will. Forever. Yes. Over."

Ines looked at Piq. Piq looked at Lucky.

"That was a technical question," she said. "Countersigning nothing."

On the screen, three seconds later, Ziva said, "What did he agree to?" and the bridge crew lost all composure. One of the strapped-in crewmen came loose from his handhold and drifted slowly up out of frame, still laughing. The back wall of Commons Three heard that, three seconds later, and lost theirs.

Lucky's vows ran to four minutes. Piq timed them. They began with the first time he had seen Ziva, across a propellant dock at Ur three years earlier, through a viewport and a pressure suit. They moved on to the eleven reschedulings of the wedding itself, each listed with its reason (crew change, solar storm, tanker refit, a cousin's funeral, a dispute over the cake, two separate Facilities audits). He described the trajectory he had once computed by hand to estimate when their orbits would next put them in the same room. He knocked on the lectern twice during this, then a third time, then apologized to the lectern.

He forgot to say *over* at the end. There was a pause, three seconds of the room holding its breath and Ziva on the screen apparently holding hers. Then her reply arrived. Piq realized afterwards that Ziva had begun it the instant the four minutes stopped, without waiting to be sure he was finished.

"Dov," Ziva said. "I don't make speeches. You know that. I come home with the tanks full. That's my vow. I come home with the tanks full, and I come home. Over."

Lucky stood very still for the first time all evening.

Piq looked at her tablet so that she would not look at anything else. There, in the background of the shot, she noticed a panel on the *Steadfast Margin*'s forward bulkhead and filed it, out of pure habit, without reading it.

"Repeat back the essential term," she said gently, after a moment.

Lucky swallowed. "Tanks full," he said. "Come home. Over."

"Countersigned."

The questions were the formal part, the JDA Civil Schedule wording. Piq read Lucky's first and he said "I do" in two words without knocking on anything, which was a personal best. Then she turned to the screen.

"Ziva Kalnins, do you take Dov Mbeki—"

"I do," said Ziva.

It arrived while Piq was saying *Mbeki*. Ziva had started it before *Ziva* could have reached her. She had read the packet too, and knew where the question went. She had simply not seen the point of waiting.

"—to be your partner under the Civil Schedule, sharing in all—" Piq carried on, because a question had to be asked in full to be legally a question.

"I said yes," came from the screen, over the top of *sharing*.

"—obligations of mass, air and water—"

"Is she still going?" from the bridge, faintly.

"—as long as you both shall live? Over."

Three seconds passed. Ziva's face did not move.

"Countersign it before she changes her mind," Ziva said. "Over."

Piq countersigned it. She had to put the seal down very carefully because her hand was not entirely level. Then she gave Lucky his line. He had requested it in the packet, underlined twice, and she had looked up the Civil Schedule specifically to make it true.

"By the power vested in me by Ibis Metrology," she said, "and by Schedule Nine of the Jovian Development Authority Civil Code, all of which I have read—" and somewhere in the second row a short, involuntary breath came out of somebody who knew precisely what else Piq claimed to have read, "—I now pronounce you married. You may—"

She stopped, because the next word was *kiss*, and they were 900,000 kilometres apart.

Lucky put his palm flat on the screen, over Ziva's face. Three seconds later, on the *Steadfast Margin*, Ziva put her palm flat over the camera. For a moment the bridge vanished behind the warm blur of her hand. The commons went quiet, and the quiet arrived on the tanker three seconds later, and then there was nothing to delay at all.

---

The cake was a printed slab of sugared protein the size of a ration case, frosted white, with a crude tug and a crude tanker piped on top in green. The tanker was considerably larger than the tug, which Piq suspected was Ziva's influence. It sat on a trestle under the cable tray.

Piq was standing two metres from it, accepting congratulations she had not earned from a pumphouse tech named Juno, when a dark shape detached itself from the cable tray. It descended into the cake with the unhurried grace of an object falling at one-eighth of a gee.

Ballast landed with all four feet in the tanker. He looked around. He had the expression of a cat who has calculated his arrival exactly and sees no reason to apologise for it. Seventy-one people stared at him. Then he bit off the bow of the *Steadfast Margin* and the entirety of the tug, crouched, and went.

His leap took him back up past the cable tray in a long, slow parabola, frosting flecking off his white bib, and into an overhead grille. It must have taken almost three seconds. Nobody moved for the whole of it, because everybody was calculating, without meaning to, where he would land.

"That's the third time," said somebody near the back.

"Who's logging it?" said somebody else.

Clerk Fitch was there. He had come because Lucky invited everyone, and he had brought a scale, because he always brought a scale. He stepped forward and lifted the remaining cake onto his platform with a grunt.

"Two point eight one kilos," he said. "Originally three point two. That's four hundred and some grams. Who pays?"

"It's a wedding," said Juno.

"Everything is someone's," Fitch said, not unkindly.

On the screen, three seconds behind, the Ur crew were watching the feed of a cat flying through a wedding. Piq had no way of knowing what they made of it. One of them was pointing.

When she turned away from the cake she found that Teo had stood up from the second row. The dose warden was watching her across the trestle, past the ruin of the tanker. Her mouth had moved. It was not a smile. It was the place where one would be, if Teo Anand permitted herself to carry anything that heavy in public. Her eyes stayed on Piq for exactly as long as it took Piq to notice, and then went to the grille where Ballast had disappeared.

Piq wrote nothing down, because she had no pad in her hand. Somewhere in her ledger, though, a line opened that had no column. She left it open. It would not foot, and she knew it would not, and she did not close it.

---

The message came at 2117, mid-reception, while Lucky was trying to teach two pumphouse techs a dance that required a great deal more gravity than Callisto was offering.

Her personal tablet buzzed once, the specific buzz she had assigned to Ibis Metrology traffic. She stepped into the corridor outside the commons, where the noise thinned to a hum, and opened it.

ORIG L4 / PELL A. / SENT 2028 UTC / RECV 2117 LOCAL

RE YOUR 0874. RECEIVED. CABAL INDICATORS NOTED AND FORWARDED TO SIGNALS. CONSISTENT WITH PRIOR. STAND BY FOR ASSESSMENT.

RE YOUR QUERY, CAT. LOG UNDER LUNAR ANIMAL TRANSIT SCHEDULE CLASS 3 (NON-WORKING, NON-EDIBLE). MASS TO BE RECORDED AS MEAN OF THREE INDEPENDENT WEIGHINGS, NOT SINGLE. RECORD METABOLIC CONSUMPTION AS RECURRING LIABILITY, EST. 60 G/DAY PROTEIN, 150 ML/DAY WATER. DEPRECIATION NOT APPLICABLE. DO NOT NAME IT. NAMED ANIMALS ARE HARDER TO WRITE OFF.

PELL.

She read it twice. Then she read the header a third time, because the header was the part that would tell her what it meant.

Her 0874 was the first full report. She had sent it on the night of Day 8, dressed in Ibis bulk headers as a routine audit upload. It covered the 340 tonnes, the restatements, the pulses, the gaps, her theory of a roster and a cabal and a throw. In the last paragraph, because it had seemed like a reasonable cover question at the time, she had asked how an auditor ought to log an undeclared cat.

It had taken a week to get to him, or a week to come back; the header did not say which leg had dawdled. Everything was slow this close to conjunction. The light was fifty minutes each way on a good day, the relay at Deimos was a bottleneck everyone on Asgard complained about, and Pell had never in his life answered anything the hour it arrived. Somewhere in that stack of delays her report had finally reached L4, and Augustin Pell had read it, forwarded her cabal to Signals in one line, and devoted four lines of his compressed, expensive bandwidth to the cat.

*Do not name it.*

She found she was smiling at a corridor wall. She stopped.

There was a second report in the queue, the 0911. She had sent it on Day 12 after the dose consult. It covered the Mule, the reactor plate, Saar, and her conviction that the hangar was the cabal's workshop. Pell would see it when he saw it. By then, she supposed, she would have learned enough to need to send a third.

She thought about that for a moment, standing in the corridor with the dance music coming through the wall. She decided it was inconvenient. Pell's terseness was a known quantity, the lag was a known quantity, and the two together made a slow, clumsy instrument. But it was an instrument. It worked. It had just proved it worked.

She closed the tablet. The cat had not been logged. She had seen him twice now and named him in her own head on the first occasion, and she suspected Pell knew her well enough to have guessed.

---

Teo found her in the corridor ten minutes later.

"You read the whole Civil Schedule," Teo said.

"Schedule Nine."

"You said 'all of which I have read'."

"I have read all of Schedule Nine."

"Have you read Appendix F?"

Piq said nothing. Teo let the silence sit until it was obvious that it was an answer, then tilted her head toward the corridor junction, away from the commons.

"Come with me," she said. "There's something I want you to read. You'll actually read it, because it's short."

The blue door, the borated poly, the ozone. Teo's office was a cell behind the intake room. It had a single desk, three screens, a cot folded against the wall, and a shelf of physical binders. They looked like something left over from the station's first decade, and were probably Haralsen's. The cracked brass dosimeter hung face-in at Teo's collarbone. She pulled the chair out for Piq, which seemed to surprise them both, and stood behind it to work the keyboard two-fingered.

"This is Amaya's last log," she said. "From the *Ninefold Grace*. Crew badges telemetered home when they had line of sight. Mine was the station of record. So I have it."

The screen filled with a dose-rate plot: time along the bottom, microsieverts per hour up the side on a log scale. For most of its length it was flat and low, the familiar background of a tug in cruise. Then, eleven hours before the end, it climbed onto a shelf, a high plateau, and stayed there.

"Filed trajectory," Teo said, and tapped. A second line appeared. It rose with the first, but it rose for only four hours and some minutes before it dropped back. "That's what her dose should have looked like if they'd followed the plan. Into the torus, through it, out. Four hours. She sat in it for eleven."

"That's the deviation," Piq said slowly. "The low-energy trajectory. Lagrange's adjusters had this."

"They had this. They read it the way you just did. Eleven hours in the torus means they took the slow road. The slow road is pilot error. Pilot error voids the policy." Teo's voice did not change. "Case closed."

"And you don't believe it."

"I believe the shelf. The shelf's real. I'm asking about this."

She tapped again, and the plot zoomed to its last minute.

At the end of the shelf the line went straight up. It did not climb. Inside forty seconds, Amaya's badge recorded a dose rate three orders of magnitude above the torus. The trace ran off the top of the chart and then simply stopped. There was no tail and no fall. Somewhere above the frame the badge had ceased to send.

"The adjusters called this a terminal artifact," Teo said. "Sensor fault consistent with impact."

Piq looked at it. She thought about every scale she had ever seen fail. Scales failed to zero, failed high, failed noisy. They failed when they broke. They did not fail upward, cleanly, for forty seconds, and then stop.

"Impact isn't radiation," she said.

"No."

"The sensor saw something before it stopped."

"The spectrum's in the packet." Teo reached past her shoulder to pull it up. Piq reached for the same line on the screen to scroll it, and their hands came to rest on the desk a few centimetres apart: Teo's knuckles, the scar on the second one, Piq's own fingers with their flat clipped nails. Neither of them moved. The badge at Piq's collar pinged the mesh, a faint electronic chirp, ninety seconds since the last. Piq counted it without meaning to, and in the space after it she had the distinct sensation of a number she had not entered.

Teo straightened up and took her hand back. "It's hard," she said. "The burst. Harder than the torus. Harder than anything a reactor would throw at you through a shadow shield. I can't place it. Haralsen's binders can't place it. The Lagrange adjuster didn't try."

"Why are you showing me this?"

Teo was quiet for a moment. "You were in L6 last week," she said. "Near an operating reactor that's not on my register, wrapped in ice. Water, the most expensive shielding on this moon. You lied about it to my face. You lie very well, by the way."

"Thank you."

"It wasn't a compliment." Teo looked at the plot, at the line that ran off the top. "Somebody on this station is building something that's meant to go somewhere dose matters. I'm not asking you what. I'm not reporting it. I wanted you to see why I'm not." She closed the window. "That's all."

It was not all. Piq knew it was not all, the same way she knew when a manifest had been written by someone tired. But Teo had already turned to the shelf of binders and pulled one down, which meant the conversation was over by procedure, and Piq had learned by now that Teo's procedures were walls she had chosen.

"Collar," Teo said without looking, as Piq stood. "Left side. Face out."

Piq checked. It was. She went anyway.

---

She did not sleep. She pulled the station budget instead.

The budget was public, nominally, in the way that most of Asgard's numbers were public: available to anyone with an audit credential, a quiet room and a tolerance for six-level nesting of cost centres. Piq had all three. She worked from the bunk with the tablet propped on her knees and her tally pad beside her, the light-pipe throwing a faint banded stripe across the ceiling. Jupiter, fixed and low, was nodding a fraction of a degree in its slow libration.

She was not looking for the sunshade. She was looking for the Brigade's money: who paid for a reactor reseat, ice cutters, a hangar marked TEMP on the as-builts for nine years. She found nothing like it, which was what she had expected. Brigades did not pay. They borrowed.

What she found instead, three levels into Facilities, was a line.

DEPOT SOLAR SHADE — PREVENTIVE / CORRECTIVE MAINT.
FY2212: 412,000 mk
FY2213: 388,000 mk
FY2214: 0 mk

Beside the zero there was a note: *Deferred. Director's discretion. Repricing year.* The cost-centre authorisation code was Rook's office code. It was the same six characters Piq had seen stamped beside every quarterly inventory restatement for a year and a half, flattening the depot's dwindling numbers back to level.

*They lose all my tickets on the shade,* Hesper had said. Nobody had lost them. Somebody had zeroed the line and let them pile up against the zero.

Piq sat with it. Then, because her father had taught her that a number was only half a fact, she got up and went to the light-pipe. She could just see the depot's edge from there, if she pressed her cheek to the cold casing and looked down along the reflection. The sun had been up for the better part of two days. On the depot's sunward face, where every crystal of frost should long since have gone to vapour, there was still a pale bloom. It was fainter now, but stubborn, catching a stripe of tawny band-light from Jupiter.

She went back to the bunk and wrote it down: *Shade unfunded FY14. Rook code. Same code as restatements. Frost persisting post-dawn.*

Beneath it, after some thought, she wrote: *Rook covering for losses. Brigade or someone. Shade cut frees maintenance funds — to where?*

It was a neat theory. It explained the code. It put Rook in the cabal's column, or at least in its pocket. It did not explain the frost at all, and she knew that. She looked at the frost line for a long time and then drew a small square beside it, the one her father used for *later*.

---

At 0340 she rewatched the wedding.

She told herself it was for the record, because a notary should review the event she had certified. That was true. It was also true that she wanted to see Ziva's palm on the camera again. She wanted to see how long the bridge had gone dark for, and to count it. She was not sure which of the two truths was the larger, so she logged them both.

Ines had saved the full feed at native resolution. Piq scrubbed past the poem, past the collision of *I do*, past Lucky's four minutes, and stopped when Ziva said *I come home with the tanks full*. Then she did what she had not had time to do in the commons. She stopped looking at Ziva and looked behind her.

The panel she had filed without reading was a propellant status display on the *Steadfast Margin*'s forward bulkhead. It was slightly out of focus over Ziva's left shoulder, and at native resolution it was just legible. Piq froze the frame and enhanced it, which helped a little, and then read it the way she would read a gauge across a warehouse floor.

PROP MAIN — 97.2%
AUX A — 100%
AUX B — 100%
EXT 1-4 — 94–98%

She sat back.

The *Steadfast Margin* was a Ur propellant tanker. Its job was to carry water, as ice or as slush in pressure bladders, between Ganymede's processing plants and the depots that bought from them. Some of that water it burned, to push the rest. On a routine Ganymede-Callisto run, fully laden with cargo, a tanker like that would load perhaps sixty per cent of its own working propellant. It would arrive with a margin of fifteen, sell the cargo, refuel at the far end and come home. Nobody filled the auxiliaries for a routine run. Nobody filled the external tanks. External tanks meant you were planning to change your orbit by a great deal more than a routine run would ask of you, or you were planning to wait a long time and then move quickly.

SM-114 had been loitering in a Ganymede-Callisto transfer orbit for nine days. Saar's advisory called its posture provocative. Piq had read that as theatre, a Martian-aligned station rattling a sabre over Europa and the JDA rattling one back.

She did the arithmetic on the tally pad. She guessed the tanker's dry mass from the class, guessed the external tank volume from the hull silhouette in the tracking image, and ran the numbers through the rocket equation's curve. The answer came out at something over three kilometres per second of capability above a routine margin. That was not provocation. That was intent.

She thought about the 0874 sitting in Pell's in-tray. *Consistent with prior.* She thought about her fraud theory: a slow slug, thrown onto SM-114's ellipse, caught. It was weak, she knew. It had always been weak. With three kilometres per second, though, you could catch a great deal. You could catch something thrown badly. You could catch something thrown somewhere nobody was watching, because the watching would be cut off in twenty-two days.

*Throw before conjunction. No one will see it until it's done.*

She wrote it all down carefully, in her father's shorthand, and drew a long arrow from SM-114 to the Sling, and another from the Sling to Hesper's name. She did not draw a question mark anywhere on the page. When she finished it was 0412, and the arithmetic sat on the pad looking more certain than she felt, which she had been taught was a warning and chose to treat as fatigue.

---

She slept two hours. At 0630, on her way to the depot for the morning weigh, she turned the corner at Ring C's east junction and found Hesper Okafor-Lund waiting for her.

The chief engineer was not doing anything that could reasonably be called waiting. She was standing beside a pressure panel with her thermos, palm flat on the casing the way she rested it on every piece of hardware she trusted, apparently reading a gauge. But the gauge was a humidity readout that nobody needed to read at 0630. The corridor ran one way in both directions. There was nowhere else for Piq to be going.

"Auditor," Hesper said.

"Chief."

"Fine wedding."

"Thank you."

"Fine protocol. *Over.*" Hesper smiled into her thermos. Her eyes did not move from the gauge. "Learned it from your father, I'd guess. Weigh-masters all talk like that. I trained under one, a long time ago."

Piq said nothing, which was the correct move, and felt it fail. In her experience a silence only worked against people who needed you to fill it, and Hesper did not appear to need anything.

"Rafe says there was a face on the grating," Hesper said. "Up at forty-one metres. Night of Day 11." She took a sip. "Achterberg says there was an auditor airborne out of the Gallery Two plenum the same night. Fitch says you came in with five reference masses and a cylinder you wouldn't let him spin. Teo hasn't said anything at all. Teo always says something."

She turned at last. Her eyes were pale and very steady, the eyes of a woman who had watched twelve-tonne slugs leave a forty-kilometre rail at six kilometres per second. She had learned to tell, by the sound alone, which of them would fly true.

"Ninety-one spoons in Canteen Two," Hesper said. "Three hundred on the books. You found that your second day and didn't file it. I've seen your work, Piq Varga. You count everything. So I'm asking you once, nicely, while it's still just the two of us in a corridor."

She set the thermos down on the panel and let her palm rest on it.

"What are you really weighing?"

## Chapter 7: Echo

"Ice," Piq said.

Hesper Okafor-Lund didn't move. She stood in the Ring C east junction with her thermos tucked under one arm and her free palm flat on the bulkhead beside her, the way she touched hardware she had decided to trust. Piq understood that she was not being offered the same courtesy. The junction lights were still on their night setting, a dim amber that made the old woman's stubble of white hair look like frost on a cable tray. Somewhere above them a fan caught on a burr in its housing and ticked once each revolution.

"Ice," Hesper repeated.

"Your depot is short." Piq had decided on the walk down from her quarters that she would give up one true thing to keep the rest, and that it would have to be a heavy one. A light truth reads as a lie. "Not a little short. I've reconciled six quarters of intake, throw and reclamation, and the books come out light every time. Someone has restated the shortfall flat, six quarters running, under one office code. I'm the auditor of record. When this goes to the Authority it goes under my name, and I don't sign totals I can't foot."

"How short?"

Piq told her. She watched the number land and filed the reaction: no surprise, only a careful kind of attention, like a pilot hearing a figure she already knew read back by a stranger.

"Three hundred and forty," Hesper said.

"Give or take the reclamation losses, which are badly logged."

"And that's what you're weighing."

"That's what Ibis sent me to weigh."

"That isn't what I asked." Hesper unscrewed the thermos, considered the tea, and closed it again without drinking. "Ninety-one spoons in Canteen Two. I told you that at the Sling. You let it go by."

"You were wrong. It's two hundred and ninety-one."

"I know it's two hundred and ninety-one. I counted them myself before you landed, because I wanted to see how long it would take you." Her eyes stayed level. "A plain auditor corrects you on the spot. They can't help it. It itches. You let it ride, so I'd keep talking. You weren't checking spoons. You were checking me."

Piq made herself breathe out slowly enough that her collar badge would have no reason to log anything. *Read the number, then look at the thing,* her father used to say. The thing in front of her was a sixty-year-old engineer who had set a trap with a spoon count and waited a week to spring it.

"Everyone on a station like this is hiding mass," Piq said. "Accordions. Bricks. A cat. I check people because people are where the mass goes. That's the job."

"Then do it." Hesper pushed off the bulkhead, and the motion carried her half a meter up and forward before her boots found the deck again. "Count what you like. But there are things I'd put my body between you and, Auditor, and I am an old woman with a great deal of body." She stopped at the mouth of the corridor. "And there are things on this moon you won't hear with an auditor's ears. You can trust me on that or not."

She left. The fan ticked. Piq stayed where she was until her heart rate came down to a figure she could have signed for.

At the bottom of her tally pad she wrote: *H. knows 340. Did not ask who restated.* She looked at that second line for a long time. An innocent person would ask whose code it was. A guilty one would ask too, to sound innocent. Hesper had asked neither, and Piq had no column for a person who didn't need the answer.

---

When she got back, an invoice from Intake was waiting on her terminal under Fitch's signature block. It billed her 160.68 mk for 0.39 kg of ceremonial protein slab "consumed by unlogged animal" during the wedding, on the grounds that the officiant had exercised "procedural control of the venue." It closed with EVERY KILOGRAM IS SOMEONE'S.

She replied that half the cake had been on Ur, outside JDA intake jurisdiction, and that the animal was not hers. *The animal is nobody's, which is the whole problem with the animal.*

Ninety seconds later, faster than anything else on the station had ever answered her: *Then whose is it?*

*Pending,* she wrote, and closed the screen.

---

She had built the prediction the way she built everything, out of plain arithmetic.

The first gap had started at 1940 on Day 4, the second at 0742 on Day 9, the third at 1942 on Day 13. The first interval was a hundred and eight hours and two minutes, the second a hundred and eight hours flat. Each silence lasted thirty hours and ten minutes, to within a couple of minutes. Projected forward in pencil, the series gave her 0742 on Day 18, give or take two minutes. She drew a box around it.

Day 17 went to honest work: Galloway's tug blanket ledgers, a reweigh on a shipment of air-scrubber cartridges, and two hours on the hydroponic tithe. The tithe turned out to be a tax on lettuce that was owed in lettuce. In the margins she kept checking her box.

Her theory was that the gaps were Hesper's solo coil shifts. Three for three. Thirty hours alone in the Sling's north coil gallery, where an engineer with a hand key and a reason could transmit, or rather not transmit. Piq had been circling the obvious problem for days. A conspirator who went quiet exactly when she was alone and unobserved was behaving backwards. Piq had patched it with an off-shift relay: a second operator somewhere on the station who keyed only while Hesper was off the net, so that the silences were the relay standing down while Hesper took delivery in person, out of sight of any logger. It explained the coincidence, and it explained the silence. It also needed a second person, a hidden courier route and a reason for both. It had too many moving parts and still held together, which is what decent stories do just before they come apart.

The duty roster, which she read under her audit credential with only a slight bending of purpose, put Hesper on the coils again from 0700 on Day 18. Four for four, if it held.

Piq set the receiver to log on its own, ran the antenna lead up the light-pipe, and stuffed the towel back into the vent grille. Then she did one thing she hadn't done before. She wrote a ten-line watchdog that sat on the receiver's buffer and, if ten minutes passed with no pulse, sent a single entry to her pad's calendar over the station's maintenance mesh, disguised as an ordinary reminder. Anyone watching would see an auditor's to-do list. She slept about as well as people sleep before an exam.

---

At 0725 she went to Canteen Two for breakfast. She didn't want breakfast. She wanted to be in a public place with witnesses when the theory held or failed.

Hesper was there.

She was not in the north coil gallery. She sat at the long table under the ventilation return with a tray of reconstituted oats and the thermos, Juno from the pumphouse on her left and a young Sling technician on her right, six or seven others within arm's reach. She ate slowly, the way people in low gravity learn to eat, so the spoon carried the oats instead of throwing them. She saw Piq come in and lifted the spoon a centimeter in greeting. It was one of the two hundred and ninety-one.

The roster said 0700. Piq had read it herself. Either the roster was wrong, or Hesper was about to be very late, or the box on Piq's tally pad was about to be wrong in a way that would cost more than lettuce.

She took her tray to a table by the wall, where she could see both the canteen clock and Hesper, and sat with her pad face-down beside the oats.

At 0731 Marek Saar came in.

He moved through the room easily, nodding at people. The nods came back half a second late and slightly stiff. Everyone knew how to react to the security chief, and nobody had decided how to feel about him. He took a cup of something hot, looked around the room once in a way that seemed to measure it, and sat down across from Piq.

"Auditor."

"Marek."

"You're up early."

"Lettuce tithe. It's owed in kind. I want to watch the weighing."

"I've heard you take lettuce seriously." He let the smile work. His grey jacket was pressed the way spin-born people pressed things, as though gravity would be along to check. "I wanted to ask about your reference masses."

"They're in my quarters, wrapped in certificate paper. They don't like being handled."

"You told Intake they were for calibrating the depot scales. Have they been to the depot?"

"Not yet. The depot scales have bigger problems than calibration."

"I'd like to see them." He drank. "Facilities is bringing a densitometer cart through Ring C on Day 20 for the habitation shielding survey. It does a full sweep, walls, closets and personal storage, every twelve years whether we need it or not. I mentioned your masses to the tech running it. Certified E2 standards, here in Ring C. He said they'd make an excellent check source. Known density, known geometry. He'll sweep them first thing, to zero the cart."

The clock said 0738.

Piq thought about what a densitometer cart would make of 1.3 kilograms of certified reference mass that was mostly a hollow shell around a Lagrange transceiver. An E2 standard is solid stainless steel, 7,950 kilograms per cubic meter all the way through. Hers would light up the tech's screen like a skull with a missing tooth.

"First thing Day 20," she said.

"Oh-eight-hundred. Ring C east, starting at your corridor. He was very pleased. He doesn't often get real standards." Saar turned his cup a quarter turn on the table. "I told him the auditor would certainly want to help. You're so fond of procedure."

"I'll be there with them in my hands."

"Good." He looked past her, toward the long table. "Hesper's in early. She's usually on the coils by now."

"Is she," Piq said.

0742. Hesper scraped the last of the oats into a neat ridge and ate it. Juno said something and the technician laughed. Nobody in the room was keying anything. Piq's pad lay face-down by her tray and did nothing, which was what it was supposed to do for the next ten minutes if the gap had come on time, and what it was supposed to do forever if it hadn't.

Saar talked about the shielding survey and the way the regolith had settled over the north galleries in the last quake. She answered in full sentences. Some part of her was keeping track of what she said, and she trusted that part to be competent.

At 0752 the pad buzzed once against the table.

She turned it over as though it were nothing. *LETTUCE TITHE — WEIGH NOW.*

The receiver in her quarters had heard nothing for ten minutes. The gap had started on schedule, to the minute, with Hesper Okafor-Lund forty meters away, in a lit room with eight witnesses and the head of station security, holding a spoon.

"Duty calls," Piq said.

"Fifty hours," Saar said pleasantly, and lifted his cup to her as she stood. "I'll tell the tech you're looking forward to it."

She walked out of Canteen Two at a pace an auditor late for lettuce would plausibly use. In the corridor she wrote on her tally pad, under the box, *Day 20 0800 — densitometer — Ring C east — masses first,* and drew a second box around it. Then she drew a line through the first.

---

The theory didn't fail gently. It failed the way a rotten strut fails, all at once and along its whole length.

She sat on her bunk with the receiver log open and made herself take it apart in order, the way she would for a client who had been cheated by a model.

One. The gaps did not track Hesper. Hesper had been on the coils during the first three because the roster put her there, and the roster put her there because someone had asked for those shifts. Piq pulled the shift-request history. All three were Hesper's own requests, logged a day or two ahead. Today's was too, for 0700. She had swapped it at 0640 with a technician named Abara, citing "breakfast."

Two. The off-shift relay needed a second operator, and in eleven days of sweeps Piq had never found a second emitter on the station. She had explained the absence with a better hiding place. She was paid well to notice when a model explained missing evidence by assuming better concealment. She had not noticed it in her own.

Three. A cell does not hold a thirty-hour silence to within two minutes across four repetitions. People are late. People swap shifts for breakfast. This was a clock, and nobody on Asgard Yard kept a clock that ran on a hundred and eight hours.

Nobody on Asgard Yard.

She sat very still. Then she heard Teo in the viewing dome on Day 11, both of them pretending not to be aware of the other's shoulder, Jupiter sitting low and banded over the frost of Valhalla:

*Europa ducks behind the old man every four and a half days. The dosimeters sing differently when it does.*

Four and a half days was a hundred and eight hours.

Piq opened the station's navigation almanac. The Sling ran its throw solutions off it, so it carried full ephemerides for the Galilean moons, and an auditor reconciling throw records had every right to read it. She asked for Europa's occultations by Jupiter as seen from Asgard Yard, Days 1 through 20. The almanac returned a tidy column.

She put it beside her own column and began to foot them.

Gap one: silence from 1940 Day 4 to 0150 Day 6, midpoint 1045 Day 5. Occultation: Europa behind Jupiter's limb from 0854 to 1233 Day 5, midpoint 1043.

Two minutes apart.

Gap two: midpoint 2247 Day 9. Occultation midpoint 2246.

Gap three: midpoint 1047 Day 14. Occultation midpoint 1046.

Gap four had begun at 0742 that morning. By the series it would end at 1352 tomorrow, with a midpoint at 2247 tonight. The almanac put Europa's next occultation at 2245.

Four for four, to within the scatter of her own timestamps. There was no Hesper in it. The silences were centered on the minute Europa slid behind Jupiter as seen from this crater.

The occultation itself lasted three hours and thirty-nine minutes. The gaps lasted thirty hours and ten minutes, and she made herself find the difference rather than wave it away. Europa is tidally locked to Jupiter, so anything sitting on its surface turns once per orbit relative to the planet. Relative to the line toward Callisto it turns once per synodic period, a hundred and eight hours, three and a third degrees an hour. Thirty hours of silence meant a hundred degrees of sky the transmitter could not see. The gap was symmetric, the same number of hours on each side of the occultation. So the transmitter wasn't on a hill with a clear horizon. It sat looking up out of a slot, something with walls, a cut in the ice. Callisto rose into that slot, crossed it, and set again. Then the transmitter went dark, and Jupiter's body sat squarely in the middle of the dark.

She pulled up the frequency log next, the one she had shrugged at on Day 16 between the vows and the cake. The carrier on the old maintenance band, nominally 410.250 megahertz, had wandered by about eighteen kilohertz either side of center. She had written *crystal, cheap, thermal* beside it and moved on.

A cheap crystal drifts with temperature. It wanders, it jumps when someone opens a hatch, it settles a little lower every week as it ages. It does not trace a clean sine wave with a hundred-and-eight-hour period.

She fitted one anyway. The amplitude came out at 18.8 kilohertz. Europa's orbital speed is about 13.7 kilometers a second. Divided by the speed of light, that is a fractional shift of 4.58 parts in a hundred thousand, and at 410.25 megahertz it comes to 18,802 hertz. She subtracted Callisto's own motion, a residual of a few hundred hertz. The fit tightened.

The zero crossings fell where the geometry required. The carrier crossed center at the middle of each gap, when Europa was behind Jupiter and moving square across the line of sight. It ran low going into each silence, while the source was still receding around the back of the planet, and high coming out, while it approached on the far limb. Then it sagged back through center fifty-four hours later as Europa crossed in front.

That wasn't hardware drift. It was the source moving, the signal of something that rode on a moon.

She wrote it in one line, because one line was all it needed: *Transmitter on Europa. Surface or near. In a cut. Keying since at least Day 4.*

Then she wrote down what that line cost the rest of the ledger, because a correction you don't carry through is just another error.

Hesper had not been going quiet when she was alone. She had gone to be alone when it went quiet. There was nothing to listen to for those thirty hours, so the old woman took the dead shifts on the coils and spent the live ones somewhere with a receiver. *Things you won't hear with an auditor's ears.* The Brigade wasn't coordinating anything over that band. It was listening to it.

*Throw before conjunction, no one will see it until it's done.* Piq had read it the way Pell had read it, as a launch someone wanted hidden. Water ice going missing, a tug being wrapped in it, all of it timed against the blackout. Seen through a mass auditor's ledger, the ice around the Mule meant theft. Seen through a dose warden's, it would mean shielding. If someone on Europa was calling, the throw was not a weapon. It was a rescue, scheduled to fly while the Compact couldn't watch and couldn't forbid it.

And somewhere in the Mars relay sat Pell's order of Day 11, forty-nine minutes stale when it reached her and staler every hour since: *Identify the throw cabal. Treat the depot crew as hostile.*

Europa's trailing hemisphere took five sieverts a day. A person unshielded on that surface was dead within a day. Under meters of ice, a person might live. Anyone keying a hundred-year-old dosimetry protocol on a band nobody listened to was either a machine left running or someone who had worked out that the only people who might still be listening were the people who had once used it.

There was one person on Asgard Yard who would know which.

---

The dose office sat at the end of Ring A, behind a door with a radiation trefoil that someone had at some point carefully re-stenciled by hand. Teo was at her desk with a column of badge readings, her reading glasses pushed up into her dark hair, and she didn't look up when Piq knocked on the frame.

"Your next consult isn't until Day 21," she said. "Your dose is fine. Your dose is boring. Go and be boring somewhere else."

"I found something in the maintenance spectrum."

That got her attention, though she didn't show it with anything but her pen, which stopped.

"The auditor's sweep includes the RF registry," Piq said. It was true as far as it went. "Every licensed emitter on the station gets reconciled against its band allocation. There's a carrier on the old dosimetry maintenance band, the one retired in eighty-something. It shouldn't be anything. It's somebody's, and I can't find whose. I thought the dose warden might want to know what's using her band before I put it in a report."

Teo took off the glasses and set them on the column of readings. "Nothing uses that band. It was decommissioned before I got my ticket."

"Something does." Piq held out the pad. "Raw log. Eleven days."

For a moment Teo didn't take it. Piq could see her calculating, as she always did, the cost of picking something up. Then she held out her hand, and Piq put the pad in it without letting their fingers touch, the way you hand over a sample.

Teo scrolled. Her face did what a careful face does with unfamiliar data: it went flat and attentive. She paged through the pulse trains, the group lengths, the gaps. Piq had arranged the log so the Europa conclusion was not on the first screen. She told herself it was method, letting the data speak before the theory did. Some of it was cowardice. She didn't know how to tell a woman that her dead might be calling from an ice field, and she wanted the data to say it first.

"These are counts," Teo said slowly. "Grouped. It's badge-dump format, or it's built on it. Field number, then value. That's the old maintenance protocol. They used to key dosimeter readings out of the deep galleries by hand when the wired net went down." She scrolled. "No. That isn't standard. The fields are wrong. Nobody used thirty-six fields. The badges only had..."

She stopped.

Piq watched her stop. It was not dramatic. Teo's hand did not shake and her breath did not catch. Her thumb rested on the edge of the pad, and for the length of three full pulse groups she did not scroll at all.

"Teo," Piq said.

"Six by six," Teo said. Her voice was quite level. "Thirty-six cells. We drew it on the underside of the top bunk in marker, because I was studying for my warden's ticket and she was fourteen and bored and I needed something to do with her that wasn't her climbing the reactor ladder. I taught her badge-dump keying so I could quiz myself. Then she wanted letters, so I made her a grid. Row and column, keyed as counts. Tally fives as long pulses. So you'd only ever need four kinds of mark." She swallowed. "I told her it was a stupid code. Anyone with an afternoon could break it."

"Did anyone else ever use it?"

"No. We used it on the bunk wall. Knocking. After lights-out." Teo turned the pad so Piq could see, and touched the screen at the end of one group. "Look. There."

At the end of the group was a doubled final pulse: two short marks where one would close the frame.

"She does that," Teo said. "Every frame. A little tail. It wastes a pulse. It costs time on the band and power, and it carries no information, and I told her that a hundred times. She said it was her signature. She said a code should know who's talking." Her thumb moved down the screen, and at the end of every frame the same small redundant flourish sat there. "Every frame."

She set the pad on the desk very precisely, parallel to the edge, and then put both hands flat on either side of it, and Piq saw her press down, as if the desk were the only fixed thing in reach. Teo's face was not crumpling. It was just failing to hold its usual shape, in small places at the mouth and around the eyes, like ice taking load and showing it in hairline lines before anything gives.

"Where is this coming from," Teo said. It was not a question so much as an order.

Piq told her. She went through the occultations, the four midpoints within two minutes, the hundred degrees of sky, the eighteen-point-eight kilohertz. She said it all in the flattest voice she had, because she understood that what Teo needed from her now was arithmetic, a ledger she could stand on, and not comfort. Teo listened without moving. When Piq said *Europa*, Teo closed her eyes, and kept them closed until Piq said *in a cut, under ice*, and then opened them.

"The ruling said automated echo," Teo said. "Saar signed it. The pulses after the loss were an echo off the debris field. A beacon with nobody behind it." She looked at the doubled pulse. "Beacons don't do that."

"No."

"I need to log this." She reached for her own terminal, then stopped with her hand above it. "No. I can't log this." She laughed once, a sound with no humor in it at all. "I've logged every microsievert on this station for nine years and I don't know which form this goes on."

"It goes on mine for tonight," Piq said. "We decode it first. Then you decide what's true enough to write down."

Teo looked at her then, really looked, as if Piq had become a quantity she would have to measure later and could not yet. Piq held still under it. She thought about how much of what she had just said was true and how much was arranged. The arithmetic was all true. The reason she had been sweeping that band was not.

"All right," Teo said. "Sit down. Don't touch anything I haven't calibrated."

---

They worked through the night under the office's single task lamp, the door locked and the ventilation turned up to cover their voices. Teo had the grid. She drew it from memory on a sheet of dose-report flimsy, six rows by six columns, and her hand did not hesitate over a single cell. Piq had the timing.

The first problem was framing. The pulses arrived Doppler-stretched, so their spacing breathed in and out across the hundred-and-eight-hour cycle and the boundaries between groups would not sit still. Piq corrected every timestamp against the fitted velocity curve, then rebuilt the frame clock from the corrected series. After that the doubled tails lined up like fence posts, and they could see where every frame began and ended.

The second problem was noise. Jupiter is loud on most of the radio spectrum, and the log was full of crackle and false pulses where the planet's own bursts had stepped on the band. Teo knew the badge-dump format well enough to reject impossible values, counts no field could hold. Piq marked the surviving pulses by confidence. They took only frames that appeared at least three times, because whoever was keying repeated, again and again, the same short message, the way you would if you did not know whether anyone had heard the first thousand times.

Somewhere past 0200, Piq saw the second thing.

It sat underneath the pulses, not in them: a slow ripple in the carrier's amplitude, a fraction of a decibel, too faint to show unless she stacked a hundred frames and averaged. It had a period of about two point seven seconds, and the period was not steady. It stretched and shortened over hours with no relationship she could find to the pulse clock or to the Doppler curve.

"Teo."

Teo leaned in. Their shoulders were nearly touching. Neither of them moved closer or away.

"That isn't keying," Teo said after a while.

"No. It's under the keying. It's on the carrier itself."

"Interference."

"From what?" Piq ran it. Jupiter's decametric bursts are tied to Io's position, and Io was nowhere near the right phase on any of the days. Mechanical coupling from the reactor's coolant pumps was a candidate, but pump vibration would be steady or harmonic, and this was neither. Multipath off the walls of the cut would track the geometry, and this didn't. She tried it as a second channel on Teo's grid, row and column from the peaks. It gave nonsense. She tried it as pulse-position code, and that gave more nonsense. She tried the ratios between successive peaks, in case it was counting something. The ratios were not integers and did not repeat.

"Did she know any other scheme?" Piq asked. "Anything you might not have taught her?"

"She knew a dozen. She was an engineer." Teo looked at the trace for a long time. "She didn't put that there. Her hand is in the pulses. That isn't a hand."

Piq opened a new column in her pad and couldn't think what to call it. She left the header blank and logged the period, the depth and the drift. Then she put it aside, the way you set aside a discrepancy that is real and too small to chase tonight, and knew she would come back to it.

They went back to the pulses.

By 0400 they had letters. They came out of the grid slowly, cell by cell, Teo reading row and column aloud and Piq checking each against the repeat frames. Teo's voice stayed even, except once, when a frame resolved into the letter A and then the letter A again, and she stopped and said nothing for a while, and Piq waited.

There was a number in the second frame, keyed in badge-dump digits instead of letters because digits were cheaper. Piq checked it four times across four separate repeats. Teo checked it against the sixth. Neither of them said anything when it agreed.

At 0517, with the lamp hot and the flimsy covered edge to edge in Teo's grid and Piq's corrections, the frames came together into a message that had been going out from the ice every hour Europa could see Callisto, for at least fourteen days, to a band nobody used, in a code only one living person could read.

Teo wrote it out in block capitals at the bottom of the sheet. Then she put the pen down and laid her palm flat beside the words, and Piq read them over her shoulder.

FOUR ALIVE. REACTOR DRUM 60 DAYS. PLEASE.

## Chapter 8: Countersignature

At 0544 on Day 19 the light-pipe in Piq's cell was still running its night cycle, a weak bluish wash meant to stand in for a sky. She sat on the bunk with the tally sheets across her knees and could not make herself lie down.

She had written FOUR ALIVE at the top of a clean sheet. Then she had written it again lower down, because the first copy was in Teo's block capitals and she wanted one in her own hand. Under it she had drawn a column for the drum: 60, an arrow, and the square her father used to draw for *later*. Below that sat the blank-headed column for the ripple, 2.7 seconds and drifting, with nothing to put at the top.

The report came first. Everything else depended on it.

She opened the kit with a quarter-turn and a reverse quarter-turn. The cylinder came apart in her hands with its usual small reluctance, like a jar nobody had opened in years. She set the burst transceiver on the blanket and plugged in the pad.

*SL-2214-0927. VARGA TO PELL. PRIORITY: FLASH.*

She looked at the word FLASH for a while. Special Losses kept it for weapons, deaths and solvency events. This qualified on two counts, perhaps three. She left it.

*0874 AND 0911 SUPERSEDED IN FULL. NO THROW CABAL. REPEAT NO CABAL. PULSE SOURCE IS EUROPA SURFACE OR NEAR SURFACE. METHOD: GAP MIDPOINTS MATCH EUROPA OCCULTATION BY JUPITER AS SEEN FROM ASGARD WITHIN 2 MIN OVER FOUR CYCLES (TABLE A). CARRIER DRIFT IS DOPPLER, ±18.8 KHZ ON 108 H PERIOD, CONSISTENT WITH EUROPA ORBITAL VELOCITY (TABLE B). KEYING DECODED. CONTENT: "FOUR ALIVE. REACTOR DRUM 60 DAYS. PLEASE." ATTRIBUTED TO AMAYA ANAND, ENGINEER, NINEFOLD GRACE, DECLARED LOST 14 MONTHS. ATTRIBUTION BASIS: PRIVATE KEYING SCHEME AND OPERATOR SIGNATURE (TABLE C). REQUEST: SUSPEND STANDING ORDER RE HOSTILE DEPOT CREW. REQUEST: GUIDANCE RE SALVAGE AND RESCUE. CREW ALIVE. CREW ALIVE.*

She deleted the second CREW ALIVE, which read like panic, and then put it back, because it was accurate and she wanted it to be the last thing he saw. She attached the tables, the raw pulse logs and the spectrum stacks. She did not attach the ripple. She had no idea yet what it was, and she was not going to send her handler a question mark labelled as data.

The draft came to 3.1 megabytes. Her notes on the Deimos relay were clear about what a 3 MB packet looked like from an auditor's room on Callisto: something worth reading. So she did what she had done every time. She wrapped it in forty-four megabytes of Ibis audit upload: scale certificates, depot tare tables, nine years of scrubber cartridge reweighs, and the hydroponic tithe ledger in full, lettuce by lettuce. Bulk was dull, and dull was safe.

The transceiver took the packet and spat it at the sky in under a second, buried in the station's outbound noise. The queue display updated.

*0874 — DELIVERED.*
*0911 — QUEUED (DEIMOS).*
*0927 — QUEUED (DEIMOS).*

She glanced at the two QUEUED lines and let them go. Conjunction was nineteen days off, on Day 38, the date she had been carrying since the shuttle. The relay was triaging, and Lisk had told her it would only get worse as the window narrowed. She was paying for her disguise by waiting, which was the deal she had made. Pell would get both reports together, the wrong one and its correction stacked in order, and he would read them the way he read everything, line by line with a pencil held like a scalpel.

She had the cylinder halfway closed when the receiver chimed with an inbound message.

*SL-2214-0391/R7. PELL TO VARGA. COMPOSED L4 D14 2210 UTC-ADJ. PRIORITY: IMMEDIATE.*

It was five days old. She opened it with a feeling she did not want to name. It was close to the feeling of seeing your own name in a newspaper.

*RE 0874. UR STATION HAS BOUND WAR-RISK COVER OVER JDA-CLAIMED EUROPA EXCLUSION ZONE. PLACED VIA PHOBOS SYNDICATE, LEAD LINE OUR OWN WAR-RISK DESK, 34% SHARE. LIMIT 2.1 BN MK. TERM: THROUGH CONJUNCTION PLUS 60. INFERENCE: UR ANTICIPATES HOSTILE ACT IN ZONE. COMPACT COUNSEL ADVISES ANY INCURSION INTO ZONE MAY BE RULED ACT OF WAR UNDER ARTICLE 11. EXPOSURE TO SOCIETY ON BOTH SIDES. YOUR PRIORITY: PREVENT ANY UNLICENSED LAUNCH FROM ASGARD. ANY MEANS. CORROBORATED. — P.*

She read it three times, and then once more with a finger under each line, the way her father had read a cargo manifest he already suspected.

*Our own war-risk desk.* So Lagrange Mutual had written cover on the war it was afraid of. One floor at L4 had sold Ur a policy against something bad happening over Europa, while another floor sent Piq to make sure nothing happened. Whoever insures the ships knows where they all are. She had always thought of that sentence as a description of power. It was also a description of a conflict of interest, and she had simply never had to stand in the middle of one.

*Any means.* In Pell's compression those two words were not a figure of speech. He used them the way an actuary uses *total loss*, with precision and no feeling. He was not ordering her to kill anyone. Pell never ordered anyone to do anything. He removed limits and left the arithmetic to his adjusters.

The order was five days old. It answered 0874, the report in which she had confidently described a cabal of depot workers rotating shifts around a hidden emitter, and in which she had called the gaps a roster. She had written that paragraph and been proud of it. Pell was replying to the woman she had been on Day 13.

Fine. He would have 0927 within days. He would read it, the order would lapse, and she would get guidance on salvage. She put the square beside *ANY MEANS* on her tally sheet. Then she was annoyed with herself for drawing it, because the square meant *I will look at this again*, and she did not want to look at those two words again.

She pulled up the briefing package instead. The background annex had come down with her and she had skimmed past it, because it held old losses in the Jovian book: three tugs, a slug carrier and a survey drone. The Ninefold Grace was the second entry. Claim LM-C-2213-1146.

She had not read it on the shuttle because she had been looking for a weapon and the Grace was a dead tug. Now she read it as an auditor, and that meant reading the manifest first, before the conclusions.

*Vessel: Ninefold Grace, Class IV tug, Asgard registry. Crew 6. Mission: survey support, Europa trailing hemisphere, Conamara sector, JDA permit 2213-E-041. Filed trajectory: Hohmann-plus fast transfer, 3.9 d. Propellant: water, 61.4 t at departure. Shielding: 18.0 t water blanket, compliant with Clause 14(c), certified by Lagrange surveyor at Asgard.*

She ticked it. It was the one line in the file that was plainly in order: the right figure, the right surveyor, the right clause. She remembered reciting that clause in Galloway's office with the Paper Lantern's crew rolling their eyes. It was minimum shielding water scaled to transit time, and it was the reason any Europa crew came home with their marrow intact. She moved on.

*Actual trajectory (from Asgard tracking and final telemetry): deviation from filed path at T+4.2 h to a low-energy transfer of approx. 9.6 d. Vessel entered Europa plasma torus region on extended residence. Telemetry terminated at T+9.1 d. Debris field located on surface by JDA overflight, Conamara sector. No survivors detected. Beacon activity subsequently ruled automated echo (JDA-SEC ruling 2213-77, M. Saar).*

*Determination: Loss attributable to unauthorized navigational deviation by master to a low-energy trajectory outside filed flight plan. Deviation constitutes pilot error under Exclusion 9(b). Final dose log anomaly reviewed and classed terminal artifact. Claim denied. Beneficiary notified: T. Anand (next of kin, A. Anand).*

Teo's name was there in the same plain type as the tonnage. Piq caught herself looking at it the way she might look at a scratch on a weight.

*Unauthorized deviation.* Why would a master take a six-person tug onto a nine-day crawl through the worst radiation in the Jovian system when he had filed for four days and carried shielding for four? Pilots didn't do that for fun. They did it when the numbers forced them. Somewhere in the file there should be a reason, and the adjusters had not written one. They had written *pilot error* and stopped. Her father would have said the equals sign was where you stop looking.

She drew a square next to *deviation*. Her sheet was filling with squares.

Then, mostly to stop staring at Teo's name, she opened the original intercept. She had read the line itself forty times. She had never read the envelope.

*FRAGMENT — HALLORAN CLEARANCE (CERES) LOT 2214-0417. SOURCE CLASS: COMMERCIAL INTERCEPT, JOVIAN, BAND UNSPECIFIED. CODEC: UR-STD VB4 (DECODED BY HALLORAN). CONTENT: "throw before conjunction, no one will see it until it's done."*

UR-STD VB4 was Ganymede's voice-and-burst standard, the one Ur's tankers used for traffic they were content to have overheard. Asgard's JDA traffic ran on Compact codecs, and so did the Brigade's, if the Brigade ever transmitted at all. Lagrange used its own. If the line had been one Asgard worker speaking to another, it should have been carried in a Callisto codec.

Unless someone on Ur had been talking about Asgard. Or someone had taken an Asgard line and re-encoded it before selling it on. Or someone had wanted it found, and wanted it to look like Ganymede.

She gave this one a question mark rather than a square. Her father, Gustav, had always said the question mark was the most honest symbol on a ledger. She put the cylinder back together, slid it into the case with the four true masses, and lay down at last at 0722. She watched the light-pipe begin to go slowly gold, and somewhere in the middle of deciding she would not sleep, she slept. It was the flat, dreamless sleep of a body that had been awake for thirty hours. When she woke the pipe had run through its whole day and was going amber toward evening, and someone was at the door.

---

The knock came at 1900, and it was not really a knock. It was a single flat-palmed strike at door height, the way a person announces themselves when they want to give no impression of asking.

Teo stood in the corridor in a clean jacket, her hair tied back with the grey cable insulation, reading glasses folded into the breast pocket. She held a slate pressed against her chest, screen inward.

"I've been to Hesper's cell," she said. "She wasn't in it."

"She's on the coils tonight?"

"No." Teo's eyes went down the corridor and came back. "She's in L6. Where you got eighty-one point six microsieverts the week before last that you told me came from a calibration rig."

Piq said nothing. Any answer would have been a second lie stacked on the first, and Teo would have weighed it.

"I'm not asking," Teo said. "I'm telling you where we're going. I want you there because you counted the gaps. I couldn't have. I've stood under that dome for nine years telling every new arrival that the badges sing differently when Europa ducks behind the old man. I turned it into a story. A bedtime story for the night shift. I never once wrote down *when*." She turned. "Bring the tables."

They walked. Piq had learned the long, slow stride the moon demanded, a push that carried you a meter and a half while you waited for the floor. Teo walked like a native, low and flat, never giving the ceiling a chance. In Ring B she said, without turning her head, "Juno. Abara. Rafe. Achterberg on the hydroponic line, I'd bet a month's tea. I've had their badge spectra on my desk for half a year with that 2.223 line in them, and I logged it every time as *source unattributed*." A pause. "That isn't lying. It's an accurate description of a source I declined to attribute."

"I know the form," Piq said.

Teo glanced at her, then away.

The service tunnel to L6 smelled of cold regolith and the ozone tang of the electrolysis plant leaking through a seal somebody should have reseated. The hangar door had a hand-lettered sign: *AUTHORIZED PERSONNEL ONLY (YOU KNOW WHO YOU ARE).* Teo did not knock this time. She keyed the dose warden's override, which opened every pressure door on Asgard, and the door slid back on light and noise.

Eleven people stopped working. Piq counted them before she could stop herself, the way she counted everything. Hesper was at the reactor end with a torque wrench, sleeves rolled to the elbow. Lucky was on a scaffold, his hand flat on the Mule's hull as if feeling for a pulse. Juno was halfway through lowering a stockpile block on a hoist, and the block hung turning slowly, about four hundred and sixty kilograms of hand-cut ice glittering under the work lamps.

Nobody spoke. In 0.126 g a dropped shim takes nearly two seconds to reach the floor, and someone had dropped one. They all watched it fall.

"Teodora," Hesper said.

"Hesper."

"I suppose you've brought a form."

"I've brought a message." Teo turned the slate around and held it up so the work lamps caught it. She had copied it out by hand and photographed it, Piq saw, rather than typing it. That was her block capitals, and the smudge where her palm had rested.

FOUR ALIVE. REACTOR DRUM 60 DAYS. PLEASE.

Lucky came off the scaffold without using the ladder. It was a three-meter drop, which on Callisto was an act of impatience rather than courage. He landed in a crouch and was at Teo's elbow before the shim hit the floor.

"Oh," he said. "Oh, oh, oh. That's her? That's the clicks? You *read* the clicks?" He reached for the slate, stopped himself, and knocked three times on the nearest crate instead. "We've had a man on the coils every hour Europa's up for five months, and we couldn't read a word. Juno thought it was Rhee doing the old Compact distress code wrong. I thought it was a prayer. Rafe thought it was a song. I told him a song would be shorter."

"It's not a distress code," Teo said. "It's a dose dump. A bunk-wall cipher I made up when I was nineteen and she was fourteen. Nobody else ever knew it." Her voice stayed level, and that took some effort. Piq could hear what it cost. "Five months, Hesper."

Hesper put the wrench down on its padded tray so it wouldn't skate. "Five months and a bit."

"You heard my sister's hand for five months and you didn't come to my office."

"I heard a hand. I didn't know whose." Hesper wiped her palms on her thighs. "I knew it wasn't an echo. Echoes don't stop for the weekends. I knew it was coming from Europa, because I'm not a fool and I can read an ephemeris. I knew Saar had signed it dead. What I didn't know, Teo, was what you'd do with it."

"I'd have *read* it."

"You'd have logged it." Hesper's voice was gentle, and the gentleness was its own blow. "Then you'd have come down here and measured every one of these children, and you'd have grounded the lot of them by the end of the week, because every rule you've got says this tug is a coffin and every person who works on it is over budget. And you'd have been right. I've watched you hold this station's dose for nine years. I've watched you hold your own grief by the same method. I wasn't going to hand you a voice you couldn't answer and then ask you to sign us all off it."

Nobody in the hangar moved. Juno's block turned on its hoist, catching the light and letting it go.

Teo's jaw worked. "That wasn't your choice."

"No," Hesper said. "Nothing about this has been anybody's choice." She looked past Teo at Piq. "And you've brought the auditor."

"She counted the gaps," Teo said. "The silences line up with Europa going behind Jupiter, to the minute. The drift is Doppler. She worked it out alone, from a band she found during a registry sweep, before I ever saw it. I didn't know my sister's hand was in the sky until she put it in front of me."

Hesper's eyes rested on Piq for a long moment. She had the look of an old weigh-master checking a scale she had once caught reading light.

"The spoons," Hesper said.

"Two hundred and ninety-one," said Piq.

"And you let ninety-one stand."

"I wanted to know what you'd do with a wrong number I didn't correct."

"So did I." Something moved at the corner of Hesper's mouth. It wasn't a smile, but it was the place a smile would go. "Show me the tables."

Piq laid them out on a crate stencilled *MASS 0.64 T — FRAME — DO NOT STACK*. Table A gave gap midpoints against occultation midpoints: four cycles, residuals under two minutes. Table B was the Doppler fit, and Table C showed the keying grid. She talked them through it in the order she had once used on Teo's screens, data first and theory after. Partly that was method. Partly it was so that nobody had to watch her draw a conclusion before they could draw it themselves. The hangar crew pressed in around her. Somebody's breath was warm on her ear. Juno smelled of cutting fluid. Lucky narrated everything a beat behind her, like the wedding link's delay: "Gap. Gap. Moon goes behind the old man. Oh, that's *lovely*. That's the moon doing the math for you."

When she finished, Juno said quietly, "We've had those gaps on the wall for months." She pointed. There was a chart of hand-ruled columns, the silences shaded in pencil. "We just called them *Europa's down*. We never thought to work out the midpoints. We knew where it was. We didn't need to prove it."

"You didn't need to," Piq said. "I did."

"Why?" said a man near the back. He was broad, with burn scars across the backs of both hands, and he had to be Rafe. "Who's she proving it to? Auditors report to someone."

Teo started to speak. Piq got there first. It had to be her.

"Ibis Metrology," she said. "A certifying office on Ceres. They care whether my totals foot. I won't sign the depot reconciliation at a number I can't stand behind, and right now there's three hundred and forty tonnes I can't stand behind." She looked at the ice blocks on their pallets. "I don't think I'll be putting this in the Ibis upload."

That was true, every word of it, and it was easy. Too easy. She noticed how it felt leaving her mouth: smooth, like a mass with no burr on it. She noticed that Teo, beside her, relaxed by a degree at the word *Ceres*, because Ceres was nobody's enemy. She noticed that she disliked herself for noticing. She entered the omission in a column she did not show anyone.

Lucky slapped the crate. "She counts. I said she counted. At the wedding, I said, that's a woman who counts. She ran my vows like a cargo handover. Over. *Over.*" He turned to Hesper. "Boss, she's the auditor who can count. We haven't had one of those. Every bit of arithmetic in this hangar is you and a slate at two in the morning, and you add like a poet."
