"I add like an engineer," Hesper said. "Which is to say I round in the direction that keeps the thing from breaking." She put a finger on Table A, then lifted it. "All right. The auditor who can count. Teo. What does sixty days mean?"

Teo had been waiting for that question. Piq could see it in her shoulders.

"It means her reactor drum had sixty days left when she started keying that message," Teo said. "I don't know when she started. Piq's logs go back to Day 4. Your logs go back five months." She looked at the receiver bolted to the hangar wall, an ugly beige box with a hand-wound coil feeding up through the roof. "If I can get clean frames out of your archive, I can find the first time she keyed sixty. That gives us an upper bound."

"It's all yours," Hesper said. "Juno, give her the archive."

It took Teo forty minutes at a crate table with her reading glasses on, typing with two fingers. Piq sat beside her and rejected false pulses, Jovian noise spikes she could spot by eye now. Nobody in the hangar went back to work. Lucky did, briefly, then came down to watch again. Someone made tea. Someone else put a wrapped protein bar at Piq's elbow without a word, and that was the closest she came, for a while, to being unable to speak.

"Here," Teo said at last. Her pencil had stopped on a timestamp. "Clean frame, sixty. Twenty-five days before today."

"And before that?"

"The archive's too noisy before that. She might have been sending sixty for months, or she might have been sending something else." Teo took the glasses off and put them on the crate very precisely. "So we have thirty-five days. At most. It could be less."

Lucky stopped talking. Nobody else in the hangar had been talking anyway.

"Blackout's in nineteen," Hesper said. "Day thirty-eight, the relay goes dark, and it stays dark for nineteen more."

Piq ran it without meaning to. Nineteen days to conjunction. Thirty-five, at most, on the drum. The drum ran out sixteen days into the silence.

"Then the rescue flies inside the blackout," Teo said, "and it flies with every kilogram of shielding this hangar can cut, and every one of you is coming to my office tomorrow to have your marrow counted. I am not doing this so you can kill yourselves on my watch." She stood. "That's my price for not having been asked. And Hesper, if you say *martyr* to me I'll put you on a dose plan so strict you'll be breathing through a straw."

"I wasn't going to say it."

"You were thinking it."

"I'm always thinking it," Hesper said. "It's my resting state."

Piq gathered the tables. At the door Hesper caught her sleeve. Her grip was strong.

"Tomorrow," Hesper said quietly. "Saar's densitometer cart is in Ring C east. Juno saw it come out of stores. Whatever you've got that you're carrying in your hands—"

"Reference masses."

"—I hope they're as solid as you are," Hesper said, and let go.

---

The cart arrived at 0758 on Day 20. It was a squat orange machine on four bounce-damped wheels with a mast of gamma detectors and, in a lead-tungsten pig on its back, the source: a cesium check pellet sealed and tagged, with JDA STORES 2201 stencilled in letters faded nearly to a guess. Behind it came the tech, a thin man named Oyelaran who wore earplugs although nothing made a sound. Behind him came Marek Saar in a pressed grey jacket, smiling at the corridor as though he had tidied it himself.

Behind Saar, against every probability Piq had calculated, came Fitch from mass intake, with a clipboard.

"Any undeclared mass found by an instrument survey," Fitch explained before anyone asked, "falls under intake jurisdiction until it's established whose it is. It's in the statute. I don't make the statutes. I'm just the only one who reads them."

"Mr. Fitch has been very thorough," Saar said. "We've done forty-one cells since six. He's found a great deal of tea."

"Undeclared tea," said Fitch, with feeling.

Saar turned the smile on Piq. "A station-wide densitometric survey, Auditor. Three hundred and forty tonnes of water ice are missing from a depot you were sent to reconcile. I thought you'd approve. I'm doing your job with better equipment."

"I approve of instruments," Piq said. "I'd want to see the calibration certificate."

Oyelaran held it up without being asked, as though people asked him often. It was current. She read the whole thing anyway, top to bottom, because Saar was watching to see whether she would, and because reading it slowly bought her eleven seconds in which nobody went near the case under her bunk.

"Go ahead," she said, and stood aside.

The cart rolled in on its damped wheels. The mast swept the bunk, the shelf, the wall cavity and the floor, and the display on its back drew her room in false colour: the blue of blanket and foam, the green of the water bladder, the hard white blots of anything dense. Five white blots sat in a neat row under the bunk.

"Reference masses," Piq said. "Five."

Saar looked at the display. "Four," he said, pleasantly. "And one that's somewhat less dense than its friends."

Piq felt the room go still around her the way a balance goes still before it reads. She kept her hands where they were.

Fitch was already turning pages.

"Day 1," he said. "Intake. Audit reference masses, five, Ibis Metrology, certified class F2, hollow-core sealed adjustment weight, one. Weighed by me." He tapped the line. "I remember. She argued about it. Thirteen hundred grams over allowance and she argued that a scale's own reference weights can't count against the scale's operator, which is in the statute, sub-paragraph six. I hadn't read sub-paragraph six in eleven years. She had." He said it in the tone of a man paying an unwilling compliment to a rival. "Declared, weighed, sealed. The hollow one weighs exactly what I wrote down. Density's not mass. Mass is my department."

"Adjusting cavity," Piq said. "Every precision weight has one. You trim it with shot and seal it. If anyone breaks that seal the calibration's void, and I'd have to recertify every scale on Asgard against nothing. The depot reconciliation would be pushed back past conjunction."

"Past conjunction," Saar repeated, as though it were a vintage.

"And declared mass under intake seal," Fitch added, "can't be resurveyed by anybody except intake, and only on evidence of alteration. The seal's intact." He bent and looked at it, which no one had asked him to do either. "Intact. I'm satisfied. I'm sorry, Chief, it's my department."

Saar regarded the five white blots for a moment longer. Then he lost interest in them. Piq saw it happen. It was not tact and it was not defeat. He had simply never come for the weights.

"Your weights are your own, Auditor," he said. "I wouldn't dream of voiding them."

The cart gave a soft, apologetic chirp.

Oyelaran took out one earplug. Everyone looked at the display. The mast had tipped back on its own sweep, and in the overhead duct above the door, where the air came in warm from Ring C, a small irregular blot glowed the green of water and meat.

*ANOMALY. 4.1 KG. UNDECLARED.*

The blot moved.

Fitch made a sound Piq had previously heard only from tug pilots on a good burn. "That's mine," he said. "That's intake jurisdiction. Undeclared, uncertified, ambulatory." The blot moved again, unhurried, toward the junction. "Oyelaran, I need a ladder. Chief, I need the corridor. Nobody touch that duct."

"Mr. Fitch," Saar said.

"Four point one," said Fitch, already backing into the corridor, eyes on the grille. "Nine months I've had a four-point-one in the scrubber reweigh residuals and nobody believed me."

A grey-striped tail showed briefly in the slats, as if to settle the question, and withdrew. Somewhere down the duct came the faint, unmistakable thump of four paws landing in low gravity, a long way from where they had started.

Saar watched Fitch go. Then he took a slate from his jacket and touched it once, and the corridor display beside Piq's door, which normally ran the dose forecast and the hydroponic rota, went white and filled with type.

*JDA SECURITY DIRECTIVE 2214-31. EFFECTIVE 0900 D20. PENDING RECONCILIATION OF DEPOT MASS DISCREPANCY, ALL MOVEMENT OF WATER ICE ON ASGARD YARD, IN ANY FORM AND OF ANY QUANTITY, INCLUDING INTRA-DEPOT TRANSFER, SLUG SHELLING, HYDROPONIC TITHE AND STOCKPILE CUTTING, REQUIRES COUNTERSIGNATURE BY JDA-SEC IN ADDITION TO EXISTING AUTHORIZATIONS. UNCOUNTERSIGNED MASS WILL BE HELD AT POINT OF ORIGIN. — M. SAAR, HEAD OF SECURITY.*

"You can't do a reconciliation while the mass is moving," Saar said. "I'm sure you'd agree. I'm freezing the ledger for you."

"Every kilo," Piq said. "That includes the shower allocation."

"I've delegated the showers." He put the slate away. "Thank you for your cooperation, Auditor. I'll let Mr. Fitch have his anomaly."

He went down the corridor after the cart, not hurrying, past the display, which kept the directive up and pushed the dose forecast into a narrow strip at the bottom where nobody would read it.

Piq read the directive twice. Then she went to Ring C.

The freight lift at the east end of the ring was the only one wide enough for a stockpile block, and it ran from the depot level up to the service tunnels that led, eventually, to L6. The lift doors stood open at 0911. A pallet sat inside, carrying a block of hand-cut ice the size of a coffin, sweating faintly in the warmer air of the ring. A red tag had been wired to its corner. A JDA guard Piq didn't know stood beside it with his thumbs in his belt.

Juno stood in front of the guard. She had a requisition slate in one hand, signed in the right places by the right people, and she was holding it out to him in the way you'd hold out a hand to someone you weren't sure would take it.

"It's the hydroponic tithe," Juno said. "It's been the hydroponic tithe every Day 20 for six years. Ask Achterberg."

"Needs countersignature," the guard said.

"From whom?"

"JDA-SEC."

"Which is who?"

The guard thought about it. "Chief Saar."

"And where's Chief Saar?"

"Countersigning," said the guard, without irony.

Piq looked at the tag. *HELD AT POINT OF ORIGIN. PENDING JDA-SEC.* There was a box for a signature and a box for a time, and both were empty. The block's mass was stencilled on the side in Juno's hand, 0.46 T. Piq knew where it was going, and it was not the lettuce. Under the block, already, the pallet had started to collect a thin film of meltwater, which in 0.126 g did not run off but beaded and climbed the pallet edge, as if looking for some way out.

Juno saw her. Her face did not change, but she lowered the slate.

"That's one block," Juno said quietly, when the guard had turned to answer his radio. "Hesper wanted forty more cut before the window. She's got the saws running down there right now. Every one of them has to come up this lift."

"And none of them is going to get a signature."

"He's not even going to say no," Juno said. "He'll just never get to it."

Piq took out her pencil. On the back of the tally sheet folded in her jacket she drew a new column and headed it with the directive number, and under it she wrote what she knew: forty blocks short, one held, and no signatures. Below that went the other numbers, the ones that did not move whatever Saar signed. It was Day 20. Conjunction was eighteen days off. The drum had thirty-four days at most.

The lift chimed, the way lifts do when they have been held open too long, and the doors began to close on the block with the red tag and nobody's name on it.

## Chapter 9: Point of Origin

The block was still in the lift at 1040.

Piq passed the Ring C east freight lift on her way down to L6 and slowed. She told herself it was the way any auditor slows for an open discrepancy. The lift's cage doors stood folded back. Juno's block sat inside on its pallet: 0.46 tonnes of hand-cut stockpile ice with a red tag wired to the strapping. The tag's signature box and time box were still empty. The JDA guard from 0911 was still there too, and he had found himself a job.

He was crouched at the pallet's edge holding a graduated sample cup under the low corner, where the meltwater collected. A tablet lay balanced on his knee. As she watched, a drop swelled on the ice, considered the 0.126 g for a while, and let go. It fell with the leisure of something that had nowhere to be and landed in the cup without a sound. The guard tapped the tablet.

"How many?" Piq asked.

He looked up. He was young. His collar badge said KOSKINEN, and his face said he had been told nothing useful in his life. "Two hundred and six," he said. "Millilitres, I mean. I'm logging by drop count and checking against the graduations every fifty."

"Why?"

"Directive says uncountersigned mass is held at point of origin." He nodded at the cup with real misery. "If it drips off the pallet, it's moving. If it's moving, I need a countersignature for it. I haven't got one for the block, so I definitely haven't got one for the drips." He paused. "I asked if I could put it back on the block. They said that's also a movement."

Piq looked at the cup, the ice, and the young man's hands. They were steady on the tablet and would have been more use doing almost anything else.

"Log the cup's tare," she said. "Separately. Before it gets wet. Otherwise you'll book the cup as water when someone finally signs."

His face cleared with gratitude. She went on down the tunnel before he could ask what she was doing at the bottom of Ring C on a Tuesday. Behind her she heard him start the drop count over.

---

The ozone smell from the bad seal met her at the L6 service door, and so did the noise. She had expected silence after this morning: the Brigade on upturned crates with nothing to cut and nowhere to put it.

Instead the saws were running.

They weren't cutting stockpile. Hesper had two of the big wire saws set up on the hangar floor itself, three metres off the Mule's port skid. The floor was Callisto: the ancient dirty ice-and-rock crust of the Valhalla basin, sintered under a skim of regolith concrete. Rafe and Abara were lifting out a block with a pry bar and a lot of swearing, and the block came up dark. It was brownish grey and streaked like old marble, with a rind of glittering frost where the cut face met the hangar air.

"Point of origin," Hesper said when the saw spun down. Her voice was perfectly flat. She pointed at the new hole in the floor, then at the Mule. "Eleven metres. I'm going to hold it so hard."

Piq crouched at the edge of the grid. The cut face gave off cold like a held breath. Fine dark sand was locked into the ice, with here and there a pebble the size of a fingernail.

"It's dirty."

"It's crust. Forty to sixty percent silicate by mass in this layer. We can't drink it and we can't burn it. You don't need to do either with a shielding block. You need to put mass between a body and the sky."

"It's worse mass," Piq said. She was reaching for her stylus before she knew it. "Per kilogram, water beats it. Hydrogen is what breaks up the heavy nuclei and slows the neutrons. Silicon and oxygen just shatter and throw secondaries at you. This stops more per metre because it's denser, so it fits on the hull. But to match a tonne of clean water you'll need more than a tonne of this. And every extra tonne is Lucky's delta-v." She looked up. "We'd be using it for one reason. It's the only mass on Callisto nobody has to countersign."

"Yes," Hesper said.

"And I don't know what it weighs. Your stockpile blocks are 0.46 because the stockpile is clean ice and you cut them to a half-cubic-metre template. This will run one point two five to one point five five grams per cc, depending on where the saw went. If Lucky straps forty of these on that hull, you could be off by three tonnes either side."

Hesper smiled for the first time that morning. It was a small smile, and she meant all of it. "I was hoping you'd say that. I don't have an auditor."

Piq felt the weigh-master's daughter in her straighten up and roll up its sleeves.

"I need a bucket of known volume," she said. "And something I can hang a block on."

---

By 1400 she had a gantry crane, a load cell borrowed from the Sling yard, a sample core from every ninth block, and a table on the back of a hangar requisition that she kept turning over to find more room. Each template block cut half a cubic metre. They came out between 0.62 and 0.81 tonnes, and the heaviest had been sawn next to a buried boulder. None of them was booked anywhere, because legally each one was the floor.

Lucky followed her from block to block with the frank interest of a man watching a magic trick he intended to learn.

"So it's a floor."

"It's a floor." She chalked 0.71 on a face and initialled it.

"Until I bolt it on."

"Then it's a floor attached to your tug. The directive governs movement. The floor hasn't moved. It's been reorganised."

He knocked three times on the block. "I'm putting that on my headstone."

"Do it after Teo's finished with you," Hesper said from the saw. "Marrow counts at fifteen hundred. All of you."

---

The blue door at the end of Ring A had a queue.

Eight Brigade members sat along the corridor wall on the floor. Achterberg was at the end of the line, a lanky man with a beard like steel wool, holding a paper cup of something orange that Teo had evidently pressed on him. He regarded it with suspicion.

"Depot spine's sweating again," he was telling Rafe. "Condensate on every conduit from Gate Four down. I put a towel over the junction box and it came back heavier than my boots."

"Scrubbers," said Rafe.

"Scrubbers don't make water. They take it." Achterberg sniffed the cup. "Something's putting it in the air."

Piq made a note of it out of pure habit, *humidity, depot spine, anecdotal*, and did not sit down. Ring A was the administrative ring. Prew walked it between Rook's office and the commissary, and Prew was Saar's adjutant, a soft-voiced man who logged faces the way Piq logged masses. An auditor sitting on the floor with the Brigade would appear in his evening summary as a line item.

Juno came out pale and dignified, holding gauze to her elbow. Teo's voice followed her. "Next. And if it's Varga, she's not on my list."

"She's on mine," Piq said, and went in.

The office was smaller than she had pictured it. That was a strange thing to notice. She had evidently been picturing it, and she logged the fact in a column without a heading. A centrifuge sat on a shelf, a rack of stoppered tubes beside it. A hand-lettered card over the counter read COUNTS ARE NOT GRADES. Teo wore her reading glasses and two layers of gloves.

Piq laid the table on the corner of the desk. "Hesper's cutting floor. It's denser than stock and worse per kilo. You'll want real numbers for your shielding geometry."

Teo stripped one pair of gloves and held the sheet close to her glasses. Her mouth did something complicated. "Point of origin."

"Point of origin."

"Saar will hate that." She tapped 0.81. "That one goes behind the pilot." She pulled out a chart from under a cup of tea gone to tar. It was an outline of the Mule's pressure hull with seats pencilled in and isodose lines curling across them in red, and she began to write Piq's numbers onto it in a hand much neater than her typing. "I'll need more of them than I'd need of stockpile. Maybe a third more."

"I know."

"Of course you know." She didn't say it unkindly. "Rafe's lymphocytes are at the bottom of normal. That's either Rafe, or six months next to that reactor collecting 2.223 MeV capture gammas that I logged as 'unattributed.' Everyone in that corridor has lied to me for five months. Nicely. And I let them. I'd see a neutron-capture line on a woman who works in hydroponics and write 'source unattributed' and feel clever about how precise it was." She looked up over the glasses. "Precise isn't the same as honest. You'd know that."

It landed somewhere under the sternum. Piq felt the 'any means' line in her notebook as though the notebook were warm.

"I'd know that," she said.

Teo called "Next." Rafe came in rolling his shoulder, and she found his vein on the first try.

"The other thing," Piq said, while the tube filled dark red. "I want to go up to the tank farm tomorrow. Tank Seven. It's been flagged unreconciled for fourteen months. The meter disagrees with the book, and farm rules bar any draw until an independent mass auditor verifies the contents in person."

"What's in it?"

Piq had looked it up at 1350 on a public terminal, between blocks. Then she had sat with the answer for a minute before she shut the screen.

"Twenty-six point eight tonnes of water. Booked as the return-leg reserve to the Ninefold Grace."

Teo's hands did not shake. She drew the needle, pressed gauze, folded Rafe's arm over it and said, "Hold that." Then she sat down in her own chair, which Piq had never seen her do with a person in the room.

"For when they came back," she said.

"The tug pool staged it before the Grace launched. Standard practice on a Europa run." Piq kept to the facts, because the facts were the gentlest thing she owned. "A reserve stays booked until the vessel is struck from the registry. Strike-off needs the hull claim closed, either paid or with the dispute ended."

Teo looked at her.

"Your appeal against the denial," Piq said. "It's still open. As long as it's open, the Grace isn't legally finished, and neither is her water."

She did not say the rest. Last night, in the lining of her own luggage, she had read Lagrange's claim file. The denial was built on four words, *deviated to low-energy trajectory*. On the asset schedule, Tank Seven sat under OFFSETS, waiting to be deducted from a settlement that would never be paid.

Teo took off her glasses and set them on the chart, across the pilot's seat.

"I filed that appeal so somebody would have to say it out loud," she said. "That they got it wrong. I didn't know I was keeping a tank full for her." She breathed out, a long and careful exhalation. "Fourteen months I've been paying Lagrange's dispute fees, and the whole time there's been a glass of water with her name on it, sitting in orbit."

Rafe had gone very still with his arm folded up.

"If I verify it," Piq said, "the operator can draw. Hesper's the operator."

"And Saar's directive?"

"Says *ice*. Tank Seven is pressurised liquid." She let a beat pass. "It's water until someone freezes it."

Teo laughed once. It surprised all three of them, and it wasn't quite a laugh. She put the glasses back on.

"You're not going outside the shell without a dose warden signing your exposure," she said. "Orbit is a whole different sky. I'm coming."

"You don't have to."

"I know what I have to do. Hold still, Rafe, you're leaking."

---

At 2310 the covert receiver woke against Piq's ribs.

Her quarters were a three-metre box with a fold-down desk, and she had stopped noticing the hum of the vent above it. Ballast had passed through the vent twice that evening. Piq could tell by the grit on the louvre. She unrolled the receiver and let it decode.

The pulse came in Amaya's keying. Piq could see it now as a hand and not a protocol: short marks clipped tight, long marks lingering as though Amaya resented letting go of them.

DOSE 38 MSV DAY. DRUM 55. RHEE COUGH BETTER. ODUYA SAYS TIDAL. ICE TALKS AT NIGHT. SLEEP OK. TELL T EAT.

Piq transcribed it twice and copied the line *tell T eat* onto a separate scrap. She would hand it over without comment.

Then she opened her Lagrange queue.

One message from Pell had come in on the evening batch, stamped as 51 minutes old at the relay and older than that in fact.

VARGA. THROW CABAL PRINCIPALS? ADVISE INTENT, MASS, LAUNCH DATE. ANY MEANS STANDS. PELL.

She read it the way she read every Pell message, which was as though it cost him a kilogram a word. Below it sat her own outbound column: the long report from four days ago, *four alive, reactor drum sixty days*, two follow-ups and an amended ledger. Each one carried the same small grey flag.

ACK pending.

The relay was batching. Everyone said so. The comms tech had said so in her second week, with the cheerful despair of a man who couldn't do anything about it. Pell never acknowledged anything until he had decided what to do about it, and he had always written like that. He had read *four alive* and replied with the question he had already drafted, because questions were what Pell sent.

She typed: *Re cabal: see my report of the 14th. No cabal. Repeat, survivors. Advise.* She marked it urgent. The urgency flag cost forty grams of her monthly bandwidth allowance, and she paid it.

It sat down in the queue beside the others under its own grey flag.

---

The hopper rose off the Valhalla pad at 0700 in a long slant of banded Jupiter-light. Callisto fell away as a pocked brown plate, and then as a curve. Teo sat across from her with her knees at her chest and a dosimeter on each wrist. She did not talk during the burn, and Piq liked her for it.

The tank farm was eleven cylinders strung along a truss in a 200-kilometre orbit, frosted on the shadow side, rotating slowly to keep their sun loads even. They cycled through the hub lock in suits, clipped to the truss rail, and went out into the full sky.

"Here's what's changed," Teo said over the suit channel. She had clipped her boots to the rail and let the rest of her swing outward, so that from Piq's angle she hung upside down against Jupiter. "On the surface, Callisto blocks half your sky. Half the galactic cosmic rays come up through the moon and never reach you. Up here you get the whole sphere. Your dose rate roughly doubles, plus whatever the old man throws when the field lines twitch. I'm giving you ninety minutes. At sixty I start nagging. At ninety I pull you in by your tether, and I've practised."

"You're upside down."

"Nothing's upside down. You're upside down. Gravity is an opinion up here, and I'm the one with the licence."

Tank Seven was the fourth cylinder: twelve metres long, with a pressure gauge on its end cap that read 21.4 tonnes. The book said 26.8. Somewhere, 5.4 tonnes of water was either missing or lying.

"You can't weigh anything up here," Teo said. "So how do you weigh it?"

"You push it and see how hard it argues." Piq unpacked the rig: a cold-gas thruster pack of calibrated impulse, a laser rangefinder and a reflector. "Known push, measured change in velocity, mass is the quotient. But the push isn't perfectly known. Thrusters drift. So first I calibrate against a reference mass." She pulled out the flat orange coil of a propellant bladder. "Five hundred kilos of water from the farm's metered line. The meter on the line is certified. The meter on Tank Seven is the one that's lying."

The fill took eleven minutes. The bladder swelled into a fat orange lozenge the size of a sofa, and Piq clipped it to the calibration bracket and fired the rig.

The bracket clip was rated for the load. It was not rated for the slosh. The water inside the bladder got a push a fraction of a second after the skin did, caught up and kept going. The clip pinged open with a sound Piq felt through her glove more than heard, and the bladder set off along the truss end over end, wobbling, its contents lagging behind each turn so the rotation stuttered and changed axis like a thing with intentions.

"That's half a tonne," Piq said. Her voice came out remarkably level. "Moving at about fifteen centimetres a second. It won't hurt anything slowly. It'll hurt it thoroughly."

"Where's it going?"

"Tank Eight's gauge cluster."

Teo unclipped her boots and pushed off, still upside down relative to everything, and drifted along the rail with her tether paying out behind her. "Keep talking. Which way is it going to flip?"

Piq watched the bladder. The water inside it was the problem and also the information. "It'll hang on the long axis, then dump to the short one. Every third turn, near enough. Don't grab the end. Get the middle when it's broadside."

"Broadside when?"

"Three. Two. Now."

Teo caught it across its belly the way a person catches a falling mattress. The water bulged around her arms, and for a second the bladder carried her, both of them turning slowly toward Tank Eight's gauges. Then Piq hit the tether winch, the line went taut and the whole assembly stopped and swung back toward her, with Teo spread across the orange skin like a woman hugging a cloud.

"Dose check," Teo said into the silence. "Thirty-one minutes. You're fine. I'm fine. That bladder is a disgrace."

"It was behaving physically."

"It was showing off."

They lashed it to the bracket with two straps, and on Piq's insistence a third, then ran the calibration. The thruster was 1.8 percent hot. Piq logged the correction, and then they went to push Tank Seven.

The first push read wrong, and so did the second. The tank moved, but the rangefinder showed a stutter. The velocity jumped, then settled, then jumped again.

"Something inside's not moving with the shell," Piq said. "The bladder in there isn't seated." She pulled up the schematic in her visor. "The ullage line. If the ullage valve's stuck, the gas side can't vent as the bladder shifts. The pressure gauge reads that trapped gas as missing water. It's been reading low for fourteen months because a valve froze."

The ullage valve was a hand wheel on the end cap, rimed white. Piq braced against the cap and turned. It didn't move. She turned again, and the only thing that moved was Piq, which in free fall meant she rotated gently around the valve stem until she was looking at Teo's boots.

"Equal and opposite," Teo observed.

"I'm aware."

"You need a fixed point." Teo clipped herself to the cap rail, set her boots against the tank, and reached around Piq to get both gloves on the far side of the wheel. Her visor bumped Piq's shoulder. "On three, you push and I pull. Same direction of turn, opposite arms. We cancel each other's reaction."

"That's a torque couple."

"It's a lecture. Shut up and count."

Piq counted. On three the wheel gave a quarter-turn with a crack that came up through the tank into both their bodies. A thin white plume of frozen gas sparkled out of the vent, and the end-cap gauge began to climb. It went through 22, 24 and 26 and settled.

They pushed the tank again. The rangefinder came back clean.

"Twenty-six point seven nine," Piq said. "Within ten kilograms of the book. Within the error of a calibration I'd sign." She signed it, in her visor and then physically on the farm's paper tag, because farm rules were old.

Teo was still braced on the end cap with her glove flat on the tank's frost. She didn't say anything for a while. The sky turned around them.

"Fifty-eight minutes," she said finally. "I'd like to start nagging."

"Go on."

"Come inside."

---

The inner lock at the Asgard shuttle port was a vertical tube two metres across. It had been built when the station's architects still believed people would be glad to stand close together. The repressurisation cycle took four minutes. They stood in it in their suit liners with their helmets under their arms and their hair flattened and steaming slightly, and neither of them pretended to read the panel.

"You counted the bladder's flips," Teo said. "In your head. While it was tumbling."

"I watched the water. The water told me."

"Most people would have panicked."

"Most people don't think in tonnes." Piq looked at her. "You went after it upside down."

"I keep telling you, there's no such thing." Teo's mouth twitched. "I don't trust you, Varga. I want that on the record. But you're the first person in a long time I'd want holding the other side of a valve."

The panel chimed at half pressure. The air thickened, and with it came the smell of the station, ozone and wet regolith and somebody's lunch. Piq could hear her own pulse. She could also hear, distantly, the auditor in her starting a column, and she declined to let it.

Teo's hand came up, slowly, and pushed a wet strand of hair off Piq's forehead. Her glove was off. Her fingers were cold from the suit.

Piq leaned in by perhaps four centimetres. Teo didn't lean back.

The duct grille above them rattled, dropped open on its hinge, and released 4.1 kilograms of tabby cat into the 0.126 g. Ballast fell between them with tremendous dignity and all four legs spread, taking almost a full second to cover the distance. He landed on Piq's helmet, which was still under her arm, and from there leaped to Teo's shoulder. He settled there and began to wash a paw.

The panel chimed full pressure.

"That," Teo said, without moving, "is undeclared."

"I'm aware of the cat."

"There's a cat on my shoulder in a pressure lock. It's been through the vent in an unfiltered supply line. Do you know what its dose history is?"

"He's never been on a manifest. He hasn't got a dose history."

"Then I'll have to start one." Teo lifted Ballast off her shoulder by the scruff and held him at arm's length. He accepted it like a man accepting a summons. Her eyes came back to Piq's, and whatever had been in the lock was still there. It had been noted and deferred. "Later," she said, and it was not clear which of them she meant.

The outer door opened onto a corridor with two port techs in it, and the moment was gone. Ballast squirmed free, sprang to a cable tray and walked off along it into the dark with his tail up.

---

The hangar at 2200 was cold and bright. The Mule wore its first course of crust blocks along the forward hull like a mottled cheek. Piq spread the ledger across the top of a saw housing. Hesper, Lucky, Teo and half the Brigade stood around it in a rough ring.

"Dry mass," she said, and wrote it as she spoke. "Mule as stripped, reactor and drum included, fifty-one tonnes. Teo's geometry for a crewed Europa landing and four survivors: thirty-one crust blocks, all weighed, twenty-one point eight tonnes. If it were clean stockpile ice it would be about sixteen."

"Clause fourteen would have made it eighteen," Lucky said. "Water blankets. Three hundred metres a second, every Europa job."

"This is worse mass than the clause's water, and more of it. Crew, consumables, medical: four point two. Four survivors and their suits on the way home: nought point six." She drew the line. "Propellant is the Brigade's stockpile through the electrolysis train, plus Tank Seven as of this morning, at nine hundred seconds specific impulse with the reactor hot."

She wrote the legs down the left side.

*Callisto surface to transfer: 3.32 km/s.*
*Europa arrival, capture, landing: 3.59.*
*Europa ascent and departure for Callisto: 3.59.*
*Callisto arrival and landing: 3.32.*
*Margin, gravity losses, steering: 0.70.*

Then she ran the rocket equation leg by leg. She subtracted propellant as it burned, added the survivors at Europa and carried the mass forward. Nobody spoke. The saws were cold, and somewhere a pump ticked.

The outbound legs closed and the landing closed. The ascent from Europa closed with the blocks still on, because Teo would not take them off and Piq would not ask her to. That left the last line.

*Available at Callisto arrival: 3.18 km/s.*
*Required: 3.32.*

"A hundred and forty," Piq said. "Short. With Tank Seven. With every block weighed."

She heard Hesper breathe out through her nose.

"Strip blocks on the way back," Rafe said.

"Then the survivors cross the Callisto approach behind three tonnes less shielding," Teo said. "They'll already be at the edge of what I can sign. No."

"Different reactor mode," Abara said.

"I've got it at max Isp already."

Lucky had his head tilted at the ledger as though it were a bet he could still talk his way out of. "Europa to Ganymede's a thousand cheaper than Europa to Callisto. Easy. Shallower well, closer orbit." He grinned, but there was nothing behind it. "Ganymede's the cheap seats. But Ur would shoot us."

A couple of people laughed out of habit. Piq didn't. She wrote *EUR to GAN?* in the margin small, because a cheaper option is always worth a line even when it's useless. Then she crossed it out, because an option is only an option if you survive arriving.

"So we find a hundred and forty," Hesper said. "We've got days."

"Fifty-five days on the drum," Teo said quietly. "As of last night."

The hangar's main door opened.

Saar came in without hurrying. Two JDA officers walked behind him. Koskinen was behind them carrying a grey evidence case, and he had the hunted look of someone who had been told to hold something and not told why. Saar wore his smile, the one that audited back. It took in the Mule, the grid in the floor, the chalked blocks and the ledger, and it stopped on Piq.

"Point of origin," he said pleasantly. "That's very good. I'll be reading the statute tonight." He did not look at the floor again. He looked at the rack beside the Mule's port skid, where the Brigade's receiver sat with its patch-cabled antenna feed snaking up through the ceiling toward the surface array. "Unlicensed reception equipment. Configured to a retired band, with no frequency allocation, on a JDA facility. Officer."

One of the officers unplugged it. The receiver's status light went out. There was a small and terrible quiet as eleven people watched a box go dark that had been the only voice coming out of Europa for them.

"You can't," Hesper said.

"I'm afraid I'm required to." Saar watched Koskinen fit the unit into the case and seal it, and his voice did not change at all. "Interference on maintenance bands is a safety matter. There's been a great deal of chatter on that one. Automated echoes, I'm told."

He nodded to the room as if he had attended a meeting, and left. Koskinen trailed after him with the case held out in front of him like a cake.

Nobody moved. Teo had gone white along the jaw. Hesper put both hands flat on the saw housing, on top of Piq's ledger, on top of the hundred and forty.

Piq stood with her arms folded over the lining of her jacket. Under her left forearm, sewn into the panel of the Lagrange comms unit she had declared on arrival as 1.3 kilograms of audit reference masses, the covert receiver lay warm and silent. It was the only ear left on Callisto that could hear Europa. Europa would clear the limb of Jupiter in forty-one minutes, and Amaya would key into a room that no longer had a box in it.

Teo's eyes found hers across the ring of faces. Piq understood, coldly and with complete arithmetical clarity, that within the next forty-one minutes she would have to decide what to tell her.

## Chapter 10: Interlock

The hangar had a particular silence after Saar left. Piq would have weighed it if she could: eleven people standing very still in a tenth of a gravity, and the air handlers suddenly loud. The empty bench where the receiver had sat still held its outline in dust. Koskinen had carried it out in the grey evidence case with both hands, like a cake he was afraid of dropping, and somebody had already set a coffee bulb in the clean rectangle as if to cover a wound.

Lucky knocked three times on the Mule's skid. Nobody laughed.

Juno said, "Five months," and that was all she said. Five months of archive was gone into a JDA locker. That was every frame the Brigade had ever logged, including the ones from before anyone knew what they were.

Hesper had not moved. She stood with her hands in the thigh pockets of her coverall and her eyes on the bench, and Piq could see her running a calculation with no good terms in it. Behind her the wall chart still read *Europa's down* in somebody's careful block capitals. Under it was the next window: 2241 tonight. Amaya keyed her long frame at 2241 every night the geometry allowed it, the way other people set an alarm, and the Brigade's set had answered with a single carrier pulse. That was one bit, and it meant *we hear you*. It was the only thing they had ever been able to send back.

Tonight nothing would answer her.

Piq checked the time on the hangar's wall clock, which ran four seconds fast and which she had stopped correcting for in her head on her second day. 2201. Forty minutes.

Teo was looking at her. She had been looking at her since Koskinen's boots cleared the hatch. Her jaw had gone white along the hinge, and she was not looking at Saar's back or at the empty bench. She was looking at Piq with the patience of someone who has already done the arithmetic and is waiting to see whether the other party will present the same figure.

*She knows I have an ear.* Teo had seen Piq's logs in the cipher room before anyone in the hangar had shown her a frame. The timestamps had been Piq's and not the Brigade's. Teo corrected people's rounding for a living. She had known for days and had said nothing, and that was its own kind of entry.

There were several ways to do this. Piq listed them, because listing was what she did when she was frightened. She could say nothing and listen alone, and Amaya would hear silence from Callisto on the first night after five months of answers. She could tell the whole room, and in a week eleven people's enthusiasm would put her kit in Saar's evidence locker next to the hangar set. Or she could tell the one person who already knew, and the one person who would decide whether the rest needed to.

She crossed to Hesper. The low gravity made it a short, deliberate glide that looked calmer than she was. She said, quietly, "I have a receiver."

Hesper's eyes came off the bench.

"Survey grade. It's part of my reference set. Ibis certifies us to sweep the bands for interference before we calibrate a scale. Stray RF walks load cells." All of that was true, and none of it was the reason. "It's been on the retired band since my second night. It's how I found the pattern before I found you."

"Your second night," Hesper said.

"Yes."

"And you sat in my hangar and watched us lose ours."

"I sat in your hangar and watched Saar look at the case it's in," Piq said. "He's already seen that one of my masses is hollow. If I'd said anything with him here, there'd be two receivers in a locker and none in the basin."

Hesper considered this. Her face did the thing Piq had seen it do over the crust geometry: it took the problem and turned it until a flat side faced up. "Can it send?"

"Burst. Short. Not much."

"A heartbeat?"

"A heartbeat. More than that if it has to."

"Then go," Hesper said. "Don't walk there with a parade. Teo goes with you, because Teo's going to go with you whether I say so or not." She raised her voice for the room, flat and unhurried. "Shift's done. Juno, cover the floor cuts. Lucky, stop knocking on things, she'll answer when she answers."

---

Piq's quarters were three point one metres by two point four, with a fold-down bunk and a desk that was also the lid of her locker. With two people inside, every movement had to be negotiated in advance. Teo stood with her back to the hatch and her arms folded, so that she took up as little of the room as possible while somehow occupying all of it.

Piq lifted the reference case onto the desk. The five masses sat in their foam: four dense, and one that had read lighter under the densitometer that morning, the way a liar's pulse reads under a cuff. She took out the fourth mass and gave it a quarter-turn and a reverse quarter-turn. The cap came away. Inside, under a skin of tungsten shot bonded to the wall to make up the declared weight, the kit lay in its pads: burst transceiver, antenna lead, receiver, cell.

Teo looked at it for a long moment.

"That isn't survey kit," she said.

"It's very expensive survey kit."

"Varga."

"The antenna runs up the light-pipe," Piq said. She was clipping the lead to the coupler behind the louvre, where Ballast's grit had left a fine grey crescent on the sill. "The pipe's a waveguide for about forty centimetres if you're lucky and nobody's cleaned it. Which nobody has."

Teo did not unfold her arms. She said, very evenly, "Here's what I'm going to do. I'm going to write down that you have an instrument I can't identify, that you've carried it since intake, and that you told me tonight and not before. Then I'm going to stand here while it's useful. You can tell me the rest of what it is when you decide I've earned it, or when you decide you have. I don't much care which."

"That's fair."

"It isn't fair. It's a dose history." She glanced at the clock on Piq's slate. "Twenty-six minutes. Do you need me to do anything or just not breathe on it?"

"Read for me. Your hands are better on her grid than mine."

That was true too, and it was the first thing Piq had said in a week that was true all the way down. Teo heard it. Something in her shoulders gave a millimetre.

At 2241:06 the receiver chirped. It was the badge-alarm tone, so that anyone in the corridor would hear only a resident getting nagged about her dose. The frame came up on the slate in raw pulses, clipped shorts and lingering longs, the hand Piq had learned to read the way you learn a colleague's signature: by the corners it cut.

Teo read it off the bunk grid without paper.

"HANGAR QUIET. YOU OK. DRUM 54." A pause. The doubled final pulse came, Amaya's mark, the thing she'd done at fourteen to show her sister she'd meant it. "Then a question mark. She uses a long-long-short for it. I gave her that. I don't think I've seen it in nine years."

"She's asking why nobody answered."

"She's asking if *we're* all right." Teo's voice did not change, but her hand had come up flat against the wall. "Of course she is."

Piq had already built the burst: a single carrier pulse and then, because one bit seemed suddenly too small a thing to send to a woman under a kilometre of ice, the grid code for *HEAR YOU* and Amaya's own doubled final pulse back at her.

She made herself say the cost aloud. "Saar logs everything I transmit. He can't read it, but he'll have the azimuth. Until now everything's gone up toward Mars. This goes to Europa."

"Will he know the difference?"

"If he looks. He looks."

Teo's eyes were on the frame and the question mark. "Send it."

Piq sent it. It took four tenths of a second. On the meter it looked like nothing at all.

---

Directive 2214-31a went up on the station notice board at 0600 on Day 22. Piq read it in the queue for the Ring C galley, between a man with an illegal cardamom pod tucked behind one ear and a child carrying a water ration in both hands as if it were a sacrament.

*Further to 2214-31: for the purposes of this directive, "ice" includes any separated composite material of which frozen water forms a constituent part by mass, including but not limited to regolith-bonded deposits, crust, floor material and fill. Such material, once separated, shall be deemed to be at its point of origin wherever it lies at the time of this notice, and shall be held there pending countersignature. — M. Saar, JDA-SEC.*

She read it twice. It was good drafting. Saar had stayed up with the statute as promised, and he had not tried to undo the floor gambit. He had frozen it in place. Twelve of the thirty-one crust blocks had gone onto the Mule's frame before midnight. The other nineteen lay chalked and initialled on the hangar floor, three metres off the port skid, and as of 0600 that was legally where they had come from. They could not move the three metres. They could not move a millimetre.

She drew a square in the 2214-31 column of her ledger. *Later.* Squares were not answers. They were promises to look.

"Auditor."

Fitch was standing by the galley hatch with something under his arm. He was the environmental tech for Ring B, with mournful eyebrows and nine months of a 4.1 kg discrepancy in his scrubber residuals, and he had the air of a man who has been patient about a thing for longer than is healthy. The something was a trap: a wire box with a spring door and, inside it, a load cell and a sliver of reconstituted fish.

"I've been meaning to find you," he said. "You're certified."

"I am."

"Then you can certify a mass." He hefted the box. "It's in the ducts. Ring B east, around Junction Fourteen. Nine months I've been carrying four point one kilos of unexplained biological in my residuals, and the regional office thinks I'm cooking the filters. I'm not cooking the filters. Something is *eating* in my ducts and breathing in my ducts and leaving—" he lowered his voice, "—*deposits*. If I catch it and you weigh it, it's on the books. If it's on the books, I'm not a liar."

Piq thought of the 4.1 kg blip her sensor sweep had flagged in a ventilation duct on her second day, and of what she had found when she put her lamp to the grille: a notched ear, a white bib, and a tabby face regarding her without the least concern, with the end of her ration bar in its mouth. It had turned and gone off down the duct like a judgement.

"I can only certify a mass that's presented," she said. "A mass without a manifest isn't a mass. It's a rumour."

"So help me present it."

"Fitch, I've got nineteen tonnes of floor that's legally where it's lying. I can't take on a rumour this week."

He looked at her for a long, sorrowful moment. Then, with dignity, he said, "It took your ration bar, didn't it."

"I'm not at liberty to discuss my ration bar."

"It's taken everybody's ration bar," Fitch said, with something close to love, and went off toward Ring B with the trap held out in front of him like a lantern.

---

Teo's office at the end of Ring A smelled of centrifuge oil and her tea, which had gone to tar in its bulb sometime the previous evening and been neither drunk nor thrown away. The card on the shelf still said COUNTS ARE NOT GRADES. Under it, today, a second card had appeared in Teo's square hand: NEITHER ARE DOSES.

She had the triage on the wall screen when Piq came in at 1500. It was a grid. It would be.

"I need four histories," Teo said, without turning. "I don't have them. What I've got is Amaya's 'thirty-eight a day', which I don't believe."

"You think she's lying."

"I think she's rounding in the direction that keeps the thing from breaking." It was Hesper's phrase, and Teo used it like a diagnosis. "Thirty-eight millisieverts a day isn't a living dose under that much ice. If they'd taken thirty-eight a day for fourteen months they'd have sixteen sieverts each and they'd be keying from the afterlife. Under a few metres of overburden, in a cut, their living dose should be a fraction of that. A few mSv. Maybe four." She tapped a cell, and the column beneath it lit. "Thirty-eight is what you get if somebody goes up to the surface every night and stands at a mast for a few minutes to key."

Piq had already done that sum in the corridor and had not wanted to be the one to say it. Five sieverts a day was about three and a half millisieverts a minute in the open. A suit did very little against what the trailing hemisphere threw. Ten minutes at the mast, with the walk up and the walk down, cost a little over thirty.

"It's the transmitter," she said. "Someone's carrying it up."

"Someone." Teo turned around at last. She had her reading glasses on, low, and over them her eyes were steady and appalling. "It's her. Of course it's her. She was fourteen and I told her that if you have to spend dose, spend it fast and spend it once. She took that to mean you're allowed to spend it."

"Then tell her not to."

"That's the plan." Teo pulled up a second sheet. "Message one. Stop the mast runs except on alternate nights. Rotate the keyer. I want each of their cumulative doses, estimated, and a symptom list. Nausea, mouth, skin, bruising, hair. I want Rhee's cough described in five words, not one." Her finger moved down the grid. "Message two goes once I know where everyone stands. Surface sequence for the pickup. Who comes out first, in what order, how long each of them is exposed between crevasse and crawler. Heaviest dose last, because the last one waits in the most ice. Unless they can't walk, in which case they go first, because whoever carries them carries them fast."

"And the rescuers?"

"That's my column." She did not elaborate. Piq looked at the column anyway. In the cell for medic Teo had written a number that was not a dose but a ceiling, and next to it a second number that was higher, in pencil, the way you write a figure you have decided you will be allowed to exceed.

Piq did not mention it. She did not have the standing.

"Can you get all that into her grid?" she said instead. "It's long."

"It'll go in three nights if you can give me forty pulses a frame."

"I can give you forty. More, and the burst starts to look like a conversation."

"It *is* a conversation."

"Not on Saar's azimuth log."

Teo exhaled through her nose and changed the number to forty.

There was one more thing Piq had to put in, and she had been carrying it since the claim file. She put it in now as an auditor would, as a line item that needed verifying.

"Ask her for the flight recorder."

Teo's hand stopped on the grid.

"The Ninefold Grace logged state, mass and control inputs until it hit the ice," Piq said. "If the recorder survived, and if they still have power to read it, it exists. You've been appealing a denial for fourteen months on the strength of a dose log and your own certainty. A recorder isn't certainty. It's evidence."

"It'll cost her drum."

"It'll cost her drum," Piq agreed. "She can decide whether it's worth it. She's an engineer."

Teo looked at the wall and the long grid of other people's bodies. "She'll say yes. She'll say yes before I've finished asking."

"Then let her answer first. Nobody else got to."

Teo added a row.

Piq took the folded scrap out of the thigh pocket where it had been riding for two days. She had copied it from Amaya's frame on the night of Day 20, in her own hand, three letters and a word: *TELL T EAT*. She set it on the desk next to the dead tea and said nothing.

Teo read it. Then she read it again. Then she took her glasses off, which Piq had learned was what she did instead of anything else. She opened the drawer under the centrifuge, took out a ration bar, peeled it, and ate it standing up, mechanically and completely, looking at the wall the whole time.

"She always told on me," she said when she'd finished. "To our mother. *Teo didn't eat.* Every shift change."

Piq kept her eyes on the grid.

---

Amaya's answer came at 2241 on Day 22, through the light-pipe and the cheap old coupler, while Teo sat on the end of Piq's bunk with her boots drawn up so they wouldn't touch anything.

It was not long. Amaya had never been long.

*OK ALT NIGHTS. ODUYA KEYS TMRW. RECORDER YES. 4 NIGHTS. COSTS 1 DAY DRUM. WORTH. DRUM 53.*

Then the doubled pulse.

"She said yes before I asked," Teo said quietly. "We sent the request in the same frame as the triage. She answered the last line first."

"She had a tape of the frame running," Piq said. "She read the end."

"She read what she wanted."

Piq was looking at the number. *DRUM 53.*

The first clean frame she had ever decoded, on the night of Day 15, the one that had come up FOUR ALIVE, had said sixty. She had taken it on faith because she had wanted to. On Day 20 it had said fifty-five. Last night, fifty-four. Tonight, fifty-three. Seven days on the calendar, seven days off the drum, to the day, every time she had a reading.

Reactors did not do that. A drum burning under a habitat with a sick man in it, a mast run every night and somebody melting ice to hold a crevasse open did not lose exactly one day per day. A table did.

"Teo," she said. "What's she measuring the drum against?"

"Days at draw."

"At which draw?"

Teo turned her head.

Piq already had the reactor plate up. Ninefold Grace drew from the same TK-Δ series family as the Mule, an older lot, and the depletion curves were standard Compact public data, because anything with fission in it had to be. In the Day 15 frame, between the dose figures, Amaya had sent a thermal output number. Piq had logged it in a column headed *?* because she hadn't known what it was for. She knew now. She ran it.

"The drum curve is sixty days at hold draw," she said. "Hold is the lowest the reactor goes without the control drums seizing. It keeps the habitat around four degrees, scrubbers on half. Her count's been dropping one a day exactly, which means she isn't reading the drum at all. She looked up the hold-draw figure once and she's been subtracting the calendar from it. And I don't think they've been at hold. Not with Rhee coughing, and not with Oduya on a mast every alternate night, and not with somebody heating up ice to keep a crevasse open. Her thermal number says they've been above it the whole time."

"So the real count is lower."

"She's quoting the best case for the worst life. That's—" Piq stopped herself before she could say *her flaw*, because she had no right to the word. "That's optimism."

"Give me the number."

Piq gave it to her the way she would have given it to an underwriter, with the error bar. "At her reported thermal output, with the recorder transfer costed in as she says, the drum hits shutdown margin on Day 51. Plus or minus three."

Teo did the subtraction aloud, because she needed to hear a voice do it. "Blackout opens on thirty-eight. Closes on fifty-seven."

"So the rescue has to fly inside the blackout. Which we knew. But it can't fly at the end of it. It has to fly early."

"With nineteen blocks frozen to the floor."

"And a hundred and forty metres per second we don't have."

Teo looked at her for a long time. Then she said, "Write it in your book."

Piq wrote it. *DRUM: Amaya 53 (hold table minus calendar). Recalc 51 ± 3 (actual draw). Fly early.* She drew no square next to it. There was no *later* left in that line.

---

The recorder came in over four nights, and over four days the station went about its business as though nothing in it were dying.

On Day 23 Koskinen was posted to the hangar floor with a clipboard and a chalk line. He was to note any displacement of the nineteen frozen blocks greater than two centimetres, which was his best reading of *wherever it lies*. Lucky asked him whether thermal expansion counted. Koskinen said he'd ask. He asked. Saar's office sent back the word *no* and nothing else. Koskinen pinned it to his clipboard and looked relieved.

On Day 23 Juno's tithe block was still held in the lift and still dripping into Koskinen's cup. Piq tared it for him again on the way past, and he thanked her as if she'd done him a favour.

On Day 24 Fitch's trap caught a cleaning drone.

On Day 24 Piq wrote her report to Pell. It went out as 0941, padded in the usual way to look like a bulky routine upload: forty megabytes of depot calibration tables wrapped around four kilobytes of truth. *Survivors confirmed alive, see 0911, 0927. Drum recalculated: failure D51 ±3. Flight recorder of NFG in receipt, partial. Request urgent advice re: claim LM-C-2213-1146. Request urgent advice re: R7 order.* Her outbound queue now had seven items. Every one of them was labelled ACK PENDING in the same patient grey. She looked at the column and thought *the relay is batching* and *Pell never acks until he's decided*. She closed the queue the way you close the door on a room you'll clean later.

On Day 25 at 2244 the last packet cleared the coupler. Amaya had added her mark to it twice.

---

They worked it in Teo's office, because Teo's office had the larger screen and a door that her own override could lock. Teo, it turned out, could lock it against her own override too, a feature she had requested and been granted after an incident she declined to describe.

A flight recorder is not a story. It is a sequence of numbers and switch states, flattened to a few kilobytes a second and then, in this case, flattened again by a woman in a crevasse who had chosen which channels mattered and thrown the rest away. Amaya had chosen well. She had kept mass state, propellant state, tank valve states, the control log and the dose sensor, and nothing else.

Piq laid it out on the screen as a ledger. She could not have laid it out any other way.

"T zero," she said. "Departure burn from Callisto parking. Total mass 117.4 tonnes: dry about thirty-eight, propellant 61.4, shielding 18. It matches the filed plan to the kilogram. They weren't overloaded. Whoever loaded them was good."

"Hesper," Teo said. "Hesper loaded them."

Piq kept going. "T plus 1.2 hours, burn complete. Propellant state nominal. Shielding tanks show *locked*, which is—"

She stopped.

Teo leaned in. "Which is what?"

Piq scrolled back through the valve channels. Six shielding tank valves were listed, sorted A to F. Through the departure burn every one of them read open to the feed. At burn cutoff, all six changed state together and took on a flag in a field the filed telemetry hadn't included. The flag was a five-character code. She had seen it before on placards and on the faces of compliance boxes on tug decks. She had once seen it in a Lagrange training manual in a font so small it might have been a disclaimer.

*14C/I.*

"It's an interlock," she said.

"A what?"

"Clause 14(c). The dose clause. When I came, I recited it to the tug crew in Bay Three." She could hear herself in that bay twenty-odd days ago, word-perfect, a little pleased. "*Minimum shielding water, scaled by filed transit time, to be retained for the duration of the exposed transit.* Retained. The 2213 revision made any cover on a Europa run conditional on an interlock box that enforces it. It locks the shielding tanks out of the propellant feed until the vessel is on the surface or out of the torus. And it doesn't arm on the pad. It arms at departure cutoff, so you can still trim and dump on the ground."

Teo said nothing for a moment. "They're water tanks."

"They're water tanks."

"Then how did Hesper file a plan that counted them as fuel? She doesn't make that kind of mistake."

"She didn't make one." Piq pulled the filing header up beside the valve log. "She filed on the tug pool's standing Europa template. It's older than the revision. Its mass sheet still books shielding as *consumable on capture*, because before 2213 that's what it was. The Grace's box was a retrofit." The maintenance stamp was there in the header, in Lagrange's own format. "Fitted nineteen days before departure, to make the revision's compliance date. Fitted to the tanks, not written into the template. So every pre-flight check on Callisto ran against six unlocked tanks of water, because on the pad they *were* unlocked. Nothing in the system saw those tanks the way the box would until the box armed. By then they'd already burned."

"Every pilot on this station used to burn their blankets on capture," Teo said slowly. "I've heard them complain about it for years. You carried the shield in and you burned it in the last minutes, when you were already past the worst of the belt, and now they—" She stopped. "Lucky. Lucky said three hundred metres a second."

"Lucky said Lagrange's water blankets cost him three hundred metres per second on every Europa job," Piq said. Her mouth was dry. "Because they do, now. He has to carry them all the way down as dead mass. They're not reaction mass any more."

She pulled the next channel up.

"T plus 4.1 hours. The flight computer runs the capture-and-landing check against current state. The check fails." The log was there in plain text, because flight computers had no tact. *CAPTURE PROFILE FAST-3.9 — REQ 3.59 KM/S — AVAIL 3.22 KM/S — SHLD UNAVAIL (14C/I) — DEFICIT 0.37 KM/S.* "Here's the arithmetic. After the departure burn, with the shielding locked out as dead mass, they've got three point two two kilometres a second left in the tanks. That's the whole budget. The fast capture and landing needs three point five nine. Three hundred and seventy short. If the blankets had counted as propellant, the way the template said they would, they'd have had about three point seven and it closes with margin."

"Could they turn back?"

Piq had already run it, because she ran everything. "Capture back at Callisto costs about three point three. Against the same three point two two. Eighty short. That doesn't close either. They're past the turn."

"So what does close?"

"T plus 4.2 hours." The control log had it, and beside it, in a field the flight computer reserved for crew annotation, three words in a pilot's two-finger shorthand: *only one closes.* "Rhee switches to the low-energy profile. A long Ganymede-perturbed fall. Capture and landing on that path cost about two point nine, which fits inside three point two two with the blankets still locked aboard, because it pays in time instead of propellant. Nine point six days instead of three point nine. And it shelves in the plasma torus for eleven hours instead of four."

"The torus shelf," Teo said. She knew her sister's dose log by heart; Piq had watched her recite it. "Eleven hours against four predicted."

"Yes."

"And the spike."

Piq ran the dose channel against the control log and found it at once, because a thing that size could not hide. T plus 9.0 days. A forty-second burst, dense, high on the energy scale, exactly as Amaya had logged it. The adjusters had called it a *terminal artifact*, a sensor having its last fit as the ship died. Two seconds into that burst, the flight computer's state field read *SEU-LATCH*. Then it read nothing. For six minutes it read nothing, and the control inputs that followed were not automatic. They were jagged and human: Rhee flying a descent by hand from a dead computer's last solution, and nearly making it.

"Single-event upset," Piq said. Her voice had gone flat in a way she recognised from her father in the weigh-house, reading a scale that told him something he didn't want to know. "A heavy ion through the processor. It latched up and lost the descent solution, and Rhee hand-flew the rest. They came down hard, but they came down. Telemetry ended at T plus 9.1 because the antenna went into the ice, not because they died."

The room was quiet. The centrifuge ticked as it cooled.

"Say it," Teo said. "Say the whole thing in order. I want to hear it in one line."

Piq said it in one line, because she owed her that.

"Lagrange's dose clause made them carry eighteen tonnes of water they weren't allowed to burn. That put the fast transfer out of reach. The only path that closed was slow. The slow path held them in the torus nearly three times as long. The torus killed the flight computer. Then Lagrange denied the claim because the pilot had *deviated to a low-energy trajectory*, and called it pilot error."

Teo stood up.

It was not quick. In 0.126 g nothing was quick unless you meant it to be. She rose with the slow inevitability of a weight on a long arm, and when she was upright her hands were shaking. Piq had never seen them do that, not on the valve, and not in the cipher room when the first letters came out.

"Fourteen months," Teo said. "Fourteen months of dispute fees. Forty marks a filing, every time I wanted them to look again, and they looked. *They looked.* They had the interlock spec. It's their box. It's their clause. They wrote the rule that put my sister into the torus, and then they billed me to ask why she was there." Her voice did not rise, and that was the worst thing about it. "And they've still got her water. Did you know that? Her return reserve. Twenty-six tonnes the Grace staged here to fill up on for the trip home, and it's still sitting in Tank Seven in our own tug pool. Lagrange has it booked as an *offset* against the claim. She's alive under a kilometre of ice, and they're holding the water she was supposed to come home on as part of the settlement."

Piq knew. It was listed under OFFSETS on page eleven of LM-C-2213-1146, which she had read in her quarters by the light of her own slate on the night of Day 18. On page four of the same file, against *Shielding: 18.0 t per Clause 14(c)*, was a tick in her own hand. She had ticked it as compliant. She had ticked it as correct.

"They put her in the ice with a safety rule," Teo said, "and they called it her captain's fault, and they wrote her off, and they're still collecting on her. Fourteen months of her down there and they never once had to look. And people like you—"

She caught herself. Her eyes went, for half a second, to the thigh pocket where Piq's slate rode, and Piq knew exactly what she was seeing: the foam, the hollow mass, the kit in its pads. *That isn't survey kit.* Teo had said it two nights ago in a room three metres long. She had written it down.

Then, visibly, she set it aside. Her hand went flat to the wall, the way it had in Piq's quarters.

"No," she said. "Not you. I know what I saw in your case. I've decided what I'm going to believe about it, and I'm going to believe that tonight." She closed her eyes. "You're an auditor. You count other people's tonnes. You don't write their rules. I'm sorry." A breath. "Vultures. I said it the day you came in, and I didn't know how right I was."

Piq heard *not you*. It went into her the way the 14C/I code had: five small characters, and the whole structure behind them. Teo had not missed anything. She had looked straight at the hollow mass and chosen, deliberately, to extend credit against it. That was worse than if she had forgotten.

Piq looked at the screen. The latch-up stood there in plain text, and after it six minutes of Rhee's hands flying a dying ship onto Europa, and after that fourteen months of ice. She was not the one who had denied the claim. She had not written the clause or fitted the box. She had only recited it, ticked it, and sent seven reports to the people who had done all three. In every one of them she had said *Ibis Metrology* when what she meant was *Lagrange Mutual*.

Her own ledger was not balancing. She had kept it since the cycler. Its entries ran *what I said* against *what was so*, and every account she had ever run had been allowed a little float of omission, because omission was not a lie, only a delay. Tonight the float was larger than the account. The figure in the *owed* column had a name. The name was standing four feet away with shaking hands and her sister's water still on her tongue, and it had just apologised to her, knowingly, for something she had done.

Piq knew how this went. She had watched other adjusters do it. You let the anger burn itself out against the institution. You let the person you've been lying to keep thinking you're on their side, because in the way that matters you are. You find the moment, later, when the confession costs less, when the rescue's flown and the claim's reversed and the truth is a story about the past instead of a knife in the present. You do it in the right order.

She had always believed there was a right order. That was the lie under all the others.

"Teo," she said.

Teo opened her eyes.

Piq didn't say it. Not here, under the centrifuge light and the second card, with the flight log still open and the latch-up still on the screen. Not in the room where Teo ate ration bars because her sister told her to. If she was going to break this, she would break it somewhere it could be heard properly, somewhere with Jupiter in it, somewhere Teo had chosen herself the first time she had wanted someone to understand a thing.

"Come to the dome with me," she said. "Tomorrow. Before shift. There's something I have to put on your books, and I want to do it under the old man."

Teo looked at her for a long time. Her hands were still now, but only because she was holding them still.

"Is it going to make this worse?"

Piq thought of the tick in her own hand on page four. She thought of seven grey flags, ACK PENDING, in a queue she had not wanted to read.

"Yes," she said.

Teo nodded, once, slowly, the way she nodded at a count she didn't like but believed.

"Oh-six-hundred," she said. "Don't be late. I'll know if you are."

When Piq let herself out into Ring A, the corridor was empty except for a fine trail of grey grit running along the top of the cable tray toward Ring B and Fitch's patient trap. She stood there for a moment with her slate against her chest and opened her ledger to a clean page. At the head of it she wrote the date and the time, 0600, and under that one line, *Tell her.* She drew no square beside it, and no question mark either. She drew an equals sign, the place where you stop looking. Then she went to her quarters to spend the night failing to rehearse.

## Chapter 11: Billable

At 0412 Piq was awake and doing what she always did when she couldn't sleep. She was counting.

She had nineteen blocks frozen on the hangar floor, twelve on the frame, and one in a lift dripping into a cup. She had seven outbound reports marked ACK PENDING. Amaya's drum count stood at fifty-three, falling by one a night. Her own recalculation put shutdown at day fifty-one, plus or minus three.

Then there were three hundred and seventy metres per second that the *Ninefold Grace* had not had fourteen months ago. Lucky's grumble in the hangar had been three hundred on every Europa job the Mule flew. The Grace had smaller tanks and a heavier frame, and the flight recorder put her figure at three-seventy. Both numbers came from the same box. It was the Clause 14(c) shielding tank, eighteen tonnes of water bolted around the crew compartment nineteen days before departure. The engines had to carry that mass the whole way, and they could never burn it.

And there was the tick on page four, in her own hand. *Day 2, Bay Three. Cl. 14(c) recited in full to tug crew. Objections noted. No exemption granted.* After it came a small, neat check mark. She had been pleased with that check mark.

That tick was where her mind kept snagging.

She lay in the dark of her quarters with the kit sealed beside her and the ledger open on her chest. Its last entry read *0600. Tell her.* with the equals sign after it. She had put the sign there deliberately. It meant she would stop looking for a different answer. She had been looking anyway for four hours.

The door chimed at 0438.

In 0.126 g she reached it in one push and caught the frame with two fingers. Prew stood outside, Director Rook's aide, in a pressed collar with a slate held to his chest. He wore the air of a man who reported to two offices and was afraid of disappointing both.

"Notary Varga. I'm so sorry. We need a seal."

"At four-thirty?"

"Four thirty-eight." He looked at the slate as though it might correct him. "It's interagency. The Correspondence Act. Anything formal that crosses jurisdiction gets a notarial timestamp, and you're..."

"The only notary within six hundred thousand kilometres," Piq said. "I know. Whose jurisdiction?"

"Ur's," said Prew, and he looked haunted.

---

The JDA security office sat at the junction of Rings A and B, behind a pressure door with a reinforced lip. The room was small and very clean. Regolith grit got into everything on Asgard, but it did not seem to get in here. Piq suspected Saar had a seal fitted that the rest of the station could not afford.

Marek Saar sat at the console in shirtsleeves, cuffs turned back twice, a stylus in his hand, with the patient face of a man doing something beneath him. Koskinen stood behind him holding a printout, his clipboard tucked under one arm. Saar's *no* on thermal expansion was still pinned to it.

"Auditor." Saar didn't look up. "Thank you for coming. We are at war."

Piq waited.

"With adjectives," Saar said. "Koskinen. The sequence, for the notary."

Koskinen cleared his throat. He was a careful young man, and he read carefully, as though every word might be weighed later.

"Twenty-three ten, day twenty-five. A communiqué from the Office of the Commissioner, Ur Station, Ganymede, addressed to"—he checked—"'the Honourable Dose Warden of Asgard Yard, with the highest consideration.'"

Piq kept her face still. "To the dose warden?"

"By name of office." Saar's stylus turned once between his fingers. "Not to the Director. Not to the Authority. To the woman who hands out badges."

"Text," said Piq.

Koskinen passed it to her. It was a single page of formal Ur protocol, the kind that read as though it had been composed by a committee of very old courtiers who were afraid of verbs:

*The Commissioner presents his compliments and, mindful of the shared radiological hazards of the Jovian environment, respectfully requests the Asgard dose authority's current operational protocols for surface activity on the trailing hemisphere of Europa, including chaos terrain, together with any guidance on the management of persons of unknown prior exposure. The Commissioner further proposes, under the Interagency Radiological Cooperation Framework of 2201, an arrangement of dose sharing between the respective medical offices, at the convenience of the Warden. The Commissioner renews the assurance of his highest consideration.*

She read it twice. The phrase *surface activity on the trailing hemisphere* sat on the page like a mass with no manifest. Ur's tanker had been carrying three kilometres per second it had no use for, and Ur had bought war-risk cover over the zone. Now it wanted Europa surface tables, and it wanted them from the one person on Callisto who would have the best ones.

*Persons of unknown prior exposure.* She nearly squared that line. Soldiers, she thought. A landing party with no dose history, rotated in from the cheap seats. She squared it.

"Then," said Koskinen, "at zero-one-ten, our response. JDA Notice 2214-SEC-112." He read with real feeling. "'The Authority notes with profound regret that correspondence addressed to Authority personnel must be routed through the Authority liaison, and that the Dose Warden is not authorised to enter into radiological arrangements with foreign offices. The Authority reminds the Commissioner that the Europa zone is subject to exclusive Authority claim, and that any landing by a non-Authority vessel within the zone constitutes an incursion under Article Eleven of the Compact Jovian Accord, which would oblige a Compact response.'"

"Seal on that one?" Piq asked.

"Director Rook's office sealed it under her own stamp," Prew said. "But then."

"Zero-two-oh-four," said Koskinen. "Ur's reply. 'The Commissioner receives with profound regret the Authority's profound regret, and expresses his own deepest regret that the Authority should have found occasion for regret.'"

Piq looked at Saar. Saar was looking at the wall.

"Protocol," Prew said in a small voice, "requires that we match or exceed the register of the preceding communication. It's in the 2198 annex. Otherwise it's construed as a diplomatic slight. Which they could log. And cite."

"So at zero-three-thirty," said Koskinen, "we sent 'the Authority's most sincere and abiding regret.'"

"Abiding," said Saar. "I signed abiding."

"And at zero-three-thirty-four," Koskinen went on, because there was no way to stop him, "they sent 'a regret beyond the Commissioner's power to adequately express.'"

"It's four seconds," Prew said to Piq, as though apologising for the solar system. "To Ganymede. They answer before we've finished the cosignature."

"Which brings us to now," said Saar, "and to you."

The draft sat on the console. Piq leaned over it. In 0.126 g, leaning was something you did slowly or you overshot. Prew had written: *The Authority's regret, which it holds to be without limit, unbounded by any measure, and*

The sentence ended there. The cursor blinked after *and*.

"We are not," said Saar, "going to unbounded."

"Protocol," said Prew.

"I know what protocol says. I am asking the notary." Saar finally turned the chair and looked at her, and his smile was the one from the second day, the one that audited her back. "Notary Varga. Is there a ceiling on register under the Correspondence Act?"

"Not in the Act," Piq said. "Ibis's style guide caps superlatives at three per instrument. Anything above that is held to be decorative and is not notarised."

Koskinen's pen stopped moving.
