"Three," said Saar.

"You've used 'most', 'sincere' and 'abiding'," Piq said. "You're at the cap. If you go to 'unbounded' I can't seal it. It becomes an unsealed instrument, and an unsealed instrument has no standing for the Ur office to cite."

Saar looked at her for a long time. It was the first time she had seen him actually weigh something she'd said, as opposed to her.

"So we exceed," he said slowly, "by subtraction."

"You match the register," said Piq. "You state that the Authority's regret is fully expressed by its previous notice. Then you're compliant and you're done. They can't escalate past a completed instrument without opening a new one, and opening a new one needs a new subject."

"Write that," Saar told Prew.

Prew wrote that. Saar cosigned. Piq set the seal and noted the timestamp, 0452:17. She entered it in the notarial register and copied it to the margin of her ledger, because she always did.

The console chimed at 0452:26.

"Ur acknowledges receipt," Koskinen read, "and has 'nothing further to add to the record at this time.'"

Prew sagged against the wall as gently as a coat on a hook.

"Well," said Saar. "Remarkable. A whole night of courtesy undone by a style guide." He pushed his chair back. "Koskinen, file it. Prew, wake the Director at a civil hour and tell her we've held the line at abiding." He stood, which in his case took a long time, as though standing were also protocol. "Auditor. You're up early."

"You woke me."

"Prew woke you." He took his jacket from the back of the chair. "I've been up all night. So has your transmitter, some nights."

The room didn't change, but the air in it did, the way pressure changes before a seal lets go.

"My uploads go to Ibis on a schedule," Piq said. "Calibration data's large. The relay prefers it at night."

"The relay," said Saar, as though committing the word to memory. "Of course." He shrugged into his jacket. "The Warden will not answer the Commissioner. You'll make sure she knows that. You see her more than most."

"I see her when my badge ticks."

"Then your badge must tick a great deal." He gave her the smile again and went out. The pressure door cycled behind him with a soft, expensive hiss.

Koskinen looked at the closed door, then at Piq, then down at his clipboard. "He's not wrong about the uploads," he said quietly. "Somebody logs them. Bearing and duration. I don't read the bearings." He swallowed. "I just watch the floor."

"Thank you, Koskinen."

"I didn't say anything."

"I know," said Piq. "That's what I'm thanking you for."

---

It was 0521 when she reached Ring A. That left thirty-nine minutes.

She went to her quarters first and did not open the ledger. Then she opened it.

She had written the confession out the night before, in order, the way her father had taught her to read a manifest aloud at a weigh station. You gave the gross weight first, then the tare, then the contents, and the exceptions last, because the exceptions were what people remembered:

1. Employer: Lagrange Mutual. Division: Special Losses.
2. Handler: A. Pell, L4.
3. Assignment: the 'throw' fragment. A cabal, a weapon, or fraud.
4. Credential: Ibis. Genuine. Cover.
5. The kit. What it does.
6. Reports. Seven. What they say.
7. R7. 'Any means.'
8. Page 4.

She read it once and hated it. It was correct and orderly, and it was a list. She knew Teo would hear it as a list, because that was what it was. Then she tore the page out, and that was worse, because the ledger was sewn and the stub showed. She put the torn page in her breast pocket anyway. She could not bring herself to leave a confession behind as unlogged mass.

Before she closed the ledger she turned back to page four and looked at the tick for a while. It was small and confident, the mark of someone who believed the rule was the reason people came home.

At 0547 she was in the long ramp up to the dome, taking it in the slow loping stride the Asgard-born used, which she had nearly learned. The ramp's lights ran amber at night cycle. Grit had collected along the seam of the floor plates in a fine dark line. Someone had drawn a small cat in it with a fingertip and then, apparently out of conscience, drawn a little box around the cat with *4.1* written inside.

At the top the pressure door opened on her own badge. The dome was for anyone. It was the only free thing on the station.

---

The dome was a shallow blister of sintered regolith and borosilicate, half buried in the berm, its glass layered so thick that the outside reached her softened, as though through water. The benches were old shipping cradles bolted to the floor. The air was a degree colder than the ring, and it smelled faintly of ozone from the electrolysis plant two levels down, the clean edge that meant somewhere ice was being turned into breath.

Jupiter hung where Jupiter always hung on Callisto's leading hemisphere, low over the rim of the Valhalla basin, and it did not move. Up here a day lasted sixteen Earth days and the planet never rose or set. It simply sat on the horizon, enormous and patient, its bands drawn across a sky with no air in it. The light it threw was banded too. It lay across the frost on the basin floor in faint stripes of cream and rust, as though the ground had been ruled for a ledger and nobody had written in it yet.

A small bright point stood just off the planet's western limb.

Europa. It was a day and a bit from going behind, by Piq's clock. Gap six would open at about 0742 tomorrow, and the old man would swallow it for thirty hours. For now it was there, as near and small as a pin in a map, and somewhere on it four people were breathing air made from heated ice and watching a counter fall.

Teo was already there.

She sat on the farthest cradle with her back half to the door and her knees drawn up, her grey cable-insulation hair tie pulling her hair back hard, a flask of tea held in both hands. The cracked brass dosimeter hung face-in against her collarbone. She did not turn when the door opened. She lifted the flask an inch in acknowledgement, and Piq noted the gesture and could not get the notation to stop. *Arrived first. Brought tea. Brought two cups.*

There were two cups on the cradle beside her.

"You're on time," said Teo.

"I said I would be."

"You say a lot of things you mean." Teo poured the second cup without looking at it. The tea left the flask in a slow amber rope and took its time finding the bottom. "It's the other kind I've started wondering about."

Piq sat. The cradle was cold through her trousers. She took the cup because refusing it would have been its own statement, and she was trying to make only one statement this morning.

"Before you start," Teo said. "Ur wrote to me."

Piq's hand did not move on the cup. "I know."

"Of course you do." Teo slid a slate along the cradle between them. The Commissioner's page was on it, the same text, but with a different routing header: *MEDICAL / RADIOLOGICAL — FRAMEWORK 2201 — AUTO-COPY*. "The Framework copies radiological correspondence straight to the named medical office. It's a twelve-year-old routing rule. Saar's people forgot it exists, or they never read it. I had this at twenty-three twelve. I've been reading it all night."

"So have they," Piq said. "Saar's office. They spent the night trading regrets with Ganymede. I sealed the last one."

"I heard you were up." Teo touched the slate and the text enlarged under her finger, one line held under the light. *Persons of unknown prior exposure.* "Do you know what that phrase is?"

"Soldiers," Piq said. "A landing party with no logged history. Rotated crew."

"It's intake language." Teo's voice was level and professional, the voice she used across a desk. "That's what I write when someone comes in off a breached hull and I don't know what they've already taken. You don't call your own crew 'unknown prior exposure.' You know your own crew to the microsievert. That's the whole point of having a crew." She took her finger off the slate. "And nobody plans a surface walk in chaos terrain. Chaos is where you go when you have no choice. Either Ganymede means to put bodies on the worst ice on Europa and wants to know how long they'll last, or..."

She stopped. The *or* hung there, like Prew's sentence on the console.

"Or what?"

"I don't know." Teo picked up the flask again. "That's what bothers me. I like knowing. I'd like to answer him and find out."

Piq looked at the pin of light off Jupiter's limb. Then she said what she had been sent to say, because she had promised herself she would leave nothing out this morning, including the parts that made her look small.

"Saar says the Warden will not answer the Commissioner." She heard how flat it came out. "He told me to make sure you knew."

Teo turned her head and looked at her properly for the first time since the door had opened.

"He told you."

"He sent me. In a manner of speaking."

"So you came up here at five fifty-nine with Marek Saar's message in your mouth." It wasn't loud. "I brought two cups."

"That isn't why I asked you here."

"Then why did you?"

Piq set the cup down on the cradle. In this gravity it settled with a delay, a small soft knock a moment after she let go, and the tea shivered and went still. She reached into her breast pocket and took out the torn page. It had been folded twice, and the sewn edge was ragged where it had come away from the stub.

"I wrote it down," she said. "So I'd say it in order."

Teo looked at the page and said nothing, so Piq began.

"One. Employer: Lagrange Mutual. Division: Special Losses."

Teo went still. It was not the stillness of someone listening. It was the stillness Piq had seen in people at weigh stations when a number came up on the scale that they had known would come up and had hoped wouldn't. Her hands stayed on the flask. Her eyes stayed on Piq's face. Even the dosimeter at her collarbone seemed to stop catching the light.

"Two," Piq said. "Handler—"

"Stop."

Piq stopped.

"Don't read me a manifest." Teo's voice had gone very precise, each consonant set down separately like a sample in a tray. "I've watched you for twenty-six days. I know how you do it. Gross, tare, contents, exceptions. If you read me that page I'll hear every line as a weight, and I'll know you picked the order so the worst of it comes last, when I'm too tired to hit it properly. Put it away."

Piq folded the page and held it, because she did not know where else to put her hands. Then she put it back in her pocket.

"All right," she said. "Without the list."

She told it plainly, and it came out badly, the way true things usually did.

She worked for Lagrange. Not for Ibis, not really, though the Ibis credential was genuine and she had earned it in a dull week at a dull desk on Ceres. She was what the society called a loss adjuster and what anyone else would call a spy. Whoever insured the ships knew where the ships were, and Special Losses existed to know the rest. Her handler was a man named Pell at L4, fifty light-minutes away, and she had never heard him use an adjective. She had been sent because of a fragment of intercepted traffic, *throw before conjunction, no one will see it until it's done*, and because Lagrange feared a weapon on the Sling or a fraud on the depot. The 340 tonnes were real. The kit in her quarters was real. It was 1.3 kilograms of receiver, encryption and burst transmitter that she had declared at the weigh-in as audit reference masses, while Teo stood two metres away refusing her a dosimeter waiver.

"That receiver is how I heard Amaya," she said. "It was built to listen for the cabal. There wasn't a cabal. There was your sister."

Teo said nothing.

"I've sent seven reports since the night we decoded her. All seven say four alive. All seven say the drum. All seven are marked acknowledgement pending, and the replies I get back read as if I never sent them. The last order I had from Pell..." Here was the place on the list she had meant to reach last. She reached it in the middle instead, because Teo had taken the order away from her. "It said Ur had bought war-risk cover over the zone, and that I was to stop any unlicensed launch by any means."

"Any means," Teo said.

"Those were the words."

"Does that include the Mule?"

"It would. By the words."

"Does it include Hesper? Lucky? Me?"

"I haven't done anything with it," Piq said. "I haven't answered it. I've been writing back telling him he's wrong."

"That isn't what I asked."

Piq made herself hold Teo's eyes. "I don't know what I'd have done with it on day five. I know what I'm doing with it now."

Teo didn't answer that. Piq hadn't expected her to.

"There's one more thing," Piq said. "It was the last line on the page. Page four."

"Your ledger."

"My ledger." The banded light lay across both their hands. "On my second day here I stood in Bay Three in front of a tug crew who were complaining about water blankets, and I recited Clause 14(c) to them in full. Every subsection. Minimum shielding water on any Europa-bound vessel. One of them said the blankets cost her three hundred metres per second a run and she'd rather take the dose. I told her the clause was the reason crews came home. I told her it as gospel. I meant it as gospel." She breathed in, ozone and cold. "Then I went back to my quarters and wrote it down, *Clause recited, objections noted, no exemption granted*, and I put a tick next to it. I was pleased with that tick."

Teo's knuckles had gone pale on the flask.

"Eighteen tonnes," Piq said. "That's what it was on the Grace. A tank fitted nineteen days before departure, because a Lagrange inspector told them to. It cost them three hundred and seventy metres per second they didn't have. That forced them onto the slow path through the torus. The dose latched up the flight computer, and Lagrange wrote it up as pilot error. Some other adjuster in some other bay ticked that tank, the same way I ticked mine. I don't know who. It might as well have been me. It's the same hand."

The dome was quiet except for the circulation fans, a breath going in and out somewhere above the glass.

When Teo spoke again, she sounded almost gentle, and that was worse.

"You'll want this numbered," she said.

"Teo..."

"No. You started a list. I'll finish it for you." She set the flask down. It took its slow low-g second to land and made no sound at all. "One. Day one, intake. You asked me for a dosimeter waiver. I thought you were vain about a badge. You wanted it off because a badge logs where the wearer goes. Was that billable?"

Piq said nothing.

"Two. Day nine, my office, the consultation. Your reading had ticked up and you came in charming about it. You asked me what the Mule's reactor was putting out at the hangar door. I told you to the decimal, because you asked like a woman frightened for her own marrow. I gave you the shielding geometry. I drew you a diagram on the back of a requisition." Her mouth moved, not quite a smile. "You kept the diagram. I watched you fold it. Was that billable?"

Piq had promised herself she would leave nothing out. That promise did not stop at the parts she said first.

"Yes," she said. "It went into report two. The reactor class. The hangar."

Teo nodded once, as though a reading had come in exactly where she'd predicted. That was worse than if she had flinched.

"Three. Day ten. Here." She did not gesture at the dome. She didn't need to. "I told you Europa ducks behind the old man every four and a half days and the dosimeters sing differently when it does. I told you I had a sister. I've told four people on this station about Amaya in fourteen months. You were the fourth, and you were the only one who didn't say sorry. You asked what she did on the Grace. I liked that. I thought it meant you could stand to hear the answer." Her voice did not rise. Each word was set down separately. "Billable?"

"The occultation note went in," Piq said. "Amaya didn't."

"Why not?"

"Because it wasn't relevant to the file."

"So it was assessed." Teo turned the phrase over like a sample she meant to keep. "And found not relevant. Four. Day fourteen. My office, after hours. I showed you her last dose log. I had never shown it to anyone outside the board of inquiry, and the board looked at it for nine minutes. You looked at the spike for forty. Your hand was on the desk and mine was on the desk, and neither of us moved." She held Piq's eyes. "You knew the Grace's claim had been denied before you ever landed. It's in your society's files. You sat across from me and let me tell you how Lagrange had called my sister's pilot a fool. Billable?"

Piq could have said that she hadn't read the claim file until day twenty. It was true. It was also an exception, and Teo had told her what exceptions were for.

"I knew the claim was denied," she said. "I didn't know why. I let you tell me."

"Five. Day nineteen, two in the morning. We sat on your floor with the receiver between us and I read my own keying back to myself in my sister's hand. FOUR ALIVE. You put your jacket round my shoulders. I've been trying all night to work out whether you did that because I was shaking or because I was the only person on Callisto who could read the code." Her voice caught for the first time, a small hitch, like a valve seating, and then it was level again. "Which was it?"

"I don't know," Piq said, and that was the most honest thing she had ever said aloud. "I'd like to tell you the first. I don't trust myself enough to swear to it."

"Six. Day twenty-two. The tank farm. We wrestled a valve for twenty minutes in a vacuum suit with a bladder trying to kill us both, and you laughed. I'd never heard you laugh. You logged that run as audit fieldwork. I saw the form."

"It was audit fieldwork."

"I know. That's the point." Teo picked up her cup at last, looked into it and put it down again untouched. "Seven. Day twenty-four. The airlock."

The fans breathed overhead. Out past the glass the frost lay ruled in cream and rust and nothing moved on it.

"Ballast came out of the duct," Teo said, "and we both laughed and stepped back, and I went home and lay awake and thought, she was going to, and so was I, and the cat saved us both from something stupid. That's what I thought. That it would have been stupid." She looked out at Europa. "Was that billable, Piq?"

"No."

"How would I know?"

Piq had no answer to that, because there wasn't one. That was the whole shape of it. She had built every conversation on this station to bear a load nobody else could see, and now she had nothing to point to that would prove any one of them had borne nothing.

"Eight," Teo said quietly. "Exceptions last. You see? I can do it too." She stood. In this gravity it was a slow, deliberate unfolding, and she did it without touching the cradle. "Eight. This morning. You came up here with a list in your pocket and Saar's message in your mouth, and you told me the truth because you'd decided to, and you put an equals sign after it in that book so you couldn't change your mind. I know you did, because that's exactly what I'd have done." She clipped the flask to her belt. "That one I can't price. I've tried. I'll keep trying."

"Teo."

"I'm answering the Commissioner." She picked up the slate. "Dose tables for chaos terrain, the management of persons of unknown prior exposure, and an open line under the 2201 Framework. Saar can file a regret about it. He's got one left before he reaches the cap." She turned toward the door, then stopped with her back to Piq and Jupiter's light banded across her shoulders. "Your uploads go out at night. He logs the bearings. Koskinen told me that too, two days ago. He tells everyone eventually. If Saar asks me what you are, I'll tell him you're an auditor, and that's true, and it's all he'll get."

"Why?" Piq asked. It came out barely a sound.

Teo didn't turn around. The pressure door opened for her badge, because the dome was the only free thing on the station.

"I won't burn you," she said. "I'm not you."

## Chapter 12: Chain of Custody

At 0730 the badge on Piq's collar read 0.191 Sv lifetime. She knew this because Teo read it aloud.

"Zero point one nine one," Teo said, and wrote it in the column. "Ring C background twelve point four microsieverts a day. Dome excursion this morning, forty-one minutes, negligible. You were in the dome until six-ten."

"Six-twelve."

Teo didn't look up. "Six-ten. The door logs it." She wrote *6:10* and initialled it. Grey cable-insulation tie, reading glasses low, the cracked brass dosimeter turned face-in against her sternum. There was no flask on the desk. Piq had noticed that before she sat down and had spent the first four minutes trying not to look for it.

The office was the one behind the blue door at the end of Ring A. It was as cold as it always was. The card was still on the shelf, NEITHER ARE DOSES, in Teo's square capitals, and the requisition pad sat beside it with the top sheet torn off. Piq had the matching sheet in a plastic sleeve in her quarters. It had Teo's reactor geometry on the back, and its contents had gone into report two.

"Any symptoms," Teo said.

"No."

"Nausea, fatigue outside sleep debt, petechiae."

"No."

"Sleep debt."

Piq thought about it. "About nine hours across three days."

Teo wrote *9h* in the margin with no inflection at all. "You'll need a weekly blood draw if you go above zero point two. You're not above it. I'm noting it because you will be." She capped the pen. "That's all."

That was the whole consultation, and it was correct in every line. Piq stood up. Low gravity drew it out the way it drew out everything, the rise taking longer than any decision should. She stood with one hand on the chair back while the room settled around her.

"The Commissioner," she said.

"I sent it at ten past seven." Teo turned to her terminal and typed with two fingers. "Chaos terrain dose tables for trailing hemisphere, mean and shadowed. Management of persons of unknown prior exposure, intake protocol rev. nine. And a standing offer under Framework 2201 to exchange real-time dosimetry with Ur Medical on any surface operation. I copied the Authority liaison as the Framework requires. The Authority liaison is Prew. Prew reports to two offices. You know all this."

"I put Haddad's request in last night's batch to L4," Piq said. "The twenty-three hundred upload. I thought you should hear that from me."

"Before I'd answered it."

"Yes."

"You delivered it first." Teo looked at her over the glasses for exactly as long as it took to say it. "I noticed."

Piq had nothing to put in that column. Haddad's letter had come to the dose warden, in the dose warden's name, about the dose warden's work. Piq had read it over Teo's shoulder, gone to her quarters and filed it to her employer six hours before Teo replied. She had told herself it was a matter of transit time and the relay's batching. That was true, and it was also the kind of true she had built a career on.

She went to the door. The seal there was cheap and gritty and hissed when it broke. Behind her the pen scratched once more, and she didn't know what Teo had written.

It would have been easier if Teo had shouted. An accusation could be answered, contested, partially conceded and closed out with a note. Teo had made no accusation. She had recorded the dose to the minute and corrected the timestamp. She had listed the symptoms and capped the pen, and she had treated Piq exactly as she treated every other badge on the station, which was scrupulously and from a great distance. There was no line in the ledger where Piq could post a reply. The account was simply closed to her, and every figure in it was correct.

---

The day ran on the station's arithmetic regardless. At 0900 Ring B's potable reserve came up six litres short. The cause was a cracked galley fitting and a cook with a soup pot, and Piq logged it as informal return-to-stock rather than fine anyone.

At 1015 Fitch came down the Ring B corridor at a low-g jog with the spring-trap under one arm. It was a mesh box with a load cell bolted underneath and a tablet taped to the lid.

"Four point zero nine eight kilograms," he said, and held the tablet up to her face. The trace showed a flat line, a soft-edged spike, then a slow decaying settle. "Junction fourteen, oh-three-twelve. It ate the bait and left through the bars. I want it certified."

"I can certify that your cell registered a four point zero nine eight kilogram transient," Piq said. "I can't certify what caused it."

"It was the cat."

"Was the cat on the manifest?"

His mouth opened and shut. She sealed it as a calibration event, and he went back up the corridor looking into every louvre he passed.

When she turned toward the ramp there were two people in grey Authority coveralls between her and it.

---

The taller one was Officer Danso. Piq had his name from a dock manifest and had never heard him speak. The other one she didn't know. Danso held a hand terminal out flat with a document on the screen, the way a man offers a dog something to sniff.

"Piq Varga. Under Authority Ordinance Six you're detained for interview. Maximum six hours, extendable on the Security Chief's signature. You'll surrender personal electronics for the duration."

Piq read the document. She read the whole of it, the citation and the hold period and the blank where a reason ought to be, because the act of reading was the only thing in the corridor she controlled. The ordinance number was correct. The signature block said *M. Saar* in a hand that sloped forward.

"My audit terminal is under notarial seal," she said. "It goes with me, sealed. You can hold it. You can't open it."

Danso looked at the other officer. The other officer looked at nothing.

"Bring it sealed," Danso said.

They walked her down the ramp to Ring D and through the pressure door that separated Authority offices from everything else. She noted the turns. She noted that Danso walked on her left and kept half a step back, and that the other one carried her terminal in both hands like something that might spill. Nobody touched her. The corridor lights in Ring D were a degree whiter than the residential rings and colder on the skin.

The room was three metres by three. It had a table bolted to the floor, two chairs bolted to the floor, and a clock set into the wall above the door, so the detainee had to lift her eyes to read it and the interviewer never did. 1031. The air was dry, scrubbed of the station's usual ozone, and her own breath was the loudest thing in it. Danso set her terminal on the table between the chairs with its seal tape facing up, red and unbroken, and left.

She sat. In 0.126 g she sank into the chair slowly, and once she was down she kept her hands flat on the table where they could be seen.

At 1047 Saar came in carrying nothing.

He took the other chair the way a man takes a chair he owns. Grey coveralls like the others, but pressed, with the creases standing. He looked at the seal on her terminal for a long moment and then at her.

"Ms Varga."

"Chief Saar."

"You've had a busy month." He folded his hands. "I'd like to go through it with you. Not the audit. The audit I've read. Your reconciliations are filed to the Authority registry every evening, and they're very good. I've learned more about where this station keeps its water from your filings than from eleven years of my own inspections."

She said nothing. A compliment opened an account, and she would not be the one to post the first entry.

"The station runs a spectrum monitor," he said. "It isn't secret. It's in the safety annex, page forty. It exists so that nobody keys a transmitter near the Sling's capacitor banks and puts a slug into the regolith three kilometres short. It logs every emission inside the pressure boundary. Band, duration, origin to within about four metres." He recited it the way she recited clauses. "Since your arrival it has logged thirty-four emissions from your quarters. Six from the observation dome. Two from a maintenance alcove in Ring C, behind the scrubber stack, where there is no reason for anyone to transmit anything. Burst traffic. Compressed and wrapped in a commercial carrier the monitor can't open. Last night at twenty-three hundred you sent eleven point six megabytes."

"Eleven point six two," Piq said.

"Thank you."

"It wasn't a correction. It's the size of the file. If you tell me the size of every file, I'll tell you what was in each one." She kept her hands where they were. "Ibis Metrology requires a daily evidentiary upload from any certified auditor working in the field. Mass reconciliations. Seal logs. Photographs of every weighing, with the load cell's serial in frame. Ibis certifies my figures to the underwriters, and the underwriters set Asgard's rates. When the upload is late, Ibis assumes the auditor has been bribed. I've never been late."

"From the scrubber alcove."

"The signal in my quarters is poor. The alcove has a line to the main antenna trunk. You can test that yourself."

"I have," Saar said. "It does." He let that sit. "And the reception. The monitor logs reception too, where it can. It has you listening for long periods on a band nobody on this station is licensed to use."

The clock read 1052. She did not look at it.

"Ask Ibis," she said.

"I intend to."

"Then you should know what you're asking for. Earth-Luna is forty-nine light-minutes today. Your query reaches Ibis's L4 office in forty-nine minutes if it goes direct, and it doesn't go direct. It goes through the Mars relay. The relay is batching commercial queries at thirty-one hours now and rising, because conjunction is close and everything without a priority flag waits behind everything with one. Someone at Ibis has to read it, find my supervising auditor and pull my upload certificates. Then the answer comes back through the same queue. You'd be lucky to have it in four days." She paused exactly long enough to be fair. "The relay goes dark in eleven."

"I can flag it priority."

"You can. Authority priority traffic is read by the Authority's L4 desk before anything is forwarded. They'll want to know why the Security Chief on Asgard needs an Ibis auditor's upload certificates confirmed ahead of the conjunction freight manifests. You'd have to put that in writing to the people who review your budget." She had worked this out at four in the morning a week ago, sitting in the dark of her quarters. It sounded worse said aloud in a dry room. "Your answer comes back after the window closes either way. Until then, what you have is an auditor sending audit files."

Saar unfolded his hands and laid them flat on the table, a mirror of hers. It might have been deliberate. It looked like a man getting comfortable.

"Then I'll open the terminal," he said. "Here. Now. And we'll both know."

"It's sealed under the Compact Notarial Act, section nine. I'm a sworn notary, registered with the Compact registry at Luna. The seal on that terminal is the chain of custody for every mass figure I've certified since I stepped off the shuttle." She turned her right hand over and touched the tape with one finger, the only movement she had made since sitting down. "If you break it, the chain breaks. Every certification behind it becomes uncertified evidence. That includes the depot reconciliation the Director signed, the Ring B water figures and the tank farm transfer."

"I'm aware of how seals work."

"It also

## Chapter 13: Backlog

Ring D let her go at 1131 by its mechanical clock. Piq had come to resent that clock. She took the long corridor back toward the spoke and did not let herself hurry. On her right hip the cover terminal sat sealed under the security tape Saar had decided not to break. Under her sternum the dose badge kept recording whatever Ring D's private scrubber loop had been doing to the background. She would read it later, the way she would read everything later.

Before the interrogation began he had made her sit for forty minutes with nothing but the sweep hand for company. His hands were long and uncalloused, the nails cut square. They were hands that had never hauled a crate or cracked a frozen valve. His coveralls had been pressed to a shine, which meant an iron, and an iron meant a power draw that appeared on no laundry allocation she had ever audited. Halfway through, the tape had nearly come off the terminal. She had stopped that by pointing out that her seal sat on the Authority's last regret notice to Ur Station. That was the one she had attested two weeks ago at 0452, because the escalating exchange of courtesies between the JDA and Ur Station had grown formal enough that both sides wanted a neutral notary. She was the only one within six hundred thousand kilometres. Saar had wanted the record uncontestable. Then he had discovered what uncontestable cost him.

At the end, with the clock reading 1129, he had said it plainly, as though reading her a fee schedule. He had signed the ruling that called the Ninefold Grace pulses an automated echo. He could not let four people exist who would make that signature a lie. Then he had told her she could go.

She had gone. Now, in the spoke lift, rising through the slow pull of a tenth of a gravity, she did the accounting he had not done. He could not verify her uploads before the window closed. Every packet she had sent through the station uplink looked like routine audit traffic, padded out to standard block length with filler so that no one counting bytes could tell a mass reconciliation from a report to Special Losses. Fifty minutes out to Earth-Luna and fifty back. He would need two full rounds to confirm anything with her nominal employers at Ibis Metrology. He did not have two rounds. The Mars relay would go dark in a little over thirty hours.

That was the alibi, and it had held. Somewhere between Ring D and the spoke lift she understood it was also a problem. She had not thought it through, because it had always worked in her favour.

Her reports were hard to verify because they were hard to find. She had made them hard to find on purpose.

The lift doors opened onto Ring B and its ozone. The electrolysis stacks were running high for the end of the shift, and the air tasted faintly of struck flint. A maintenance tech was chasing a dropped torque driver along the deck. It turned end over end at the speed of a thought you were trying not to have. Piq stepped around them both and went to find Ruairi Danso.

---

The comms shack was a converted pump room on the outer curve of Ring B. Its walls still carried pipe brackets that no one had found worth the mass to remove. Danso had hung his jacket on one and his headset on another. He was eating something from a pouch that had been labelled lentil before the label wore away.

He had explained the Mars relay to her on her second day, cheerfully and at length, the way people on Asgard explained things they suspected no one else cared about. She had cared. She had written it down. Then she had treated it as background, the way you treat the floor.

"You look like you've been in Ring D," he said.

"Is that a look?"

"Flat. Everyone comes out of Ring D looking ironed." He swallowed. "What do you need? I've got about thirty hours of relay left, and half the station wants to send its mother a birthday message before the dark."

"I want to talk to the relay operator. Not the queue. The person."

Danso put the pouch down. "Nobody talks to the operator."

"There is an operator."

"There's a person at Deimos who signs the logs, yes. But you don't talk to her. You send into the queue, the queue sorts you, and the relay forwards what it forwards. Talking to the operator means a priority-class header, and nothing on this station has one except the Director's emergency channel and the Authority's diplomatic traffic." He stopped, then looked at her the way people on Asgard looked at a mass anomaly in a duct. "Which you've notarized."

"Which I've notarized," she said. "The relay has my seal on file, because every regret notice the Authority sent Ur in the last fortnight went out carrying it. Under the Correspondence Act a notarial instrument that carries a statutory deadline gets Class A handling. Conjunction is a statutory deadline for any claim under an inner-system policy. I'm a notary. I can make an instrument."

"About what?"

"About my own outbound traffic. A notarized protest of non-delivery." She set her cover terminal on his bench and peeled back the corner of Saar's tape just far enough to free the data port. It left a faint sticky residue on her thumb. She did not intend to explain the tape. "I am attesting that the following sixteen packets were lodged with the station uplink on the following dates. I am requesting the relay certify their status. The certification has to come from the operator. Instruments need a human signature on both ends."

Danso stared at her for one long second. Then he laughed, short and unwilling. "That's the most auditor thing I've ever heard."

"Thank you."

"It wasn't a compliment. But it'll work." He pulled his headset off its bracket. "Callisto to Mars is about thirty-four minutes one way right now. Call it seventy-plus for a round trip if she answers straight away, and she won't. She'll be drowning. Everyone in the system is shoving traffic at her before the dark."

"Then I'll wait."

"You'll wait in here?"

"I'll wait in here," Piq said. She sat on an upturned spool of fibre that someone had logged as furniture, and began composing the instrument.

It took her nine minutes. She kept it short, because the relay judged messages by length as well as class, and that thought had begun to sit in her stomach like a swallowed bolt. She listed her sixteen outbound packets by date and size. She attested under seal that each had been lodged. She requested certification of delivery or non-delivery. At the bottom she added a sentence that was not strictly instrument language: *If any of these remain in your queue, I request their release under this seal before conjunction hold.*

Danso read it over her shoulder. He could not help it, and she did not stop him.

"Sixteen," he said. "All padded."

"All padded to standard block."

"Every one of them a fat padded packet." He rubbed his eyes. "I told you, the first week. The relay deprioritizes big padded packets as conjunction comes on. They look like bulk. Somebody's backup. Somebody's ten thousand photographs of a load cell."

"You told me," she agreed.

She pressed the seal into the terminal's notarial field. It was a physical die in a physical housing, which the Act required. Old law, written for people who did not trust anything they could not hold. The die gave its small mechanical click, the instrument closed, and Danso sent it.

---

For the first forty minutes she worked, because she did not know what else to do with her hands.

She opened her own outbound log, the true one. It lived inside the hollow comms unit she had declared at the shuttle port as audit reference masses, and she laid it out in two columns. In the left she put what she had sent and when. In the right she put what Pell had said back and when it had arrived. She had never done this. She had kept the two streams in separate folders as though they were separate conversations. Each of his messages answered something. She had simply assumed that it answered her latest.

The left column went like this.

Day 3. A short report, padded. Pulses on a retired dosimetry band, gaps of about thirty hours every four and a half days, consistent with a shift roster. *Assess as coordinated activity, possibly throw cabal.*

Day 6. Short, padded. A question about how to log a contraband cat. She had written it at two in the morning, when it had seemed both reasonable and funny.

Day 8. Long, padded. The Mule. The hangar. The Brigade.

Day 14. Very long, padded until it was as fat as a slug of ice. *The transmitter is on Europa. Four alive. Reactor drum sixty days. This is not a cabal. Recommend immediate revision of all prior assessments.*

After that came a dozen more, each longer than the last, each full of delta-v and dose figures and the flight recorder file. Each carried the quiet, rising panic of a woman who could not understand why her handler kept writing as though she had said nothing.

The right column went like this.

Day 12. *Identify throw cabal. Treat depot crew as hostile.* She had read that order on Day 12, two days before she knew about Europa, and it had seemed reasonable.

Day 16. *Ur has bought war-risk cover over the claimed Europa zone. Stop any unlicensed launch by any means.* On Day 16 she already knew. Her Day 14 report had said, in so many words, that the launch was a rescue. She had read *any means* and felt the floor tilt.

Day 21. *Report status of throw cabal.* As though Europa had never been mentioned.

Day 22. At the wedding, with Lucky and Ziva's vows colliding across three seconds of delay, Pell's earnest answer to her two-in-the-morning cat question had arrived. *Re: feline. Recommend Schedule F, livestock, non-consumable.* She had laughed. She remembered laughing. She remembered thinking that the cat question must have jumped the queue. She had not asked herself why the cat question could jump the queue when Europa could not.

The cat question had been small. That was why.

Her messages had been sitting in the outbound queue this whole time, and every one carried a small grey tag, ACK PENDING. She had looked at those tags for a week and filed them as the normal sullenness of a relay approaching conjunction. ACK pending meant the relay had not yet confirmed delivery downstream. It had been telling her, packet by packet, exactly what was wrong, and she had decided the instrument was miscalibrated because the reading was inconvenient.

She sat on the fibre spool and looked at the two columns. A low-g cough of air from the vent stirred the hair at her temple. The arithmetic had already closed. She just had not let herself read the total.

"You've gone grey," Danso said.

"I'm reconciling."

"Does it usually go grey?"

"Only when it balances," Piq said.

Something heavy and furred landed on the pipe bracket above Danso's jacket, sending a slow puff of regolith dust drifting down through the shaft of the lamp. Ballast looked at them both with the patience of an animal that had never once been declared. Then the cat reached out one paw, considered Danso's lentil pouch, and withdrew the paw again, unimpressed.

"Four point one," Piq said, mostly to herself.

"What?"

"Nothing. Schedule F." She looked back at her columns. "Livestock. Non-consumable."

---

The operator answered at seventy-eight minutes. That was faster than Danso had predicted and slower than Piq could stand.

The message came in Class A, so it arrived first in its frame and alone, a brief stack of plain text under a Deimos relay header. It was signed CELESTINE AHN-MBATHA, OPERATOR, DEIMOS STORE-AND-FORWARD, and it read like a woman who had been awake for two days and had not expected anyone to address her by job title.

> Notary Varga. Instrument received and certified under your seal. Answering in human because you asked in human, which nobody does.
>
> Your sixteen packets. Four delivered: your Day 3 and Day 6 shorts, plus two small housekeeping uploads. Twelve remain in queue as of this writing. They are your large padded uploads, oldest first, and they never cleared bulk-class sorting.
>
> I want you to understand why, because you're going to be angry with someone, and it shouldn't be me and it shouldn't be the machine.
>
> As we approach conjunction the relay's bandwidth to inner system drops. Priority traffic goes first: navigation, medical, diplomatic, legal, anything with a class header. Then small packets, because small packets are cheap and usually human. Then bulk. Bulk is identified by size and by entropy profile. Padded packets look like bulk. That is what padding is for. You padded your uploads to standard block so that nobody counting bytes could tell them apart from backup traffic. Nobody could. Including us.
>
> You disguised your reports as unimportant, and the relay agreed with you. Every time a priority frame came through, your packets dropped a place in line. Two of your largest have been in this queue for eleven days.
>
> Under your seal I am releasing all twelve now, at Class A, to their downstream addressee. I am also holding the inbound frame for your addressee's responses so that it goes to you whole before hold, because inbound to Callisto from that addressee is backed up for the same reason. Expect it in one burst. It will not arrive in order. Nothing arrives in order this close to the sun.
>
> Hold begins at 1704 Deimos time tomorrow. That's 1612 yours, approx. After that, nineteen days of nothing.
>
> I hope whatever it is was worth padding.
>
> C. A-M.

Piq read it twice. She did not reread things. She read it twice.

*Including us.*

There was a word for a control that works exactly as designed and still produces a loss: an operational loss. She had written that phrase into claim files twenty times. She had never once written it about herself.

Danso was quiet for a long moment. "Your boss," he said at last, "has been getting the short ones."

"My boss has been getting the Day 3 report and a question about a cat."

"Your Day 3 said cabal."

"My Day 3 said cabal." She heard her own voice, level and dry, the voice she used for tug crews disputing a manifest. "Every order he's sent me in two weeks was written to a woman who thinks there's a weapon on Callisto. He's been answering the last thing I told him. The last thing I told him was wrong."

"So he's not—"

"I don't know what he is. I've been arguing with him as though he could see my ledger." She closed her mouth, then opened it again, because the next part had to be said or it would sit in her like the bolt. "He couldn't. He was looking at a page I'd torn out."

---

The inbound burst arrived at 1940. Twelve of her packets had gone out at 1702, so it had been enough time for them to cross to Deimos, cross again to L4, reach Pell, and for Pell to do whatever Pell did, which apparently took less time than she would have guessed. Then the operator had sealed the frame and sent it back across thirty-four minutes of dark.

Danso had gone off shift and come back with two bulbs of tea. He had a strong feeling, he said, that something was about to happen in his pump room that he would want to tell people about later, and he wanted to be able to say he had watched.

Nine messages, one frame. The relay had sorted them by size and class, smallest first, exactly as it always did. Piq did not read them in that order.

She stripped the headers and laid them out by L4 timestamp. She did not open a single body until she had them sequenced. It was the discipline her father had taught her on the cycler: weigh the crates before you open them, or what's inside will tell you how heavy it is.

Then she read them in order.

**1.** *Day 13, L4 time. Re: feline. Further to my earlier response, Schedule F requires vaccination certificate or veterinary attestation in lieu. If neither is available, notary may self-attest. Pell.*

She was the notary. Pell had told her, eleven days ago, that she could certify a cat on her own authority. She looked up. Ballast had relocated to the top of the comms rack, where the warm exhaust came out, and was washing one paw with enormous care.

"Absolutely not," she told the cat. Danso coughed tea onto the bench.

**2.** *Day 14, L4. Report status throw cabal. No update received since your Day 3. Pell.*

This was the same message she had received on Day 21, the one that had made her want to throw the terminal at the bulkhead. This was its first send. A Day 21 resend would have meant someone at L4 who knew nothing. A Day 14 send meant only someone at L4 who had not heard anything yet.

**3.** *Day 15, L4. Your silence is noted. Assume communications compromised. Do not attempt priority channels, which are monitored. Continue standard uploads. Pell.*

He had told her to keep padding. He had told her to make her reports look exactly like the thing the relay would bury. He had done it to protect her.

**4.** *Day 16, L4. Ur war-risk purchase confirmed over JDA-claimed Europa zone. Assess as preparation for armed incident. Stop any unlicensed launch by any means. Pell.*

This was the order that had made her want to burn him. She held it now next to her left column: a man at L4 with a week-old report of a cabal on Callisto, a confirmed war-risk policy over Europa, and an adjuster who had gone silent. *Any means* was a terrible sentence. It was not a cruel one. It was a man telling her to keep her head down in a world he could see only one week of.

**5.** *Day 17, L4. Disregard previous re feline vaccination. Schedule F amended. Cats now Schedule G, companion organism, effective last quarter. Apologies. Pell.*

Danso put his head down on the bench. His shoulders shook.

"It's not funny," Piq said.

"It's a little funny."

"It's a little funny," she conceded. Ballast, on the rack, did not consider it funny at all and turned to face the wall.

**6.** *Day 18, L4. Expense query. Your intake fine for 1.3 kg overage has posted to Special Losses as "reference masses, audit." Accounts asks what was referenced. Do not answer this. Pell.*

**7.** *Day 20, L4. No ACK from you on my 3 through 6. Relay shows your end pending. Concerned. If you are able to send anything, send it short. Pell.*

*Send it short.* He had worked it out. On Day 20 he had looked at his own queue, seen the same grey tags she had been ignoring, and told her the one thing that would have fixed it. Like everything else, the instruction had been small enough to go through only once she had already stopped listening for it.

She looked at that one for a long time. Her own ACK pending flags had been his flags. They had both been watching the same instrument from opposite ends of the solar system. Each had read it as the other's silence.

**8.** *Day 26, L4, received 1758 L4. Your Day 8 report arrived this hour. Mule, Brigade, hangar. Revise cabal assessment pending further. Holding all orders. Pell.*

Her Day 8 upload had reached him today. It was nineteen days old. He had read about the Mule as though it were news, because to him it was.

**9.** *Day 26, L4, sent 1839 L4. Twelve of your uploads have arrived in the last forty minutes under notarial release. I have read the Europa report and the flight recorder summary. I have read all of them.*

*The existence of four survivors of Ninefold Grace has now reached me and I have logged it with the Society.*

*Lagrange Article 31, the salvage clause, can fund recovery of insured crew as salvage of a covered asset. I have invoked it. You have funding authority. That is all I can give you from here.*

*All prior orders are rescinded. I wrote them to someone you were not anymore. I am sorry for that.*

*Help them. You'll be alone for 19 days.*

*Pell.*

She read it a second time, and then a third. That made two things today she had read more than once. It was getting to be a habit.

Then she went back to the top of her own column and looked at it again.

Day 3: *Assess as coordinated activity, possibly throw cabal.*

That was her sentence. For two weeks she had been fighting a man who disagreed with her about whether four people were alive on Europa. She had built him in her head as cold, as actuarial, as the kind of handler who would see four survivors as a liability on the Society's balance sheet. She had carried him that way through every delayed order. All that time he had never disagreed with her about Europa, because he had never heard of Europa. He had only ever argued with her Day 3 report. She had been arguing with him on its behalf. Her opponent had been a ghost of her own earlier thinking, written down, padded, and sent out into the queue to wait.

That was her flaw, and she knew its exact shape, because she had been told it in performance reviews in language softer than this. She trusted her own numbers so completely that she assumed everyone was reading from the same page. She had built a ledger and then lived as though it were the world. She had believed the world had a copy. The world did not have a copy. The world had nineteen-day-old fragments of a copy, sorted by size.

"So," Danso said gently. "Is he the bad guy?"

"No," she said. "He's the one who's been reading my mail late."

---

She found Teo in the dose office on Ring C, as she had known she would. The dose warden did not leave the dose office the night before a relay hold. Hold meant nineteen days with no outside medical consults, no Earth-side oncology reads on anyone's blood panel, no second opinions. Teo would sit up and recheck every resident's running total, because the rules were the only thing that had never lied to her.

The door was open. It was always open. A warden who closed her door was a warden people stopped bringing their badges to.

Teo did not look up. "If it's your badge, leave it on the tray. Ring D runs on a separate scrubber loop and its regolith cover's deeper than ours. You'll read low. I'll log it in the morning."

Since the dome, it had been like this between them. Teo had not reported Piq to anyone and had not burned her, exactly as she had said she would not. She had been scrupulously, coldly professional. She had cleared Piq for the hangar, logged her doses without comment, and looked at her the way one looks at a reading on a badge one does not trust.

Piq unclipped the badge and put it on the tray. Then she put the terminal beside it, open to message nine.

"I'm not asking you to forgive anything," she said. "I'm asking you to read this. It came in an hour ago."

Teo looked at the terminal for a long moment without touching it, as she might regard a sealed source. Then she pulled it across the desk and read.

Piq watched her read and did not try to translate it. She knew Teo's face well enough by now to read its micro-movements, though not well enough to know what they added up to. Teo's mouth went still on the third line. At *I have invoked it* she put one fingertip on the screen, the way she would touch a dose figure she was about to correct. At *I wrote them to someone you were not anymore* her eyes went up to Piq's and then back down.

She read the rest of the nine too, all of them, in the order Piq had sorted them. She read the cat messages without smiling. She read the expense query without smiling. Piq had expected nothing else. At message seven, *send it short*, she stopped for a while.

"Your reports were buried," Teo said finally. "Because you padded them."

"Because I padded them to look like nothing."

"So for two weeks he's been telling you to shut down a cabal."

"There was never a cabal. I told him there was. On Day 3. Before I knew." Piq kept her hands still at her sides. "Every order you heard me quote came from that."

Teo closed the terminal. She did not slide it back.

"I want to be careful," she said, "about what I say next. Because I'm angry, and I've been angry for a long time, and I don't trust it."

"All right."

"You lied to me. For weeks. About what you were and who you were sending things to." Teo's voice was low and level, warden's register. "That's still true. Your handler turning out to be a person doesn't make it less true."

"No."

"And it was your employer's clause that put my sister in the ice."

"Yes."

Teo looked at the tray, at the badge, at the small orange figure on its readout that she had not yet logged. "But you came to me with the pulses," she said. "Before you knew they were her. Before you knew they were anyone. You had a handler telling you to treat the depot crew as hostile, and you broke cover far enough to bring a dose warden an unknown signal on her own old band. You didn't know what I'd do with it. I could have walked straight to Saar." She paused. "That cost you something. I've been pretending it didn't, because it was easier to be angry at one thing."

Piq said nothing. Saying anything would have been asking for more than had been offered.

"It doesn't fix it," Teo said.

"I know."

"I'm not—" Teo stopped and restarted, precise as a recalibration. "I'm going to log your badge now. I'll want your Ring D exposure separated from your hangar time, because you've been near that reactor again and I'm not combining those."

"I'll get you the timestamps."

"You'll get me the timestamps." Teo pulled the badge toward her, slotted it into the reader, and watched the number come up. Eleven point four microsieverts for the room. Piq had guessed eleven. For one second something moved across Teo's face that was almost the beginning of the thing it used to be. Then it was gone, filed, logged.

"Article 31," Teo said, without looking up from the reader. "It's real? He can actually do that?"

"He has done it. It's logged with the Society."

"Then Lagrange is paying to bring her home." It was not quite a question. Teo's hand had gone still on the reader.

"Lagrange is paying to bring her home," Piq said. "If we can get there."

Teo finished logging the badge. She held it out across the desk, and Piq took it. For half a second their fingers were on the same small square of plastic. Neither of them moved. Then Teo let go, and Piq clipped it back to her sternum, face out, as the rules required.

"Nineteen days," Teo said.

"From tomorrow at 1612."

"And the drum."

"Thirty-nine days on the reactor drum, from Amaya's last count."

Teo nodded slowly. "Then I'm going to need your delta-v ledger by morning," she said. "All of it. If Lagrange is paying for a rescue, I need to know how much dose it costs, per person, per minute, before anybody gets near the Mule." She turned back to her screen. "Goodnight, Varga."

It was the first time in eleven days she had used a name.

---

Piq did not sleep. Sleep had begun to seem like something other people could afford.

She went back to the true terminal. Under Pell's message nine she wrote a reply of four lines, short, unpadded, under her notarial seal: *Received. Understood. Working. Short from now on.* She sent it at 0210 and watched the tag. Within the hour it changed from ACK PENDING to DELIVERED, DEIMOS. It was the first of her messages to make that journey cleanly in two weeks. It would reach L4 before the dark. He would know she had heard him. That was something. It was the one thing she could give him from here.

At 0340 her covert receiver caught Amaya's pulse on the old dosimetry band, faint, on schedule, in the keying Teo had taught her sister when they were girls. Europa was still clear of Jupiter's limb for another eleven hours. Piq decoded it at her bench with the receiver warm in her hands. It was the only one left on Asgard, now that Saar had the Brigade's.

DRUM 39. DOSE HOLDING. ICE LOUD TONIGHT. TELL HER.

She sat with that until the stacks cycled again and the ozone came up through the vents.

At 0800 she gave Teo the ledger. At 1100 she gave it to Hesper and Lucky in the hangar. It was still one hundred and forty metres per second short of returning to Callisto with four extra bodies aboard. The figure was the same as it had been for a week, except that now there was money behind it and nowhere to send a complaint.

At 1611 she was back in Danso's pump room, because it seemed like the place to be.

The relay status line on Danso's display had been green for as long as she had lived on Callisto. At 1612:00 it went amber. A plain-text notice from Deimos came through, the operator's signature on it one last time:

> DEIMOS STORE-AND-FORWARD ENTERING CONJUNCTION HOLD. ALL INNER-SYSTEM TRAFFIC SUSPENDED. NEXT SCHEDULED CONTACT: T+19 DAYS. — C. A-M.

Then it went grey.

Danso let out a breath he seemed to have been holding since lunch. "Well," he said. "That's us. On our own."

Piq did the sum without wanting to. Nineteen days of silence. Thirty-eight days on the reactor drum as of this morning's count. The relay would come back with nineteen days of drum still burning under the ice, if nothing in the ice went wrong, and a great many things in the ice could go wrong. Europa's next clean window from Callisto opened in sixty-one hours. After that came another, every four and a half days, and each one cost the people on the surface another slice of the drum.

The station address system chimed: three ascending tones, the security register. Every speaker in every ring carried it at once. Danso's headset buzzed faintly against its bracket. Up on the comms rack, Ballast lifted its head.

"This is Marek Saar, Jovian Development Authority Security, Asgard." The voice was level, unhurried, pressed flat. "Effective immediately and for the duration of solar conjunction, all launches from Asgard Yard are suspended under Authority emergency statute. This includes the Sling, all surface pads, all hangar egress, and all vessel movements, crewed or uncrewed. Ice transfers require a security countersignature. Unlicensed departure will be treated as hostile action against Authority infrastructure. This order remains in force until relay contact is restored." A pause, precise to the second. "Thank you for your cooperation."

The tones descended. The ring went quiet. Somewhere below them the electrolysis stacks went on splitting water into breath.

Nineteen days of dark. Thirty-eight days of drum. Sixty-one hours to the next window. And the only man on Callisto who could sign a launch had just made sure no one would.

Piq looked at the grey status line and then down at the notarial die in its housing on her belt. It was physical and old, the kind of thing an older law had trusted because you could hold it.

"Danso," she said. "Who else on this station has to obey a seal?"

## Chapter 14: Custodian of Record

The question hung in the comms shack long enough for Ballast to finish turning his back on both of them.

Ruairi Danso sat with his knees against the rack and the lentil pouch still in one hand. Piq had come to think of the look he wore as his relay face: polite and patient, a man used to answering questions that had arrived out of order.

"Who has to obey a seal," he said. "Everybody who wants their mail, apparently."

"Besides the mail."

"Courts. Inheritance clerks. Anyone selling a ship." He rubbed his nose with the back of his wrist. "The Authority, when it wants its own paperwork to stand up. You saw that this morning."

She had. Her seal sat at the bottom of a regret notice Saar had needed to be beyond contest. In the interrogation room the most dangerous man on Callisto had stopped short of breaking her terminal tape, because breaking it would have cracked the foundation under his own document. A seal was a small thing that larger things rested on.

"I need the Authority Asset Ordinance," she said. "The whole thing, with schedules. Not the residents' summary."

Danso already had a hand on his keyboard. "You know it's fourteen thousand pages."

"Then I'll need tea."

"The tea's in the potable allocation. You'd need a countersignature."

She looked at him. He looked back. Three hours ago Marek Saar had made every gram of moving ice on the station subject to his office, and nobody had yet decided whether a kettle counted as moving ice.

"I'll go without," Piq said.

---

The Ordinance did not run to fourteen thousand pages. It ran to fourteen thousand two hundred and six, because the Authority had amended its definition of *asset* nine times since Asgard was dug. Each amendment was appended rather than merged, so the history of the Jovian Development Authority's opinions about property lay in strata, like the regolith over the rings.

Piq read the way her father had taught her to weigh a hold. First the gross: index, schedules, definitions. Then the tare: everything that was only procedure, only the weight of the container. Then the contents. Exceptions last.

Danso watched for twenty minutes, fidgeted for ten, and then started narrating, which she gathered was how he fidgeted.

"Schedule C is vehicles. Schedule D is vehicles that used to be vehicles. Schedule E is the argument about whether a Sling slug is a vehicle while it's still on the rail."

"Is it?"

"Depends who's paying for the rail. There's a footnote. My predecessor said the Ordinance was written by a committee that believed every object in the universe was secretly trying to escape taxation."

"Your predecessor was right." Piq marked her place with a fingertip. "Here."

*APPENDIX F. ASSETS UNDER NOTARIAL IMPOUND.*

It was four pages long, which in this document counted as a haiku.

*F.1. A notary registered under the Correspondence Act and holding current audit standing may, on a finding of material discrepancy in the declared mass, provenance, or valuation of an Authority-registered asset, place that asset under impound pending certification.*

*F.2. An asset under impound shall not be moved, modified, disassembled, fueled, defueled, or otherwise altered except under the direction of the impounding notary or a custodian of record named by that notary.*

*F.3. Impound is effected by the physical application of the notary's seal to the asset's registration plate and to its primary access, and by entry in the Authority asset register.*

*F.4. Assets under impound are held in neutral custody and are outside the jurisdiction of operational offices (see Schedule of Operational Offices, Annex 7) pending certification or release.*

*F.5. The impounding notary shall, within the impound period, produce a reconciled certification of the asset's mass and provenance, including all materials incorporated into the asset since its last registered weighing. A seal applied to an unreconciled ledger is void ab initio, and the notary shall be liable under Section 40.*

*F.6. Release: on certification, to the registered owner or a custodian of record.*

She read it twice. Danso read it once over her shoulder, very slowly, and she could hear him working out the shape of it.

"You want to impound a ship."

"I want to impound a ship that is about to be scrapped for security reasons." She did not say which ship. He was a comms tech on a station of nineteen hundred people, and he knew. "F.2. Nothing can be done to it without me. Not defueled, not disassembled."

"Not launched."

"Not launched by anyone I haven't named."

Danso sat back. Ballast, on the exhaust grille, flicked one ear as if he too had a stake in the matter.

"Annex 7," Danso said. "Operational offices. Is security one?"

She had already gone looking. The cross-reference opened a page that said *Annex 7 is maintained separately by the Office of the Administrator and is incorporated by reference.* Below it was an empty frame where a document had once been linked. The Office of the Administrator had last touched the link six restated quarters ago, during the same period it had cut the depot sunshade budget to zero.

She drew a question mark in her notebook. It was the honest symbol: *I do not know this yet.* Beside it she wrote *F.4, Annex 7, Rook's office*.

"So you don't know whether the seal keeps Saar's people off it."

"I know it keeps everyone else off it. I know he can't defuel it without breaking F.2. Whether he has to obey it, or only should, is a different question."

"That's a big difference."

"It's the difference between a wall and a sign." She thought of Saar's ironed coveralls, and of the tape he had not broken. "He respects signs more than most people. He likes them to be his. If all I get is a sign, it still buys a day."

Danso was quiet. Then he said, carefully, "The nine messages. Your boss. He's the one paying for whatever this is?"

She had decided on the answer during the walk down to the shack, and she found she had decided correctly. "He's paying. He's not here, and he won't be for nineteen days."

"Right." Danso nodded several times. "I won't say anything."

"You'll say something," Piq said. "You'll say it at the canteen, to one person, quietly, because it's a good story and you watched it happen. I'm asking you to wait until it's over."

He had the grace to look caught. "How long?"

"The window opens at 1612 tomorrow. Twenty-three and a half hours."

"I can do twenty-three and a half hours," Danso said, the way a man says he can hold his breath.

---

The lockdown had already changed the station's sound.

Asgard ran on ice. It came up from the depot, through the melt loops and filters and down the potable lines, and the station heard it constantly without listening: the knock of a valve in the wall, the long sigh of a transfer pump cycling up two rings away. Walking Ring B's curve, Piq could hear the gaps where a pump sat idle because nobody would sign its order.

At Junction 12 a gardener from Gallery Two stood next to a four-litre ice bucket with a printed form in her hand. Piq recognised her as the lettuce woman at the same moment the gardener recognised Piq.

"Countersignature," the gardener said. "For irrigation. Lettuce doesn't care about conjunction."

"Lettuce doesn't care about anything."

"One point three seven kilos of it cared about you." The gardener lowered the form. "Forty minutes I've been waiting. One officer signing for the whole ring, and he reads every line. He asked me whether the bucket was going to Europa."

Piq had nothing useful to add, so she said, "He'll get to you," and kept walking. Behind her she heard the bucket set down, the patient scrape of an object that would wait as long as it had to.

---

Teo's door was open. It was always open. Piq had once read that as a dose warden's policy and now understood it as a statement of character: *anyone can come in, and I will be here, and I will have the number.*

Teo was at her terminal in her reading glasses, typing with two fingers, with a mug of tar-dark tea at her elbow that had no business existing under the new regime. She did not look up.

"Your Ring D timestamps."

Piq set the slip down: entry 0938, exit 1131, the interrogation room's minutes broken out. Teo slid it under her keyboard without reading it, which in her dialect meant she believed it.

"You haven't slept."

"I've had a busy relay."

The corner of Teo's mouth moved. She took the glasses off and looked at Piq the way she looked at a badge read that had come in a little high. "What is it?"

Piq had carried it for more than thirteen hours, through the blackout tones and Saar's announcement and fourteen thousand pages. She had kept it folded behind her sternum, where she kept things she had not yet decided how to book. She had told herself she was waiting for the right moment. That was a lie of omission told to herself, and she had a professional's contempt for those.

"There was a pulse at 0340," she said. "Before the dark. Amaya's mark on the end."

Teo went still. It was the stillness of someone holding a full cup in low gravity, knowing the slightest motion would send the contents out in a slow, silver, unrecoverable sheet.

Piq laid the tally sheet on the desk, with the raw pulses above and her decode below in square capitals.

DRUM 39. DOSE HOLDING. ICE LOUD TONIGHT. TELL HER.

Teo put two fingers on the last line, the way she touched a dosimeter face in bad light.

"She always did that," she said. Her voice was perfectly level. "When she was fourteen and I was nineteen and Mum was on nights at the clinic. She'd leave me a pulse on the old maintenance band, two walls apart. *Fed the plants. Done homework. Tell her I'm fine.*" A breath. "She'd have me pass it on, like I was the relay." She took her hand back. "You didn't bring this at 0340."

"No."

"Why?"

*The blackout. The Brigade. Being let out of an interrogation room.* Every one of those was true, and every one was tare.

"Because I didn't know which column to put you in," Piq said. "Yesterday you weren't speaking to me. I couldn't tell whether bringing you her words was something I had the right to do or something I'd be doing to get back into your good books. I couldn't tell them apart, so I did nothing. That was worse."

Teo looked at her for a long time.

"That," she said, "is the most honest thing you've said to me that you weren't paid to say."

It was not forgiveness, but it went in a column Piq could read.

"I want to answer her."

"Europa's clear tonight. The spectrum monitor logs every emission. Each time I key, I give him a bearing."

"Then it'll be short. She taught me short. I taught her short." Teo wrote in the margin in the six-by-six bunk-grid shorthand their mother's clinic had once used for badge codes: HEARD. COMING. HOLD DOSE.

"Coming," Piq said.

"I'm not going to key her a hedge." Teo set the pencil down. "Also, Haddad answered. An hour ago. I gave him the public surface tables last night, copied to Prew, so Saar has it too. Here's his follow-up."

Ganymede was not behind the sun. Ur's packets crossed the Jovian system in seconds and never touched Mars. Piq read the message, a tower of courtesies built around one question.

*The Commissioner would be grateful for the Dose Warden's professional estimate of cumulative exposure for a surface party of three persons, duration not exceeding four hours, in chaos terrain of the Conamara type, assuming crawler shielding of the standard Ur pattern (eleven centimetres borated composite), together with any guidance the Warden may offer on the sequencing of non-ambulatory persons.*

Conamara was where the Ninefold Grace had come down. *Non-ambulatory persons.* Piq filed both phrases under *Ur* and *threat*, because that was the column she already had open.

"He's planning a landing in the Authority's zone," she said.

"He's asking how not to kill people." Teo's mouth thinned. "I'll give him the tables. The sequencing I'm still thinking about."

"Non-walkers first," Piq said, before she meant to. "Heaviest dose last."

Teo looked at her sharply.

"It's your triage plan. I relayed it to them."

"Yes." Teo looked back at Haddad's message like a woman reading a dosimeter she did not trust. "Yes, you did."

---

The alcove behind the Ring C scrubber stack smelled of ozone and old filter media, warm from the electrolysis cells on the other side of the wall, where water became breath. Piq threaded the antenna lead out through the light-pipe coupler. Teo knelt beside her, a little too close, because the alcove was built for one person and a scrubber.

Piq held the key out. Teo looked at it.

"If I key it, she gets a message," Piq said. "If you key it, she gets you."

Teo keyed slowly, two-fingered even here, and ended on a doubled final pulse. Piq logged it: 2246, nineteen seconds. Somewhere a spectrum monitor was writing down that someone had transmitted from Ring C on an unlicensed band. That made three emissions from the alcove, and each one narrowed the cone.

"Varga," Teo said. It was the second time that day. "You're giving him bearings for me."

"For her. And for me." Piq coiled the lead. "It's all on the same ledger."

Their fingers did not touch when the key passed back. Piq noted the not-touching with the precision of someone who had been keeping a running total and was beginning to lose count.

---

At half past eleven on the first night of the lockdown, the hangar sounded like a church that had lost its funding.

The Mule sat in its cradle under the work lights, a fat ugly hull in a broken coat of hand-cut ice. Twelve blocks were on, and nineteen more stood in a ragged row off the port skid. The stockpile had wept and refrozen, and a translucent rind glazed the floor. Koskinen's cup sat under the drip from Juno's block, full to the brim.

Eleven people sat on crates and tool lockers and an upturned bucket. Teo stood by the reactor bay with her badge face-out. Hesper sat on the edge of the cradle, ankles crossed, waiting for a test result she already knew.

Lucky was standing. He did not appear capable of anything else.

"They put a man at the door. An actual man. He asked me what was in my supper."

"What was in your supper?" asked Rafe.

"Ice! It was frozen! It's a *frozen burrito*!" Lucky rapped the hull three times. "He wants a countersignature for a burrito. This is how civilisations end."

"Lucky," Hesper said.

"I'm setting the mood."

"The mood is set." Hesper looked at Piq. "Numbers."

"Two things first," Piq said. "You'll want to throw me out after the first. Hear the second before you do."

Koskinen, who leaked everything to everyone, put a hand over his own mouth in a gesture of preemptive restraint that Piq estimated would last until breakfast.

"I hold the Ibis credential, and it's real. Everything I've weighed for you is weighed true. But I came here for Lagrange Mutual, Special Losses. I was sent to find out what 'throw before conjunction' meant. My handler thought it meant a weapon. I thought it meant you."

The silence had mass. It settled on the hangar floor like dust in 0.126 g, slow and thorough.

"Lagrange," Abara said. "The ones who denied the claim."

"Yes."

Achterberg stood up from his crate. He didn't come toward her. He stood as if sitting had become a statement he no longer wanted to make. "You were in here counting our blocks. And sending it to them."

"Yes. For a while they didn't get it. My reports sat in a relay queue at Mars. My handler spent eleven days answering a version of me who still thought you were building a bomb. Last night he got all of it: the pulses, the drum, the four names." She set her slate on a crate where everyone could see it. "Before the dark came down, he invoked Article 31, the salvage clause. Recovery of insured crew counts as salvage of a covered asset, and Lagrange pays. I have funding authority. It's real money, and it's on my seal."

Lucky had stopped moving. That told Piq more about the room than anything she could have measured.

"So the company that killed them," Rafe said slowly, "is paying to bring them back."

"That's the second thing," Teo said from the wall.

Every head turned. Teo did not speak in Brigade meetings. She stood in them like a load-bearing column, and people checked their badges when she was in the room.

"Clause 14(c) locked eighteen tonnes of the Grace's water out of her engine," she said. "That forced her onto the slow road through the torus. The torus froze her flight computer. Rhee flew her down by hand. Lagrange calls that pilot error. Saar calls the antenna in the ice an automated echo. I've checked it three times."

Lucky said, very quietly for Lucky, "Three hundred metres a second."

Piq looked at him.

"Every Europa job, Lagrange's blankets cost me three hundred metres a second. I said it in front of you." He looked at the Mule as if it had betrayed him personally. "I thought it was a joke. It's the joke. It's the whole joke."

On the cradle, Hesper had not moved. Then she said, in her ordinary engineer's voice, "I did the retrofit."

Nobody else in the room had known. Piq could see it land on them one by one.

"Nineteen days before departure. Lagrange stamp on the work order. I put the shielding in, I balanced the mass, I signed it. I trained Rhee. I trained Amaya. I did the margin myself."

"You did the margin right," Piq said. "You booked the water as reserve, the way our template told you. Somebody at L4 changed the clause and not the template. That error is ours. I recited that clause in Bay Three like scripture, so it's on my ledger too."

Hesper's eyes came up, flat and dangerous. Piq saw that being absolved had not helped. It had only cleared the ground for something worse.

"Then here's mine," Hesper said. "I put them on that road. So I'm flying her."

"I'm the pilot," Lucky said.
