"You're the pilot. I'm going. You'll need someone who knows that reactor with her eyes shut, and I'm not sending anyone down there to clean up my arithmetic." She stood. "That's settled. The other thing isn't." She turned to the room. "You can throw her out. She'll go. She won't report you, and there's nobody to report to for nineteen days anyway. Or you can keep the only person here with funding authority, a notary's seal and the only working ear to Europa, while four people sit in the ice with about thirty-eight days of drum."

"Thirty-eight as of tomorrow's count," Piq said automatically. "Thirty-nine at 0340."

"See," Hesper said to the room. "Can count."

The silence came back, distributed differently.

Koskinen took his hand off his mouth. "I'm going to have to tell someone about this eventually."

"After," said six people at once.

Lucky sat down on the bucket as if his legs had filed a separate opinion. "Okay. We have money now. What do you buy with money? Do we buy delta-v?"

"You can't buy delta-v," Piq said. "You can only buy mass, and spend it."

"That's the saddest thing I've ever heard."

"Here's a sadder one." She opened the ledger. "The Mule dry, fifty-one tonnes. Crust, about 21.8 when the last block's on. Crew and consumables 4.2. Survivors 2.4 with Teo's medical margin. Isp nine hundred seconds. Callisto to Europa, land, lift, back to Callisto: 3.32, 3.59, 3.59, 3.32. With them aboard, we're a hundred and forty metres per second short on the return."

"Less ice?" Juno said.

"Then you're under 14(c), and Teo grounds you."

"14(c) set the wrong number," Teo said. "The torus doesn't care what the clause says. Under eleven centimetres of composite and twenty-one tonnes of ice, a surface party takes a survivable dose. Under twelve, it doesn't. I won't put a crew into the torus with less water than physics wants."

"Then we need a different road," Hesper said.

Piq thought of a line she had written in her ledger a week ago and crossed out: *EUR to GAN?* She thought of an Ur tanker loitering with far more propellant than any routine run needed. She had those entries in separate columns, each labelled *threat*.

"I don't know yet," she said. "There's a question mark."

"She uses question marks," Teo told the room. "It means she'll find out."

---

The meeting broke the way Brigade meetings broke: people drifted back to blocks and pumps as if work were the only adequate response to anything. Lucky went to the cockpit to talk to the instruments, which Piq gathered was his version of prayer.

Hesper caught her by the reactor bay. She pulled a battered paper ledger from her coveralls, the sewn kind with a cardboard cover, a tally book from the depot floor.

"Every block," Hesper said. "Every haul, every kilogram we took off the depot since the first pulse. I wrote it down so that when they hanged us they'd hang us for the right number."

The running total ran down the right margin in small, engineering-neat figures and closed on the last page with a double line.

*244.1 t.*

Piq read it three times.

"The gap in the depot ledger is three hundred and forty."

"I know what the gap is. We didn't take the rest. I've been in that depot every shift for six years. Nobody moves ninety-six tonnes past me with a forklift and a prayer. Nobody took it."

Ninety-six tonnes. The number arrived in Piq and sat down. It was specific and real and unaccounted for, the kind of figure that stopped an audit dead.

She thought of F.5. *A seal applied to an unreconciled ledger is void ab initio.* The Mule was built of depot ice. To certify the Mule, she had to reconcile the depot, and the depot had a ninety-six-tonne hole in it that no one had dug.

"I'll give it back," she said, and tucked Hesper's ledger inside her own.

"Don't hang us with it."

---

At 0118 Piq sat on her bunk with the two ledgers open side by side.

*Depot gap: 340 t.*
*Brigade (Okafor-Lund): 244.1 t.*
*Unaccounted: 95.9 t.*

She drew a square beside it: *later*. Then she rubbed it out with her thumb, because later had run out.

Things she had filed began to come back like slugs arriving on trajectories she had thrown long ago.

The first was the frost she had seen through the shuttle port on the way down, glittering on the sunward face of the depot where no frost should be.

The second was a budget line in Rook's restated quarters, depot sunshade maintenance, cut to zero in the year the colony was repriced.

The third was the residents complaining about humidity, on a station that electrolysed every drop it could find.

She laid the three side by side, and they stopped being three.

The shade had failed. With nothing between the sun and the sunward face, that ice was warmer than any ice on the depot had been designed to be. At Callisto's pressures warm ice does not melt; it goes straight to vapour. The loss was a thin, steady sublimation over a face the size of four Sling cradles, running for six quarters. The vapour did not go far. It refroze wherever it met something colder: the broken shade struts, the lee of every rib, the shadowed seams. That was the frost she had seen, ice robbed from the sunlit face and redeposited where the sun couldn't reach. Whatever didn't refreeze was drawn into the depot-side intakes and pushed into the station's air as damp.

Nobody had taken ninety-six tonnes. The sun had, one molecule at a time, and someone had seen the dashboard and said nothing.

She wrote *sublimation, shade = 0* and drew a line to *95.9 t*. Beside it, very small, she wrote *Rook*.

The PA chimed with the three ascending security tones, and Saar's office spoke, giving the clock time before every sentence like a man initialling each line of a contract.

*0131. Notice. Under Authority emergency statute, the Office of Security will inspect Hangar Four at 0600. The inspection will include reactor and propellant systems of any vessel present. Personnel without inspection duties will vacate the hangar by 0545. Obstruction will be treated as hostile action against Authority infrastructure. Notice ends.*

Four hours and twenty-nine minutes to the inspection. The window opened at 1612, ten hours after that. *Propellant systems.*

She put both ledgers in her breast pocket, clipped her badge face-out, and went to find out whether Ilse Rook was the kind of woman who slept.

---

She was not. The light under the Administrator's door on Ring A was the colour of a terminal, and when Piq knocked, Rook said "Come," as though she had been waiting for someone, though not this person.

Rook sat in a cardigan with a cup of something gone cold. On her screen was the depot schedule. Piq had seen her glance at it in every meeting since the first, like a woman checking a mole she had promised herself she would have looked at.

"Auditor. It's half past one."

"The sunshade," Piq said, and sat down without being asked.

Rook did not pretend not to understand. That was the first thing Piq booked in her favour.

Piq laid it out: the frost, the zero line, the humidity logs she had pulled off Danso's board on the way up. Then Hesper's ledger, open to the double line, with her thumb over the name. "Three hundred and forty missing. Two hundred forty-four point one accounted for, by people I won't name to you tonight. Ninety-five point nine unaccounted, which is what an unshaded face of that area loses to sublimation over six quarters. You knew."

Rook turned her cup a quarter turn on its saucer. "I knew the shade was failing. I didn't know the number."

"You didn't look for the number."

"No." The warmth in Rook's face had not gone, but it had nothing behind it now. "We were being repriced. A disclosed depot loss on top of that is a rate class. A rate class is forty families on the next shuttle out because the colony can't carry them. I thought I'd fix it in a quiet quarter."

"There hasn't been a quiet quarter in six."

"No," Rook said again.

"I'm going to tell you what I am," Piq said, "because you'll find out by lunch. I work for Lagrange Mutual. Special Losses."

Rook went grey around the mouth.

"So here's the arithmetic. If I find a concealed loss, it's misrepresentation, and misrepresentation voids your cover. Every ship and slug and person on this station uninsured on the first day the relay comes back. If you disclose it to me tonight, as a notary, it's late notification. Late notification is a premium adjustment. The rate goes up either way. The ice is gone either way. The only thing you still get to choose is which of those two it's called."

Rook looked at the depot schedule for a long time, the way someone looks at a thing they have been looking away from.

"What do you want?"

"Your signature on the sublimation finding, so the depot reconciles. And Annex 7."

Rook frowned. "The operational offices schedule? That's my predecessor's. I don't think it exists anymore."

"It's incorporated by reference. If it doesn't exist, that's another finding."

Rook stood, went to a cabinet that had not been opened in some time, and began going through physical folders. Paper had mass, and on Callisto people kept it only when they wanted something to outlast a database purge. Piq watched her and did not offer to help. Some things a person has to find herself.

At 0412 they had the finding drafted. At 0447 Rook signed it. Her hand was steady. Piq countersigned, and the depot ledger closed for the first time in six quarters: 244.1 to unauthorised transfer into an Authority-registered asset, 95.9 to sublimation, 340.0 total. The ice had never left Authority property. It had only changed which Authority object it was part of. Piq wrote that sentence very plainly, because someone was going to read it under oath.

Rook was still in the cabinet when Piq left.

---

At 0545 the Brigade filed out of Hangar Four past a security officer with a slate. At 0600 Saar's inspection team filed in: four people in pressed coveralls with a cutting rig on a trolley.

Piq went in at 0604 on her notary standing, which no one had yet thought to suspend. The officer at the door checked his slate, failed to find her on it, and failed to find a reason to stop her.

They did not inspect the reactor. They went straight to the propellant manifold where the depot feed came through the hangar wall, a braided line as thick as a thigh, frosted where it ran cold. One of them clamped it on both sides of the coupling. Another fitted the cutting collar.

"Inspection finding," the team lead said to his recorder. "0618. Propellant feed to unregistered fueling operation presents a hazard to Authority infrastructure. Isolating."

The collar sang. The line parted with a soft cough of vapour that hung in the low gravity like breath on a cold morning and took nearly a minute to settle. The Mule's tank gauges stopped climbing. Piq watched the needle on tank C sit at sixty-one percent and stay there.

She walked to the Mule's registration plate on the forward skid and pressed her seal to it. Wax would have been theatre. The seal was a thumb-sized polymer stamp that took her print and the date and fused to the metal with a sound like a kiss. She climbed the cradle ladder to the main hatch and did it again. Then she entered the impound in the Authority asset register from her slate, citing F.1, *material discrepancy in provenance*, and attached the reconciled certification signed at 0447.

"What is that," said the team lead.

"Appendix F," Piq said. "This vessel is under notarial impound. Under F.2 it may not be modified, defueled or disassembled except under my direction. I'd ask you not to touch it."

He called Saar.

She had assumed it would end there, with a sign and a man who respected signs, and a day bought before the emergency statute rolled over it. She was counting on the day. Saar would invoke the statute. It would take him hours of paper. Hours were what she had.

Saar arrived at 0631, unhurried, ironed. He held out his hand for her slate as though it were already his, and she gave it to him.

He read Appendix F. Then he read it again from the top. Piq watched his eyes stop on F.4 the second time and stay there.

"Annex 7," he said, "is not linked."

"It is now," said Ilse Rook from the hangar door.

She had changed out of the cardigan. She was carrying a paper folder with a coffee ring on it and a digital copy on her own slate, and she handed him the slate. "Schedule of Operational Offices, current as of the third restatement. I've restored the link to the register. Item four."

Piq had not seen item four. She read it over Saar's arm.

*4. Office of Security.*

The Authority's own Ordinance placed an impounded asset outside the jurisdiction of operational offices, and the Authority's own schedule named Security as one. The emergency statute ran through Saar's office. Saar's office could not reach the Mule.

She had meant to put up a sign, and it had turned out to be a wall. She booked it honestly: it had worked for the wrong reason.

Saar read Annex 7 twice as well. When he looked up, his face showed nothing she could audit.

"Release on certification," he said.

"Certification is attached. Reconciled at 0447 and countersigned by the Administrator."

"Then the asset is released to its owner, which is the Authority."

"F.6. On certification, to the registered owner *or a custodian of record*. The impounding notary names the custodian." She took her slate back. "I haven't released it yet."

He looked at Rook. "You signed a sublimation finding at five in the morning."

"At a quarter to five," Rook said. "I'm precise."

For a long moment the only sound in the hangar was the drip into Koskinen's cup.

"You understand," Saar said to Piq, at the pitch he had used in the interrogation room, "that a seal is a promise. When you break it, and you will, because there are four corpses on Europa and you can't make them anything else, I'll be the one reading the Section 40 liability to you. Slowly. Both times."

He handed the team lead his own slate, said "Stand down," and left. His team took the cutting rig with them, and the severed feed hung from the wall, frosting.

---

"Restore it," Rook said to the depot ops board on her slate. Her voice was the one she used to approve canteen menus. "Feed to Hangar Four. Authorising under audit custody, at the direction of the impounding notary." She glanced at Piq. "That's the correct phrase?"

"It is now," Piq said.

The Brigade came back in through the door in a slow wave, like water finding a level. Hesper went straight to the cut line and put her palm flat against the frosted braid. Piq watched her read it through her skin.

"They can patch the coupling in an hour," Hesper said. "That's not the problem. The problem's the other end." She turned. "Your finding. The sunward face."

"Sublimating since the shade went."

"Then it's soft. Every transfer pump on that side is pulling slush and vapour instead of clean ice, and it's been fighting that for six quarters. The automatics keep tripping. I've been resetting them by hand every shift and calling it a firmware fault." She laughed once, a short dry sound with no humour in it. "I've been holding the depot together with my thumbs and didn't know why."

"With the lockdown," Rook said, "the automatics are on countersign. Security still has the depot, even if it doesn't have the ship."

"So someone feeds the Mule by hand from the depot end," Piq said. "Eight hours of manual transfer off a rotten face, and then the depot to keep from cavitating for nineteen days after."

Nobody said a name. Everyone looked at Hesper.

Hesper looked at the Mule: the ugly crust, the hatch with Piq's seal on it, the cockpit where Lucky was not yet talking to anything. Piq watched the guilt she had confessed seven hours ago arrive at the same place as the arithmetic and find no room left in the column.

"I put them on the wrong road," Hesper said. "I was going to go and get them, so it would be me paying. That's not a margin. That's a debt with my name on it." She took her hand off the feed line. "If I'm on that tug, the depot cavitates by Thursday and you've no propellant to come home to, no water for the station, nothing. I'm the only one who knows those pumps." Her mouth worked. "I trained Amaya. I don't get to be the reason she has nowhere to land."

"Hesper," Lucky said from the hatch.

"Fly my ship, Dov." She was already walking toward the depot access. "Bring them back. I'll hold the pumps."

Piq wrote in the register under *Custodians of record*: *H. Okafor-Lund (depot transfer); D. Mbeki (vessel)*. She initialled it and watched the entry take.

The feed coupling came back at 0709. Down the line, faint and far off, she heard a depot pump cycle up under human hands. Tank C's needle shivered and began to climb.

She opened the ledger. Fuel or no fuel, seal or no seal, the column had not moved. Callisto to Europa, land, lift, back to Callisto: 3.32, 3.59, 3.59, 3.32. Four survivors at 0.6 tonnes each. Twenty-one point eight tonnes of crust that Teo would not let them shed.

The clock on her slate read 0712. The window opened at 1612.

Nine hours, and the Mule was still a hundred and forty metres per second short of home.

## Chapter 15: Cheap Seats

At 0709 the depot feed came back. The hangar told Piq before the gauge did. A low note rose in the transfer line and went up through the deck, through the crate she was using as a desk, into the heels of her hands. Tank C had been frozen at sixty-one percent for fifty-one minutes. Now it began to climb again, at the pace of a woman at the far end of the station opening a valve with her own arm.

Piq wrote *0709, feed restored, audit custody* in the margin. The clock above the hangar door said she had nine hours and three minutes. Under it she wrote the number she had been writing since D26 and had not yet managed to cross out.

*Return CAL: −140 m/s.*

Beside it was the honest symbol, a question mark, and beside that the word *Ganymede*. All night it had read to her like a joke someone else had told.

She hadn't slept since D26. She noticed it the way you notice a scale reading high: the instrument might be drifting, but you still have to work with what it shows. Her handwriting was fine. Her arithmetic was fine. The trouble was the edges. Twice in the last hour she had looked up and found that a block of ice had moved across the hangar without her seeing it go.

Achterberg came back through the side hatch with his hands empty and the look of a man who had been insulted in a new way.

"Forty-one minutes," he said. "For a countersignature on one block. It's the same block I took to him yesterday. The one he already countersigned. He asked me whether the block had changed."

"Had it?"

"It had sublimated. Slightly. He made me reweigh it." Achterberg held up a slip. "Point three kilograms lighter. He wanted to know where the point three went."

"Into the air," Piq said. "Then the struts. Then the humidity complaints. I can give you a citation."

"I gave him yours." Achterberg's mouth twitched. "He signed. The gardener from Gallery Two was in the queue behind me with her irrigation bucket again, so I don't think I'm the one who's having the worst day."

"Did he ask if it was going to Europa?"

"He asked if it had been to Europa. She told him it had been to the lettuces, and that the lettuces had a better safety record than his office." He set the slip on the crate and lined it up square with her ledger, as if he had decided she was owed that much. "Nineteen blocks left. Thirteen through the queue by noon, if the tall one keeps his door open. Koskinen's cutting the rest on the skid. If we're cutting on the skid, nothing's moving, so it isn't a transfer."

"That's correct," Piq said. "Please never say it out loud near a terminal."

He went off. Above the cradle doors something shifted in the ductwork with a soft four-kilogram sound. A tabby face with a notched ear looked down at the cold ration bar on her crate with the patient interest of a creature that had never once been weighed. Piq moved the bar six centimetres closer to her elbow, gave the cat one look, and declined to log it.

---

Teo came in at 0724. She carried a tablet, her reading glasses and a mug of tea dark enough to have a mass of its own. She hadn't slept either. On her the lack of sleep showed as a tighter version of what she already was: hair cable-tied higher, the cracked brass dosimeter turned face-in against her collarbone.

"He wants sequencing," she said. She didn't say who, and didn't need to. "I told him tables by eight. He'll have them by eight." She set the tablet down on Piq's ledger and turned it around. "Read me his questions again. I want to answer the ones he actually asked, not the ones I think he meant."

Piq had read them four times since the message came in. Every time, she had read them as an auditor reads a buyer's due-diligence request, looking for what the buyer was really trying to find out. She had filed them under *threat* with the war-risk purchase and the tanker. It had been a reasonable place to put them. Her father would have called it a reasonable place to put a crate you hadn't opened yet.

She read them aloud.

"Cumulative dose for a surface party of three persons. Duration not to exceed four hours. Terrain type: Conamara chaos or equivalent. Shielding: Ur-pattern pressurized crawler, eleven centimetres borated composite. And guidance on the sequencing of non-ambulatory persons during transfer between a surface shelter and the crawler."

"Yes," Teo said.

"Three persons," Piq said. "Not four. He isn't asking how to bring four people out. He's asking how many to send in."

Teo looked at her over the glasses.

"Four hours," Piq said. "You don't put a four-hour cap on a reconnaissance. You cap reconnaissance at the minimum time to see what you came to see, and you don't ask about the dose of the people doing it. You set four hours when you've already decided you'll be carrying something. Conamara isn't a guess." She was talking faster now, the way she did when a column started to sum. "He's naming the terrain, so he has a bearing. Borated composite is neutron shielding, which means a reactor nearby that isn't the crawler's. And non-ambulatory." She stopped. "You told me yesterday. You said he was asking how not to kill people."

"I did say that."

"I put it in the wrong column."

"You put it in the column you were paid to keep," Teo said. She didn't say it to cut. She said it the way she'd read out a dose, as a fact about what had been absorbed. "Go on. You've got the face."

"What face?"

"The face you had in the alcove when the gaps lined up." Teo pulled the second stool across with her foot and sat. "Go on."

---

Piq laid it out on the hangar floor because the crate was too small. Gustav Varga had taught her to do that on the cargo deck of a ship she no longer named, even to herself. When the manifest won't close, take everything off the shelf and put it on the floor where you can walk around it. Gross, tare, contents. Exceptions last.

She had been doing exceptions first, and she knew it.

Four sheets. She weighed them down with whatever was to hand: a torque wrench, Teo's mug, the ration bar, and the thumb-sized impound stamp, which she set down carefully because it had already won her one argument. When she glanced back at the ration bar a minute later, one corner of its wrapper had been opened with surgical precision. The duct above was empty. She logged nothing.

The first sheet was the wedding feed. She had frozen it on the frame where Ziva Kalnins stood at the tanker's helm in a borrowed dress, saying *tanks full, and I come home* into a three-second delay. Over Ziva's shoulder, in the corner of the frame, was the Steadfast Margin's tank board. Piq had read it on the night of the wedding and written beside it: *≈3 km/s excess. Why?* Back then the answer had been: to throw something, to chase something, to reach something the JDA didn't want reached.

The second sheet was Pell's message about war-risk cover. Ur had bought war-risk over the JDA's claimed Europa zone. She was an insurer, or she had been until about three days ago, and she knew what war-risk was for. Nobody buys war-risk to start a war. Wars are excluded or priced so high that a sane buyer self-insures. You buy war-risk when you are about to do something you expect to be called an act of war. You buy it the way you'd pay a notary: in advance, so that afterward someone has to read the clause aloud.

The third sheet was Haddad's questions, and they were no longer the questions of an enemy.

The fourth was the tanker's orbit. JDA bulletins called it a loiter and a provocation, a Martian-aligned tanker parked on a Ganymede-Callisto transfer with nothing to do but be seen. Piq had logged it as a buyer waiting for delivery. Now she pulled the public ephemeris up on her slate and did what she should have done at the start: she stopped asking where the ship was and asked where the orbit went. She propagated it forward. It wasn't a loiter. It was a phasing orbit. Tomorrow, on D29, it reached a node where one burn would drop the Steadfast Margin onto a Ganymede-Europa transfer and park it high on the Europa end, just outside the worst of the torus. The size of that burn matched the excess on Ziva's tank board.

It sat there like a hand held out.

"Lucky," Piq said, without looking up.

He was on the port skid with Koskinen, holding a block of ice that was nominally the size of a person and, under 0.126 g, weighed about as much as a full duffel. He let it settle onto the skid and came over in long flat strides, still talking to Koskinen over his shoulder about whether cut faces should go hull-side or space-side for luck.

"Boss."

"Before the receiver was confiscated. In the hangar. You said something about Ganymede."

"I say a lot of things about Ganymede. Ziva lives there. I'm legally obliged to."

"The cheap seats."

"Oh." He grinned, then saw the floor and stopped grinning. "Ganymede's the cheap seats, but Ur would shoot us. It's a tug joke. Europa to Ganymede's downhill. Well, it isn't downhill, it's sideways, but sideways is cheaper than up. Everybody knows you'd come home cheaper to Ur if Ur weren't..." He looked at the four sheets and the orbit on her slate. "Piq. Why are you looking at me like I'm a receipt?"

"Because you were right, and I logged it as a joke." She tapped the phasing node. "Where will she be tomorrow?"

He crouched. A pilot's eyes went over the arc faster than hers had. She watched him find the burn, find the transfer, and stop where the orbit stopped, high on the Europa side, holding.

"That isn't a loiter," he said quietly.

"No."

"That's a tanker waiting for a lander. That's someone parked where they can come down and pick..." He stopped again, and his face did something complicated and quite young. "Tanks full, and I come home. She said it in the vows. She told me. She stood there in that dress and told me, and I cried, and I thought it was about..." He put a hand flat on the deck. "She was telling me where she'd be."

"She was careful about what she said on an open channel," Teo said.

"She's careful about what she says on a closed one." He laughed. It came out wet. "God. I married a spy."

"You married a tanker pilot," Piq said, and kept her eyes on the slate. "Get me the rendezvous numbers. I want the match at the highest point she could hold if her crew is dose-limited the way Ur crews are. Teo, what's Ur's surface allowance?"

"They don't have one. They have an annual. A hundred and fifty millisieverts for Margin-class crews, waivered. She can't sit in the torus. She'll sit where the dose falls off." Teo was already pulling a table. "There." She drew it on the slate with a stylus: an isodose curve wrapping Europa's orbit like a cuff. "Above that, a few tenths of a millisievert an hour through hull. Below it you start paying."

"Then that's where she'll be," Piq said, "and that's where we have to get to."

---

She rebuilt the ledger on the back of the war-risk sheet, because it was the sheet with the most room.

Mule dry, 51 tonnes. Crust when complete, 21.8. Crew and consumables, 4.2. Survivors, 2.4 tonnes for four people, Teo's medical margin included, written in Teo's hand on a sticky note Piq had refused to throw away. Isp nine hundred seconds. Legs: three point three two to Europa; three point five nine down; three point five nine up; three point three two home.

She crossed out the fourth leg.

She didn't cross it out cleanly. Her pen dragged through it, she had to go over it twice, and for a second the tiredness came up so close to the surface that the numbers swam. Teo's hand came down on the edge of the sheet to hold it still. It didn't touch hers. It was two centimetres away. Piq logged that too and went on.

There would be no Callisto return. There would be ascent from Conamara, then a climb up the cuff of Teo's isodose curve to a tanker that would already be waiting with its own reaction mass and its own reactor, and an airlock that had been to a wedding.

Lucky called it out from his slate. "Ascent onto the transfer and the match at her height, with her doing her half of the match. Three point one one. If she does more than half, less. If Ziva does it, she'll do more than half."

"I'm not ledgering your wife's generosity."

"You should. It's very reliable."

Piq ran it. She ran the mass ratio forward and backward, the way her father had taught her: forward to see whether it closed, backward to see whether it had been lying. Her fingers knew exponents the way other people's fingers know prayers. She heard the hangar going on around her: the transfer line's note, Koskinen's cutter on the skid, Juno saying *less ice?* to someone in a voice that meant she was asking again because she hadn't believed the first answer.

The equals sign came. Piq wrote the number on the far side of it, and for the first time in two days it had no minus in front.

*+68 m/s.*

Sixty-eight metres a second. You could spend that on a single bad hover. A nav error could eat it. So could a crawler that came back heavy, or a pilot who tapped the hull three times and then hesitated one second too long over a crevasse. It was thin.

It was also on the right side of the line.

"Say it," Teo said.

"It closes."

Nobody in the hangar cheered. Seven people had stopped what they were doing, and the sound they made wasn't a cheer. It was the sound of something heavy set down that had been carried a long way. Rafe sat on the skid. Achterberg put both hands on top of his head. Koskinen turned the cutter off. In the new quiet the transfer line was very loud.

"It closes if he says yes," Piq said. "I have a number, not a ship. The ship belongs to a man who has spent a month talking to us in formal notices." She stood. Her knees told her how long she had been crouched, and in this gravity that was saying something. "I'm going to call him."

"Openly?" Teo said.

"Openly." Piq looked at her. "Saar's had everything we've done through a crack in the wall. Prew copies him on your tables. The monitor has your three emissions. He'll know within the hour whatever I say. So I want him to know it from a record. Intra-Jovian traffic doesn't go through the relay. It's timestamped at both ends. If I call the head of Ur Station on an open line and ask him to help us bring four people out of the ice, then whatever happens next happens to the people who said no in public."

Teo held her eyes for a moment longer than the logistics needed.

"Danso's shack," Teo said. "I'll walk you. You're not walking anywhere alone today."

---

Danso had been awake as long as she had. He was eating dry cereal from the box, one piece at a time, the way people eat when they've forgotten food comes in quantities.

"I didn't tell anyone," he said as soon as she was through the door.

"I know."

"Two people asked me. I said I was very busy with the conjunction queue. I pointed at the screen. You'd be amazed how often that works."

"I'm going to make it not work," Piq said. "Open channel to Ur Station. Commissioner's office. Log it at both ends, and put my notary number in the header."

Danso looked at her, then at Teo, then at the box of cereal as if it might advise him. "Saar's office copies that log."

"Yes."

"Every word."

"Every word," Piq said. "Danso. If this goes badly, you put the call through because a registered notary invoked her standing and you had no basis to refuse. Say that."

"I put it through because a registered notary invoked her standing and I had no basis to refuse," Danso said. "I also put it through because I want to see what happens." He raised the link. "Ganymede's at about five and a half light-seconds this morning. Round trip eleven. Don't talk over him. He hates it."

"How do you know?"

"I listen to the wedding feed when I'm sad."

The grey Ur seal came up and held while the handshake crossed the gap. Piq stood in front of it and did the sum she had been putting off since the floor.

Notary and mass auditor. That was the true half of her, the half with a certificate and a stamp. It was the half that had sat across the table from Saar and said *routine audit uploads, Ibis Metrology, check with them in a hundred minutes if you can.* It had held because he couldn't check. Say only that half now, and it would go on holding.

It would also leave 244 tonnes of Authority ice inside a hull that a group of station workers had diverted, and an unlicensed launch into a claimed zone. On the record it would be theft carried out by people she liked. Pell's ninth message, the last one, the one that had finally caught up with her, had put the other half in writing: *Lagrange's salvage clause can fund the rescue.* A salvage clause was paper, and paper needed someone of record to invoke it before the event, in a place where it could not later be unsaid. There was exactly one adjuster of record in the Jupiter system. The channel in front of her was the most public record she would ever have.

The cost was simple to state. Ibis was finished. Saar would have the line within the hour, and he would know that every upload he had failed to verify had gone to Special Losses. Lucky was somewhere in the corridor behind her, as he always was when something mattered. So was half the Brigade. They had taken her in as the auditor who could count, and they had never asked whose money she counted for.

Asset: the salvage claim, the Brigade's cover, a rescue that someone would be obliged to pay for. Liability: herself.

The columns weren't close.

"Piq," Teo said quietly behind her. It wasn't a question. It meant *I can see you doing it.*

"I know," Piq said, and the seal resolved into a man.

Yusuf Haddad-Ferreira was smaller than she had pictured. He sat very straight, with a sense of having been sitting very straight for a long time. Behind him there was no window, only a wall of paper binders labelled by hand. Piq liked him at once, which she distrusted, and she set it aside.

"Commissioner. This is Piq Varga, registered notary and mass auditor on Asgard Yard, and an adjuster of record for Lagrange Mutual Special Losses. I'm on an open channel. This call is being logged at both ends and copied to the JDA Office of Security on Asgard." She took a breath. "I believe the Steadfast Margin is staged to recover survivors of the Ninefold Grace from Conamara Chaos, and I believe your dose questions were the planning for it. I have a vessel and a crew that can land. I don't have the delta-v to bring them home to Callisto. I believe I have it to bring them to your tanker. I'm asking whether you'll take them."

Then she waited.

Eleven seconds was long enough to count, and she counted. Teo stood behind her shoulder, not touching. Danso stopped chewing. Out in the corridor, someone who had been leaning on the wall stopped leaning.

Haddad's face, when the delay ended, had not changed at all. Ur Station had received her words five and a half seconds earlier. She was watching him hear them now, unhurried. His eyes moved once, to something off-screen. Then he spoke.

"Ms Varga. Ur Station acknowledges your communication and notes, with appreciation, your observance of open procedure. It notes also your second designation." A pause, not a delay. "Ur Station has, as a matter of humanitarian prudence, positioned certain assets in anticipation of a possible recovery operation. Ur Station has not undertaken that operation, because an Ur vehicle landing within the zone claimed by the Jovian Development Authority may be construed under Compact Article Nine as a hostile incursion." He stopped. Then, with no change of tone at all: "We have heard them for most of a year. Our listening post at Nicholson Regio caught a beacon on a retired maintenance band within months of the loss, long before the Margin was fuelled. We could not read the keying. We could resolve a bearing, Conamara, within forty kilometres. We could not resolve a site. We have been waiting for someone to tell us where to land without being shot for it."

Most of a year. The tanker, the war-risk cover, the questions: none of it had been in reaction to anything. Forty kilometres. Piq heard Teo breathe in behind her.

She keyed. "Your bearing is right. I have the site to within three hundred metres from four emissions and the Doppler track. I'll send the fix on this channel when we're done. Our vehicle is Authority-registered and is currently impounded under audit custody. If our vehicle lands, an Ur vehicle hasn't. If your tanker meets us above the torus, she meets us in open space on a Ganymede transfer. We need your tanker for the last leg. You need our registration for the first. And Lagrange Mutual is invoking salvage on the Ninefold Grace as of this call, so the recovery is underwritten. Whoever ends up paying for it, it won't be the people who built it."

Eleven seconds.

"That is an elegant construction," Haddad said. "It will not be read as elegant in every office." For the first time something moved in his face. It wasn't a smile. It was a man setting down a binder he'd been holding for a long time. "Ur Station accepts. The Steadfast Margin is coasting to her phasing node now. She will burn there tomorrow, D29, and hold station above the cuff of the torus until your ascent. She will be waiting before you arrive. Commander Kalnins has asked me to tell your pilot, verbatim, that she has kept the tanks full." The pause came again. "I am told he will understand."

Behind the shack wall, from the corridor where nobody had been invited, came a sound that could only be Lucky Mbeki trying and failing to be quiet. There was more in it than one thing.

Piq didn't turn. "Commissioner, one more item. On the record."

Eleven seconds of a man waiting.

"Lagrange Mutual sent me here because of an intercepted fragment," she said. "Seven words. *Throw before conjunction, no one will see it until it's done.* It reached us through a commercial intercept broker in Ceres. It was encoded in an Ur-standard codec. VB4, lot 2214-0417." She kept her voice level. "Did Ur Station send it?"

The delay, then the answer, and in all of it nothing moved on his face. That was how she knew he was telling the truth. A lie would have been allowed some expression.

"No," Haddad said. "Ur Station did not originate that text. No one in my chain would have sent anything to a broker in Ceres. If it had gone out under my authority, I would have signed it. I have not signed it." He let that sit. "If it travelled in an Ur codec, Ms Varga, then someone wished it to look as though it came from us. I would very much like to know who. So, I suspect, would you."

The skin along her arms had gone cold, and she couldn't account for it.

"I'll put it in the square," she said, and immediately wished she hadn't, because he couldn't know what that meant. Then she said, "Later. I'll look later."

"Later is a luxury," Haddad said. "Allow me to spend some of yours now. You understand what will follow. A recovery inside the claimed zone, a vehicle in Authority registry, an Ur tanker on the zone's margin: the Compact will answer it. Not this year. Earth is fifty light-minutes away and its ships are slower than its lawyers. But it will answer." He folded his hands. "When it does, it will want people who were there. I am noting in this log that you made this call, on this channel, at this hour, under both your names, and that you made it first. I am naming you witness. I am sorry. It is the only protection I can give you that will survive conjunction."

"Understood," she said, and found she meant it.

"Send me your fix," Haddad said. "And send the dose warden my thanks for tables by eight. They were here at seven fifty-one."

The link closed on the grey seal. Piq sent the fix. It was three numbers and an error ellipse, derived from four emissions and a Doppler track she had reduced by hand. She copied it into her ledger beside the closing equals sign, because she copied everything. Danso put the cereal box down very slowly, as if it might go off.

"Well," he said. "That's going to be a fun log for the tall one."

---

Lucky was in the corridor. He had his back against the wall and his face in a state she would have needed three columns to record. Ziva was in it, and the tanks full, and something else that had arrived on the same line eleven seconds earlier.

"Special Losses," he said.

"Yes."

"Lagrange." He said it the way he had said it the first night she'd watched the hangar: *Lagrange's water blankets cost me three hundred metres a second.* "The clause. The Grace. The denial. That's your shop."

"That's my shop," Piq said. "The clause was ours. The denial was ours. I recited it to your crew the second day I was here like it was scripture."

Behind him, Rafe and Achterberg had come up the corridor from the hangar, because whatever was said in Danso's shack was said to Saar, and nothing said to Saar stayed out of the hangar for long. Achterberg looked at her as if a slip he had squared to her ledger that morning had come back reweighed.

"You weighed our blocks," Rafe said. "For them."

"I weighed your blocks for the Director's signature," Piq said. "As of twelve minutes ago, every one of them is cargo on a recovery that an adjuster of record put under a Lagrange salvage notice, on a log Saar can't edit. If it works, you're not the people who stole two hundred and forty-four tonnes. You're a contractor Lagrange owes. I couldn't do that as a notary. I could only do it as what I am."

"And if it doesn't work?" Achterberg said.

"Then I'm the one who said it out loud."

Nobody spoke. Down the corridor the transfer line hummed on, indifferent. Lucky looked at the deck, then at the shack door, then at her.

"You could've said it in the hangar," he said. "Before. To us."

"Yes," Piq said. "I should have. I put you in the wrong column too."

He let out a breath that was mostly air and partly a laugh with nothing funny in it. Then he pushed off the wall.

"Ziva kept the tanks full," he said, to the corridor generally. "I'm flying." He walked back toward the hangar without waiting to see whether anyone would follow. After a moment Achterberg did, and then Rafe, and none of them said anything kind. Piq hadn't expected them to, and she logged the silence at its correct weight, which was heavy.

Teo hadn't moved from her shoulder.

"That cost you," Teo said.

"It cost what it cost." Piq found her pen was in her hand, which was a habit. "It bought more."

---

The crew argument took four minutes. Piq had budgeted twenty.

They had it standing around the Mule's main hatch, beside her seal, the little fused polymer square with her thumbprint and the date. Lucky said, "I'm flying," again, and nobody argued, because nobody could. He said it plainly, without the patter, which made it sound like a vow.

Teo said, "I'm going as medic," and that was argued.

"Your dose," Piq said.

"Is mine to calculate."

"You're the dose warden. You can't sign your own..."

"I can." Teo had the tablet out. On it was the medic cell, the field Piq had seen once before: a ceiling, and next to it, faint, a higher number in pencil. Teo erased the pencil with the side of her thumb and wrote a new one, higher still, in pen, with a timestamp. "There. Logged. Deputy Ilunga takes the office while I'm gone. She knows the tables and she's meaner than I am." She looked up. "There are four people in that ice. One of them's had a cough for six weeks that I can't diagnose by Morse code. One has a dose history I've rebuilt from pulse fragments, and it frightens me. You can land them without me. You can't keep them alive in the transfer without me. Write that down."

Piq wrote it down.

"And you," Teo said.

Piq had expected to have to argue for herself. She had the argument ready: the margin is sixty-eight metres a second, someone on board has to watch every kilogram, and nobody else on Callisto counts the way I count. She had it in order, gross to exceptions. She had a second argument underneath it, about whether the Brigade would want a Lagrange adjuster aboard at all, and she found she was not going to make that one.

"You're at a hundred and ninety-one millisieverts lifetime," Teo said. "Plus eleven point four microsieverts from Ring D. A surface round at Conamara inside a borated crawler will put you through point two before you're back up the ladder, and I'll be drawing your blood every week for the rest of your life." She paused. "You're going as mass master. You're the only one who can count it."

The hangar was very quiet.

"Are you grounding me or clearing me?" Piq said.

"I'm logging you," Teo said, and looked at her. It lasted perhaps two seconds, the length of the wedding delay. Something passed between them that didn't need a light-second to arrive. It settled the way a weight settles on a scale that has been swinging: not stopped, but done with the swinging. Piq didn't name it. She didn't have a column for it. For once she didn't go looking for one.

"Right," said Lucky, a little hoarse, not looking at Piq yet. "Right. Good. Three. Three's luckier than four, everybody knows that." He tapped the hull three times and then, after a moment, a fourth time, quietly, as if for someone not present.

---

Hesper was in the depot pump gallery when Piq found her at 1140. She had one arm through a manual bypass to the elbow and her forehead against a cold pipe. The gallery smelled of ozone and wet regolith. Frost had grown in feathers along the shadowed ribs overhead. Piq knew now exactly where it came from, and she couldn't look at it without seeing ninety-five point nine tonnes.

"Pump three's pulling slush," Hesper said without lifting her head. "I can hear it. If I take my hand off this, it'll cavitate inside a minute."

"Then don't take your hand off it," Piq said. "I'll come to you."

"Dov called down." Hesper still didn't lift her head. "Who you work for."

"Yes."

"I asked you what you were really weighing. Weeks ago." A breath against the pipe. "I suppose now I know."

Piq crouched and took the sewn cardboard book out of her own ledger: the depot-floor tally, every block in Hesper's square, stubborn hand, closing at 244.1. She held it where Hesper could see it.

"You promised," Hesper said.

"I promised to give it back. I'm giving it back." Piq slid it into the thigh pocket of Hesper's coverall, the only part of her that wasn't holding the station together. "Every number in it is in a certified ledger with the Director's signature, and as of this morning every one of them is cargo under a Lagrange salvage notice, invoked on a public log by the adjuster of record. Nobody can hang you for supplying a salvage the insurer has already said it will pay for. Keep it anyway. It's good work."

Hesper laughed into the pipe. It turned into something else partway through.

"Bring them up," she said. "Bring Dov up. He'll talk all the way there and he'll be useless at the bottom, and then he'll do something no one else could, so let him." She turned her head at last, cheek to the cold. "Tell Amaya I'm sorry about the margin."

"You can tell her yourself. Ganymede's eleven seconds away."

"Eleven seconds," Hesper said, "and a lifetime of ice." She shut her eyes. "Go. I've got the pumps."

---

At 1258 Saar shut the doors.

He didn't touch the Mule. He couldn't: it was under her seal, outside his jurisdiction, in neutral custody he had read twice. He didn't cut the feed again. Rook's line on the restore order had made the feed the notary's, and Saar had learned once that week what reading a statute twice felt like.

What he did was lock the hangar's outer pressure door on a security interlock for a structural inspection of the cradle approach. Then he put four officers on the cradle track to carry out the inspection. The tall one stood in front.

Piq read the order on the hangar terminal. It was perfect. It was pure procedure. It touched no asset; it only stood between the asset and the sky. It was the cruellest thing she had seen him do, because it was entirely legal and would take exactly long enough.

"Inspection estimated at six hours," Rafe read off. "Six hours from 1258..."

"Is 1858," Piq said. "The window opens at 1612 and closes at 1700. He's done the arithmetic." She had the ordinance open in her head. Schedule C, vehicles. Schedule D, ex-vehicles: the Mule was registered under D, and she could argue the lockdown's scope for an hour and lose. Annex 7, already spent. Nothing in the 14,206 pages let a notary open a door. Notaries certify things. They don't open things.

Teo had gone very still beside her. It wasn't the stillness of someone without an answer. It was the stillness of someone looking at an answer she didn't like.

"There's a code," Teo said.

"Which?"

"One I've never used for anything it didn't say." She was already walking.

Piq went after her. The outer pressure door was a slab of composite and regolith with a security plate glowing red. The officers on the far side were visible through the viewport, their suits pale in the light from the cradle track. Behind them the Sling ran out across the basin for forty kilometres, a line of coils under frost, with Jupiter low and banded at the end of it.

Teo set her palm on the override plate. The red stayed red. For a moment her hand just rested there, and Piq understood that she was not waiting for the plate.

"Dose warden override," Teo said to the door, in a voice Piq had last heard at intake on her first day. "Code Four. Source removal." The plate went amber. The officers on the far side turned.

Teo keyed the intercom.

"Officers on the cradle approach, this is the Dose Warden of Asgard Yard. The vessel in Hangar Four carries an operating fission reactor, drum warm, which I am now classifying as a mobile radiation source in an occupied structure under Dose Code Section Eleven. A source of that class at an occupied boundary shall be removed to the nearest unoccupied exclusion area. The cradle approach is that area." She didn't hurry. "Under Section Three I have authority to ground any person whose projected dose exceeds their allocation. You are standing forty metres from the source and inside the drum's line. I have your badges. Officer Hale, your projected exposure by the end of a six-hour inspection is zero point four one seven millisieverts above your quarterly allocation. Officer Nakashima's is zero point three nine eight. I'm grounding all four of you, effective now. Clear the approach."

Nobody moved for a long moment.

Then a new voice came on the channel from somewhere on the security net. It was dry and unhurried, and it smiled without needing to be seen.

"Warden Anand. Inventive."

"Chief Saar."

"Your numbers are correct. They always are." A pause, measured. "Your classification is not. A drum-warm reactor inside a shielded hangar is a contained source. You have run that hangar's survey every quarter for three years and signed every one. You know it, and as of this minute the log knows you know it." The smile stayed in his voice. "I'll clear the approach. Your order is lawful on its face, and I'm not going to have four officers stand in a dose field to argue a definition. But I am filing a dispute with the Director and with the Compact medical board: misuse of dose authority for operational ends. You'll answer it when you're back. If you're back." Another pause. "Safe travels to the auditor."

The channel closed. On the far side of the viewport the four officers turned and walked back along the track in long, flat, deliberate strides, every one of them visibly hating it. The plate turned green. The door began to open.

Teo took her hand off the override plate. It was shaking a little. She put it in her pocket so no one would see, and Piq saw.

Then Teo took it out again, opened her own log on the tablet, and wrote while the door was still grinding up its track. Piq read it upside down: *1306 D28. Code Four invoked, Hangar Four. Classification contestable. Purpose: to clear the cradle approach for launch. Dispute expected. T. Anand.*

"You logged the pretext," Piq said.

"It's my book." Teo didn't look up. "I've just bent a rule for the first time in twelve years. I'm not going to lie in the one place that's never lied to me as well." She closed the log. "He's right. That's the part I'll have to answer for."

"You quoted his dose to three decimal places."

"I always do," Teo said. "Those were true. The rest was a door." She said it like a reading, and Piq heard what it had cost her to say it.

---

At 1540 the Mule went out onto the cradle under its own sealed authority. Lucky was in the left seat. The voice of the custodian of record came through the depot speaker from somewhere below, with an arm still through a valve. The thirty-one blocks of the crust were fitted at last, Juno's dripping one among them. Tank C stood at a hundred percent. Piq had weighed the whole ship once more at the cradle load cells, because she would have been ashamed not to: gross, tare, contents, exceptions last. It came to within four kilograms of her ledger. Two of the four were the hollow comms unit she still hadn't let herself put down.

She went through the nav pack the same way, line by line against the manifest, because a file was cargo too. Ephemerides, relay tables, Europa control network, *EUR CONAMARA SECTOR SURVEY, rev. 2214.7c, issued JDA-SEC 0350 D28, checksum verified.* That was the pre-conjunction refresh JDA Security pushed to every hangar terminal before every blackout. Lucky had loaded it at 0412 with the rest of the standard pack. It was the only Conamara survey in the system. Every chart she had ever seen of that ground descended from the Authority's, so there was nothing aboard to set it against except the ground itself. She copied the revision and timestamp onto the ledger line under her own site fix, three hand-reduced numbers and an error ellipse. On approach she would lay one over the other, as she would on any chart, on any approach, for any client. Gross, tare, contents.

The cabin was the size of her quarters, and full of water.

Teo was strapped in across from her with the medical kit between her boots and the dosimeter turned face-out for once. Piq noticed that, and didn't know what to do with it.

"Thirty-seven days on the drum," Teo said into the quiet, to nobody, to herself. "Four hours of surface. Sixty-eight metres a second."

"Sixty-eight," Piq said.

"Cradle's live," said Hesper from below, tired and steady. "Coils are warm. Pumps are holding. The Sling is yours, Dov. It's a gentle throw by her standards. Don't insult her by enjoying it."

"I'll enjoy it a little," Lucky said, "privately." His hand went to the bulkhead. One, two, three. "Ziva. Margin. If you're listening."

Nine seconds later the answer came from a tanker still coasting toward the node where she would burn tomorrow. It was a flat, unreadable voice that had once said *I do* before it was asked. "Tanks are full."

"Throw on my mark," Hesper said. "Three. Two."

Lucky turned his head, just enough, and looked at Piq for the first time since the corridor. It wasn't forgiveness. It was a pilot checking that his mass master was strapped in. She nodded, and he turned back.

Piq looked once across the cabin. Teo was already looking back.

"Mark."

The Sling took them.

There was no roar, only weight arriving all at once from behind: more than three Callistos, then more than Earth, pressing Piq into a couch that had been built for slugs of ice and adapted for people in a single night. Through the frosted port the coils went past like the teeth of a comb, faster and faster, until they were one grey line. Jupiter didn't move. Jupiter never moved here. Everything else moved toward it.

The ledger lay in her lap under her palm, open at the equals sign. Below it were two lines she had not yet compared: her fix, and the Authority's chart of the ice they were going to land on. Behind them, on a public log, the name she had given up was already on its way to every office that would want it.

At 1612 D28 the Mule left Callisto.

## Chapter 16: Exceptions Last

The cradle let go of them at 1612, and for twenty-four seconds Piq weighed more than she ever had.

The Sling did not shove. It pulled at fourteen g, held steady down forty kilometres of rail, and the load came through the couch as one long press, like a palm flattening a letter. Her vision narrowed to a grey tube. She counted through it out of habit, because counting was something to hold. At twenty-four the rail ran out and the weight came off all at once. The Mule's cabin went quiet in a way she felt in her inner ear before she heard it.

Something drifted past her face: the stub of Teo's pencil, turning end over end, very slowly, its eraser describing a circle the size of a coin. Nothing pulled it anywhere. It would keep going until it met a wall.

"Throw nominal," Lucky said to the board. He did not say it to them. "Leg one, three point three two outbound. We're on the line."

Hesper's voice came up on the depot channel with a little hiss of distance, still close enough to be warm. "Clean release, Dov. I'm going back to my pumps. Don't bring her back scratched."

"Copy."

That was all. Piq had expected more from him and was ashamed of having expected it. She wrote *1612 D28, release, 3.32 nominal* in the notebook clipped to her thigh and drew no box around it.

Teo had already unstrapped. She caught the pencil without looking and put it behind her ear. Then she pushed off toward the bulkhead where she had taped her tables. There were three laminated sheets in her own handwriting, gridded in the six-by-six blocks of the old maintenance protocol she had taught her sister at nineteen. Teo hooked one foot under a rail and began to read the cabin's dosimeters aloud. Nobody had asked her to. It was the way a person runs a hand over a wall in the dark.

"Cabin centre, point zero four microsieverts an hour. Flight couch, point zero six. Aft locker, point zero five." She wrote. "We're still in Callisto's lee. This is the last of the cheap air."

---

The Mule had been built to haul and had never been meant to be lived in. It was a stubby pressure cylinder six metres long, with a flight deck at one end, a reactor bulkhead at the other, and a stripped cabin between. Outside, thirty-one hand-cut blocks of Callisto ice were strapped and foamed to the hull, Juno's drip block among them. Together they made 21.8 tonnes of crust that turned the tug into something like a hermit crab. Aft of the cabin sat Tank C, a cylinder of water the size of a wardrobe. It was propellant and shield at once, and it would grow thinner as one became the other.

In the lee of Tank C, Teo had built what she called the water room. It was not a room. It was the region where the tank, the crust and the reactor bulkhead's shadow overlapped. On her map it showed as a lopsided lozenge shaded in grey pencil, its shape changing with their attitude, their distance from Jupiter and the volume left in the tank. Piq had watched her draw it on the hangar floor. It had looked like a lung.

"You'll sleep here," Teo told her that first evening, and tapped the darkest part of the lozenge. "Lucky sleeps here," she said, pointing to the lighter grey beside it. "When he's on the couch he's on the couch. The couch is where the ship's eyes are, and the eyes need a window."

"Where do you sleep?"

Teo looked at the map as if the question had been asked about someone else. "Wherever's left."

Piq let it stand. Over the last fortnight she had learned that some of Teo's answers were doors and some were walls, and that knocking on a wall only told it you'd mistaken it for a door. She took her ration, a dense brick the Brigade's galley had labelled *ENERGY (SAVOURY)*, and ate it slowly in the dark grey, watching Teo work.

Teo worked the way Piq's father had weighed, except that she had no scale. She had three dosimeters on the bulkhead, one at the couch, and her own cracked brass badge, worn face-out on her collar. Every fifteen minutes she read them, wrote, projected and redistributed. When Lucky came off the couch for a ration and two hours' sleep, she moved Piq a hand's width aft to make room. Then she moved herself a hand's width forward. Piq noticed both adjustments and the direction of each.

---

*Steadfast Margin* burned at the node at 0628 D29. They saw it as a datum, not as light. It came three seconds late on the Ur public tracking feed that Haddad had opened to them under the joint plan. On Lucky's side screen the tanker's solid line peeled off its old loiter ellipse and lay down along the dashed predicted track like a thread pulled into a seam.

Ziva's voice packet followed, flat as a ruler. "*Steadfast Margin to Mule. Transfer insertion within two metres a second. Holding high above the cuff per Dose Warden Anand's tables. Tanks at seventy-four percent.*" A pause. "*Which is full, for what we need.*"

Lucky put his face in his hands for about four seconds. Then he keyed the reply, which would reach her in three. "Mule copies. We're on our line. You're on yours. Love you. Over."

He turned his head, perhaps for the first time since Callisto, and looked at Piq. "What's the fourth leg now?"

"Three point one one. With her doing half the match. Every metre a second she carries for us is one we don't. It's her propellant and her dose."

"She knows that."

"I know she does."

Piq wrote *0628 D29 SM node burn; 74% = full* and, under it, *L asked a question*. She didn't know what column that belonged in. She left it without one.

---

The dose climbed on schedule. That was the only comfort in it. Callisto lay outside the belts, in the quiet suburbs. Europa was the old town, where the plasma ran thick and the moon's own ice sputtered off and glowed. The Mule was descending a slope, and the slope kept getting steeper.

"Five microsieverts an hour," Teo said on the morning of D30, the way someone else might announce rain. "Couch is seven. That's through twenty-one tonnes of ice. We're still fine. I want everyone to understand that we're still fine, until about 0300 tomorrow, when we cross the cuff. After that I'll stop using the word."

At 1100 a packet came up on 410.250 MHz. Piq heard it first, because the covert receiver in the hollow comms unit was strapped beside her head. A rhythm sharpened out of the static on the band nobody else used.

They decoded it as they had on Callisto. Teo read the pulse groups off the waterfall and Piq set them into the grid. Piq caught herself mouthing the letters before Teo said them.

R-H-E-E C-O-U-G-H B-L-O-O-D. O-D-U-Y-A H-O-L-D-S. D-R-U-M 3-5.

Then came the doubled final pulse. Amaya.

Teo read it three times, which she never did. Her face did nothing at all, and Piq had learned that this was not the same as feeling nothing.

"Thirty-five days on the drum," Piq said, because someone had to say the number that wasn't the worst one. "Thirty-seven on D28, less two. She's counting right."

"She was always counting right." Teo looked at the pencil in her hand. "If Rhee's bringing up blood, it's dose in his gut lining, or infection on a marrow that can't fight one, or both. I can't do anything for him from here."

"You can tell them we're two days out."

Teo keyed with two fingers: *2 DAYS. SITE FIXED. KEEP RHEE STILL. FLUIDS. HOLD DOSE.* Then she sent her own doubled pulse, the mark she'd taught a fourteen-year-old so that they would always know which of them was speaking.

No reply came. Every climb to the transmitter cost Amaya dose. Piq wrote down the time anyway.

---

That night Teo did the arithmetic for Rhee, and Piq did the arithmetic for Teo.

Neither of them said so. Teo sat in the light grey with her knees folded under a rail and the survivors' 2.4-tonne note smoothed out on her thigh. She was working out how long the crawler's shielding could hold four extra bodies before their mass and breathing cost more than it could carry. Piq floated in the dark grey with Teo's laminated sheets, doing what her father had taught her to do with a manifest someone had handed her in good faith. She checked it.

It was clean. The assumptions were conservative in every column but one.

"Teo. Your column."

Teo didn't look up. "What about it."

"Lucky's projection through the descent is thirty-eight millisieverts. Mine is forty-one. Yours is sixty-four. You've put yourself at the forward rail for every couch rotation and by the hatch for the crawler transfer. And you moved yourself a hand forward every time you moved me a hand aft. Four times yesterday."

The ventilator's tick became audible. "The medic goes where the work is."

"The medic is also the only person aboard who can read a blood count."

"And you're the only person aboard who can close a ledger." Teo looked up then. In the red night-lighting the brass badge on her collar caught a single point of it. "We each have one thing. I'm not going to spend yours to protect mine."

"That isn't how a ledger works."

"It's how a dose budget works. That's what I'm keeping."

Piq set the sheet against the bulkhead and let it float there, held by static and the ventilator's faint pressure.

"Your rules," she said. "You bent one on D28, at the hangar door. You logged it. Your hand was shaking." She chose the next words one at a time, as if crossing a rail with nothing beneath it. "I'm asking whether you've decided that because you bent a rule for them, you have to pay for it out of yourself. Because that isn't in any code I've read."

Teo looked at her for a long time.

"When Amaya was fourteen," she said, "I was nineteen and working dose at a relay on Himalia. A rock with a dish on it, eleven people. She was on a training rotation at Ur. Our mother had died the year before. We didn't have anyone who would ask about us in a way that meant anything. So I taught her the maintenance protocol. Badge codes on a six-by-six. It was meant for engineers to report dosimeter faults without tying up voice. Nobody used it. I thought it was ours."

"It was."

"Every night at 2100 Himalia time she keyed me her dose. Her whole day's dose. Nobody needed to know. She did it because I'd told her it was important. Because if you have a number and somebody's watching it, you aren't lost." Teo pressed her mouth flat. "I believed it. The badge reads what it reads. The limit is the limit. If you keep inside it, nothing on the dose side of your life can hurt you."

Piq said nothing.

"Then the *Grace* went down, and Lagrange said it was pilot error. I read fourteen-c in the denial. Minimum shielding water on any Europa-bound vessel. A dose rule. I'd have written it myself." Her voice did not rise, but something under it came very near the surface. "For fourteen months I hated them for the denial, and I never once thought the rule was what killed her. Because rules don't. That's what I'd told her, and what I've told everyone on Asgard for twelve years."

"It didn't kill her," Piq said. "She isn't dead."

"No. She's under ice with a man coughing blood, doing a dose warden's job on three people with her bare hands, because I taught her to keep the number. And she's doing it right. And it isn't enough, and she knows that, and she keeps doing it." Teo looked at her own column. "So, no. I didn't decide to pay for D28 out of myself. I decided that if the rules can kill her, I'd better be standing where they're aimed."

The ventilator ticked. Outside the crust, Jupiter's field poured past in a wind no one aboard would ever feel.

"My father was weigh-master on a cycler that never stopped anywhere," Piq said. "You've read my intake. He weighed me every month until I was twelve and could do it myself. Twenty kilos. That's what I've been since I left. Twenty kilos, plus whatever I can declare." She took a breath. "He used to say: gross, tare, contents, exceptions last. Weigh the whole thing. Take off the container. What's left is what you're carrying. And at the very end, separately, you write down the things you can't reconcile. You don't fold them into the totals to make the totals balance. You write them in their own place, so whoever reads the manifest knows they're there." She looked at the floating sheet. "I've been good at the first three and bad at the last. I've folded a lot of exceptions into a lot of totals. I did it to you."

"I know."

"I'm not asking you to say anything about that. I've spent a long time with nobody to weigh me but me, and I've only lately noticed how much I wanted someone to. And I won't watch you spend yourself on a rule that has no line for it without writing it down. That's all."

Teo reached across the gap between the light grey and the dark, slowly, as if through water. She took the sheet and erased the sixty-four, carefully, as she did everything. The graphite came away in a little smudge that drifted and hung between them in the red light.

She wrote fifty-one. She moved her crawler-transfer position from the hatch to second-in-line, behind Lucky, and adjusted the other two numbers to match.

"That's still the highest," Piq said quietly.

"It's still the medic." Teo put the sheet back. Her hand stayed flat against the laminate a moment longer than it needed to, a few centimetres from Piq's shoulder. Neither of them moved. "Exceptions last," Teo said. "Put me in that column if you want. I'll know I'm there."

Then she pushed back into the light grey, and the smudge drifted until the ventilator found it.

Piq did not write anything down. She lay in the dark grey with her eyes open, listening to Teo's breathing slow. When it was slow enough she realized she had been counting it.

---

They crossed the cuff at 0247 D31. There was nothing to see, only the couch reading sixty-one, then a hundred and forty by 0400. Teo pulled Lucky out every forty-five minutes and took the couch herself for the ten minutes that let him eat. Piq noted it and let it stand.

Europa grew in the forward window from a bruise of light into a world. It was the colour of old ivory, laced with red-brown lines like veins in cut stone. On the night side the ice gave off a faint, sickly glow, sputtered gas recombining. Nothing in the pictures had told her it was the colour of a wound.

At 0600 Lucky loaded the terrain survey into the optical navigation stack. As the surface resolved under the cameras, the ship would match what it saw against the survey and know within metres where it was. *EUR CONAMARA SECTOR SURVEY, rev. 2214.7c, issued JDA-SEC 0350 D28, checksum verified.* It was the only Conamara chart there was.

Piq had copied that revision line three days earlier and had underlined the issuer without knowing why. Surveys came from the Authority's survey office. This one had come from security, at ten to four on the morning Saar locked the launches. *Checksum verified* proved only that whoever had signed it had signed it.

She dropped her own fix onto the map. It was three numbers and an error ellipse under three hundred metres across, built from four emissions and the Doppler track. The ellipse fell squarely onto a broken raft of ice four kilometres long, seamed with a dark fissure. The survey tagged it *R-K7* in amber: *FRACTURED, UNSTABLE, NO-LAND*. Two kilometres northeast lay a smooth green plate, *R-K4*.

"I set us down on K4 and we crawl across," Lucky said. "Teo?"

"An hour each way at rated speed. Two hours on site. Inside four. Tight. Not impossible."

At 0640 the optical nav began matching. At 0652 it disagreed with the inertial solution by a hundred and eighty metres.

"Gyro wander," Lucky said. "Three days without a terrain update. Optical's always right." He let the optical solution win. The ship's idea of itself slid southwest, and the autopilot, still aiming at K4, bent the descent track a fraction further northeast to make up the difference.

Piq wrote down the number. At 0711 the gap was three hundred and forty metres. At 0729 it was six hundred and ten.

"We took fourteen g's on the throw," Lucky said. His voice had gone flat. "Could have knocked something."

"It didn't knock anything for three days." Piq turned the notebook toward him. "I've logged star tracker against inertial every four hours since Callisto. They agree inside twenty metres. They agreed this morning. The gyros didn't change. The ground did. Can you show me residuals by tile?"

He looked at her, then pulled up a diagnostic panel she hadn't known existed. It was a lattice of small squares, each coloured by how well its terrain matched the cameras. Most were green.

A block of nine tiles, centred under her fix, was red. Every residual in them pointed the same way, two kilometres southwest, and the disagreement stopped at their edges as cleanly as a parcel's sides.

"Honest errors spread," Piq said. She heard her own voice come out level and far away, the way her father's had sounded when he found a short weight on a pallet stacked carefully to hide it. "A bad datum, a worn gyro. They smear across everything, because nobody was aiming them. This is nine tiles, square, centred on the one place on Europa we are trying to reach."

"Show me what it does," Teo said.

"If optical flies us in, it thinks we're two kilometres short the whole way down. It carries us two kilometres past K4. We put down four kilometres from the fix and believe it's two."

Teo didn't need the sheet. "Four out, four back. Four hours in the crawler. Nothing left on site."

"We'd find out halfway across, when the ground stopped agreeing with the map. Out there, under five sieverts a day."

Lucky looked at the issuer line. "JDA-SEC."

"Security doesn't survey anything," Piq said. "It countersigns."

He said the name once, the way you'd read a figure off a scale. "Saar."

"Three witnesses," Piq said. "Gyros, stars, my emission fix. They agree with each other, and the green tiles agree with all of them. One document disagrees, in nine squares, around the people it would be convenient to lose. You don't throw out three receipts because one invoice says otherwise."

Lucky masked the nine tiles out of the solution by hand. The ship's estimate jerked six hundred metres back and the descent track straightened. He killed the downrange velocity and held them in a hover over the chaos while the inertial re-solved against star fixes and the green tiles at the block's margins. Piq read radar altitude aloud every five seconds. The board counted propellant out of the landing reserve in steady digits.

"Could I put her on the fix?" Lucky said, half to himself. Below them the raft was a puzzle of grey blocks and black seams. "No. I've got no hazard map I trust in those tiles. Maybe the NO-LAND is a lie. Maybe the cracks aren't. Amaya's said for weeks that the ice is moving. I'm not setting forty tonnes on a slab I can only see through a window, over people living under it." He slid them northeast onto ground the cameras and the green tiles agreed on.

The Mule touched down on R-K4 at 0758 D31. Europa's gravity came back faintly, a little more than Callisto's, and every loose thing in the cabin settled.

"Hover cost thirty-eight metres a second of reserve," Lucky said.

Piq wrote it in its own line.

Teo was already at the crawler hatch with her sheet. "Two kilometres. Crawler interior runs about nine millisieverts an hour at this flux. Outside it's two hundred and nine. An hour each way is eighteen millisieverts each, before we're on site. Two hours there is another eighteen. That's thirty-six on top of the descent, and that's the cap. The clock starts when the hatch seals."

It sealed at 0815.

---

The crawler was a low box on four tracked pods with a water jacket thirty centimetres thick, and it moved as though it disliked every metre. Lucky drove. Teo called time and dose at every quarter hour. Piq watched the inertial and the star tracker and nothing else.

Conamara was not a plain. It was a field of shattered plates, some the size of buildings, tilted and refrozen in a matrix of jumbled ice, ridged and pitted. The crawler climbed onto a block, nosed off it and climbed again. Jupiter sat on the horizon, cut in half by it, enormous and banded, throwing ochre light across frost that glittered for a moment and went dark. The exterior counter never dropped below two hundred.

"Zero eight forty-five. Four point six," Teo said. Later: "Zero nine hundred. Six point nine."

At 0912 the fissure opened ahead of them, a black seam three metres wide running into the raft. Wedged twenty metres down it, tilted forty degrees and furred white, lay the forward section of the *Ninefold Grace*. Someone had strung a line of chemical lights down the wall, and two still glowed. A soft-collar adaptor the Brigade had built for exactly this hull stood folded at the crawler's nose. Lucky eased the crawler to the lip and paid it down. Piq listened to the collar hiss as it mated with the *Grace*'s dorsal lock.

Teo went second, behind Lucky, as she had written it.

Inside, the air was cold enough to hurt, and it smelled of scorched insulation and of people kept too long in one place. The cabin had been walled into a burrow with bagged ice and stowage panels. In the middle of it a man lay strapped to a cot, grey-faced, with a stained cloth folded under his chin: Rhee. A tall woman with her hand flat on his chest was Oduya. A wiry man was already standing and reaching for a bag, as though he had been packed for months: Wierzbicki.

And a small figure crouched at the far wall beside a radio rig, hair cropped close, a dosimeter badge pinned face-out on her collar.

Amaya stood up slowly. She didn't speak. She lifted two fingers and tapped them twice against the visor of Teo's helmet, the doubled pulse.

Teo said her name once. Then she did what she had trained her whole life to do. She took her sister's badge between gloved fingers, tilted it to the light and read it. Her lips moved on the number. She let it go, and her hands closed on Amaya's shoulders, and she held on without a sound while Amaya pressed her forehead to the faceplate. Neither of them moved for eleven seconds. Piq counted them and wished she hadn't.

"Rhee first," Teo said, and her voice was entirely steady. "Fluids, then the litter."

---

The hull groaned.

It was not a loud sound. It came through the deck plates into Piq's boots, a long settling, like a heavy man shifting in his sleep. A strut by the radio rig was scored with chalk marks and dates in Amaya's small, square hand.

"The walls," Amaya said, following her eyes. Her voice was hoarse from disuse. "Forty centimetres since we came down. Nine in the last week. It's speeding up." She looked at the bulkhead as if she could see through it. "Another month and it closes on us."
